How This Book Works

This book teaches you the entire AI ecosystem — from "what is AI, actually?" to the chips it runs on, the buildings those chips live in, and the people who build all of it. By the end, you'll be able to read any AI headline, evaluate any claim, and explain how the whole system works to anyone who asks.

Who this book is for: You're smart and curious. You don't have a computer science background. You're tired of feeling like everyone else understands this and you missed the memo. (They don't. You didn't.)

What you won't need: A maths degree. Programming experience. Prior knowledge of any of this.

What you will need: Willingness to follow the thread. Each chapter exists because the previous one made you ask a question. If you read them in order, each answer builds on the last.

Three Principles This Book Follows

1. Follow one moment deeper. Each chapter exists because the previous one made you ask a natural question. Chapter 1 explains what AI is → you ask "but how does it learn?" → Chapter 3 (data) and Chapter 5 (training) answer that. The whole book is one continuous thread pulled through.

2. Always locate you in the picture. For every concept, you'll know: is this something happening behind the scenes that I'll never touch? Or is this something I interact with directly? Both matter — but for different reasons.

3. Teach the task, not the category. Every concept is anchored to something the system is being asked to do. Not "here are five types of neural networks" — instead, "the system is trying to tell cats from dogs in photos, and here's what it does."

What the Boxes Mean

Throughout the book, you'll see coloured callout boxes. Here's what each one signals:

📌 Key concept pinned. The one thing in this section you most need to retain. If you're skimming, read these.

⏸️ Your natural question. "Wait, but..." — we anticipated the question you're about to ask and we're answering it right here.

💡 Insight. An implication or connection that isn't obvious but matters.

🏆 Landmark Moment. A real historical moment that made this concept matter. Three parts: what happened, what concept it demonstrated, and why it mattered.

📰 Big Picture. A pointer to a deeper investigative piece on the social, ethical, or economic implications. The textbook teaches how things work; Big Picture pieces explore what that means.

🔍 Concept in Practice. Something you've already noticed in real life (AI streaming word by word, products getting worse after updates, the same model behaving differently in different apps) — now explained mechanically with what you've just learned.

Other Things You'll Find in Every Chapter

  • Learning objectives at the top (what you'll be able to do after reading)
  • Key terms table (every new concept, defined in plain English)
  • "Try This" exercises (ways to apply what you've learned without needing any technical tools)
  • "What's Next" bridge (the question this chapter leaves you with, answered by the next)
  • System diagram (a cumulative visual showing how the whole system is building up, chapter by chapter)
  • Quick reference glossary at the bottom (for flipping back to later)

A Note on Time

AI moves fast. This book is regularly updated — model names, benchmark scores, costs, and company details are checked weekly and refreshed as things change. The concepts themselves (how training works, what tokens are, why GPUs matter, how agents loop) are stable fundamentals that won't shift underneath you. But the specifics? Those stay current.

Ready?

Chapter 1 starts with a spam filter. Trust the process.

Chapter 1: What "AI" Actually Means (and What It Doesn't)

Part I · What Is This Thing?

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

  • Explain the difference between ordinary software and AI in one sentence
  • Identify which parts of products you already use are AI and which aren't
  • Spot when "powered by AI" is real and when it's marketing
  • Use a single question to cut through any AI claim you encounter

Key Terms Introduced in This Chapter

TermPlain English
AlgorithmA set of instructions a machine follows — like a recipe
AutomationSoftware that follows rules a human wrote in advance
Artificial Intelligence (AI)A system that figured out its own rules by learning from examples
Training dataThe pile of examples a system learns from
LabellingHumans marking examples as "this" or "not this" so the machine can learn the difference

(You'll see these bolded the first time they appear in the text.)

The Spam Filter That Gave Away the Secret

There was a brief, glorious window — call it 2003 — when you could sneak absolutely anything past a spam filter if you were willing to look slightly unhinged. The word FREE got your email binned, so people wrote "F-R-E-E." Or "FR33." Or "V!agra," with the little exclamation mark standing in for the i.

That is how a whole generation of inboxes quietly gave away something about the filter guarding them.

It wasn't clever. It was a list. Some actual human being, at some software company, had sat down and typed out the words that meant junk, and the filter did precisely what it was told. See a banned word, bin the message. Don't see it, let it through.

That's the entire reason the misspellings worked. You weren't outwitting a mind. You were stepping around a fence, and the fence only stood exactly where a person had planted it.

That filter was automation — software following rules a human wrote, exactly as written, nothing more.

Then, somewhere in the years after, the fence stopped mattering. The tricks quit working. "FR33" started landing in junk right alongside "FREE," and nobody had run around adding every possible spelling of every dodgy word to the list. That list would be infinite, and infinite lists don't get written on a Tuesday.

Something else had happened.

This new filter had been shown examples instead of handed a rulebook. Every time a person somewhere clicked this is spam and this one's fine, that was one more labelled example dropped onto the pile. Millions of them. The pile of labelled examples is what's called training data. And from that heap of human verdicts, the filter worked out for itself what junk tends to look like. Nobody ever wrote the rule "an exclamation mark wedged into a word is a bit suspicious." The thing noticed, on its own.

That's AI — a system that learned its own rules from examples.

Now watch what didn't change. A human is still in this story. Someone still did the labour — a lot of someones in fact, all those little yes-junk / no-keep verdicts. The difference between the old filter and the new one was never person versus machine. It was which job the person did. In the old one, a human wrote the rules and the machine obeyed them. In the new one, humans supplied the examples, and the machine wrote its own rules.

That swap is the exact seam between the software you've used your whole life and the stuff now getting called AI.

📌 The Core Distinction Automation: A person writes the rules → the machine follows them exactly. AI: A person supplies examples → the machine figures out its own rules. A person is involved in both. The difference is which job the person did.

⏸️ Let's make this concrete — same email, two different filters:

An email arrives: "Congrats!! You've W0N a FR33 iPad!!! Click here NOW!!!"

The rule-based filter (automation):

  1. Check sender against blocklist → not on the list
  2. Scan for banned words: "FREE" → not found (it's "FR33")
  3. Scan for "click here" → found! Flag as spam.
  4. But only because a human happened to add "click here" to the banned list last Tuesday.

The learned filter (AI):

  1. Convert the email into numerical features — word patterns, punctuation density, sender reputation score, formatting signals, time of day, dozens of factors
  2. Run those numbers through its learned patterns (adjusted over millions of labelled examples)
  3. Output: 97.3% probability this is spam. Flag as spam.
  4. It flagged it because the combination of excessive punctuation + disguised words + urgency language + unknown sender matches patterns it learned from millions of emails marked as junk. Nobody wrote a rule for this specific combination. The system found it.

The difference you can point to: In the first, you could find the specific line of code that made the decision ("if contains 'click here' → spam"). In the second, no such line exists. The decision lives in the collective weight of millions of numerical adjustments. That's the seam.

What Isn't AI (And Why It Still Matters)

Now that you've seen the seam in one place, let's make sure it holds up everywhere. Here are things you use every day that are impressive, useful, sometimes even remarkable — but are not AI.

Your calculator. You type 7 × 8. It says 56. It will always say 56. A human being wrote the multiplication rules into it decades ago, and it follows those rules every single time, perfectly, without deviation. It has never seen a million multiplication problems and spotted a pattern. It was told the answer. That's an algorithm — a set of instructions — and it's not AI.

The "sort by date" button in your photos app. It puts your newest photos at the top. A human wrote that instruction: look at the timestamp, arrange by it. Done. Not AI.

Your thermostat set to 21°C (70°F). Below 21, heat on. Above 21, heat off. You set that number. It obeys. Not AI.

An email filter you set up. "Anything from noreply@store.com goes to my Promotions folder." You wrote that rule. It fires every time, exactly as you specified. Not AI.

None of these are stupid or simple — the calculator is doing something you'd need pen and paper for. But in each case, a person decided exactly what should happen and when. The machine does precisely that, nothing more. That's automation. Calling every useful computer feature AI? As if.

💡 Quick Test Ask yourself: Could I write out the exact rule this thing is following? If yes → it's probably automation. If the answer is "I have no idea how it's deciding that" → it might be AI.

What IS AI (Real Examples You Already Use)

Now look at things that work differently.

The face grouping in Google Photos or Apple Photos. Open your phone — go to the albums or people section. Your phone has sorted hundreds, maybe thousands, of photos into groups: all the photos of your daughter in one group, your colleague in another, your dog in another.

You never told it who anyone was (or if you did, you named one photo, and it found the rest). Nobody at Apple or Google wrote a rule that says "if the nose is this shape and the eyebrows are this far apart, it's this person." That would be an insane project — you'd never finish writing it.

What happened: the system was shown millions of photos of faces, labelled by people ("same person / different person"), and it worked out its own internal rules for what makes a face that face. It generalised. It can now look at a photo of your kid at a weird angle, blurry, in bad lighting, and still group it correctly.

⏸️ "But what does 'figured out its own rules' actually look like?" Fair question. You might be picturing the machine writing down a sentence like "noses shaped like X belong to person Y" — but that's not what happens. The rules it figures out aren't in words. They're in numbers — millions of numbers that, together, represent patterns too complex for any human to write down or even describe. It's less like writing rules in a notebook and more like developing an instinct: showing someone enough faces that they get a feeling for who's who, except the machine's version of "a feeling" is a massive pile of numerical adjustments. You'll see exactly how this works in Chapter 5. For now, the key point: the machine's "rules" aren't readable. They're statistical patterns — which is part of why AI can be so hard to explain and predict.

No human wrote that rule. The machine figured it out from the training data. That's AI.

Spotify's Discover Weekly playlist. Every Monday, thirty songs you've never heard. Some of them are eerily good. Nobody at Spotify sat down and wrote "if this user likes the Cranberries, suggest Tracy Chapman."

What happened: the system looked at the listening behaviour of millions of people — what they played, what they skipped, what they put on repeat — and it found patterns. People who listen to A and B at this time of day tend to also like C. That pattern came from the data. A person didn't decide it. That's AI.

Gmail's spam filter (the modern one). An email arrives from a sender you've never seen before. It goes straight to spam. You didn't make a rule for that sender. Nobody at Google wrote a rule for that specific email. The system looked at it and — based on patterns it had learned from billions of emails that other people marked as spam or not-spam — made a judgement.

It might be wrong. But it made a decision that nobody directly programmed it to make about that specific email. It generalised from its training data. That's AI.

📌 The Pattern In every "not AI" example: a person decided exactly what should happen. In every "AI" example: a person provided a massive pile of examples, and the machine worked out the rules for itself. The person is still doing work in both cases — but it's different work. Setting the rules vs. supplying the examples.

When a Product Uses Both (Most of Them Do)

In real life, you don't encounter "AI" and "not AI" as separate things. You encounter products — and most products have both going on at the same time, in the same app, working together.

Gmail. You wrote a filter: "anything from noreply@store.com goes to my Promotions tab." That fires every time, exactly as you set it up. That's the rule you wrote — automation. But in the same inbox, Gmail is also catching spam from senders you've never heard of, blocking phishing attempts it's never seen before, sorting emails into Primary vs Social vs Promotions even when you didn't set a rule. That's the learned part — AI. Both are working on your email simultaneously.

Spotify. A playlist like "90s Jams" — where some editor at Spotify picked TLC, Alanis, No Doubt, one track at a time? That's curation. A person decided. Not AI. Your Discover Weekly? Learned from data. AI. Both live in the same app, both called "playlists," and now you can tell them apart.

A smart thermostat. When you first install it, you set it to 21°C (70°F). It obeys — that's the rule you set, not AI. Over the next two weeks, it watches when you turn it down, when you turn it up, what time you leave the house. Then it starts adjusting on its own — learning that you like it cooler at night, warmer when you wake up, off entirely by 8:30am on weekdays. That part is AI. It learned from your behaviour. Same device, both happening at once.

🔍 Sidebar: Why Does This Matter? Because "AI" has become a marketing word. Companies stick it on everything. When you see "powered by AI" on a product, you can now ask one question that cuts through it: Which part of this actually learned from data, and which part is just software following rules someone wrote?

  • Sometimes the answer is "genuinely, the core of this product learned from examples and makes judgements no person could have hand-written." That's real.
  • Sometimes the answer is "they added a chatbot to a product that's mostly hand-written rules, and called the whole thing AI." That's marketing.
  • And sometimes — most of the time, with products you actually use — it's a mix. Some parts are rules. Some parts learned. Both working together. Now you can see which is which.

⏸️ "Wait — hasn't this stuff been around for years?" Yes. Face grouping has been on your phone since about 2015. Spam filters have been learning from examples for over a decade. Spotify's Discover Weekly launched in 2015 too. So if all of that is AI... why does everyone act like AI just started in 2022? Short answer: it didn't. What happened in 2022 was a new kind of AI — one that could generate text, images, and code, and that regular people could talk to directly. Everything in this chapter is the foundation that was quietly working for years before that moment. In the next chapter, we'll meet the full family — and you'll see exactly where the 2022 explosion fits in the bigger picture.

Try This

Sort these into "automation," "AI," or "both":

  1. A spell-checker that flags any word not in its dictionary
  2. Netflix recommending a show you end up loving
  3. A microwave's "popcorn" button
  4. Your phone unlocking when it sees your face
  5. An Excel formula that calculates your monthly budget
  6. Google Maps rerouting you around a traffic jam it predicted
  7. An alarm that goes off at 7am because you set it to 7am

(Answers below — but try first.)

Answers

  1. Automation — it's checking against a fixed list a human compiled
  2. AI — it learned your preferences from your watching behaviour and millions of other viewers
  3. Automation — pre-set time and power level, written by an engineer
  4. AI — it learned what your face looks like from examples
  5. Automation — you wrote the formula, it follows it exactly
  6. Both — the route itself follows map rules (automation), but the traffic prediction learned from patterns in historical and real-time data (AI)
  7. Automation — you set the time, it obeys

What's Next

You now know the seam between ordinary software and AI. In the next chapter, we'll meet the family — because "AI" isn't one thing. It's a set of nested layers, each built on the one before, and the products you use sit at different levels. Understanding which level does what is how you'll start making sense of the headlines.

📖 Key Terms — Quick Reference Algorithm — A set of step-by-step instructions a machine follows. Automation — Software that does exactly what a human told it to do. The human writes the rules. Artificial Intelligence (AI) — A system that worked out its own rules by learning from examples (training data). The human supplies the examples, not the rules. Training data — The collection of labelled examples a system learns from. Labelling — Humans marking examples ("this is spam / this isn't," "same face / different face") so the machine can learn the difference.

Chapter 2: The AI Family (and Why 2022 Felt Like an Explosion)

Part I · What Is This Thing?

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

  • Explain the difference between machine learning, deep learning, and generative AI
  • Identify which layer a product is working at (and why it matters)
  • Understand why AI seemed to "arrive" in 2022 when it had actually been working for years
  • Describe what agentic AI is and how it differs from AI that just answers questions

Key Terms Introduced in This Chapter

TermPlain English
Machine learningThe broad category: a system that learned patterns from data (everything from Chapter 1)
Deep learningMachine learning that can handle complex, messy things — images, speech, language — not just neat rows of numbers
Neural networkThe internal structure that makes deep learning work — named after the brain, but it's just maths. We'll look inside one in Chapter 5
Generative AIAI that creates new things (text, images, code, audio) rather than just sorting or recognising
Agentic AIAI that doesn't just generate — it acts. It can use tools, remember things, plan steps, and work toward a goal
TransformerThe breakthrough design (2017) that made modern generative AI possible — better at understanding language

Everything in Chapter 1 Has a Name

Everything you learned in Chapter 1 — the spam filter that learned from examples, the face grouping, Discover Weekly — has a technical name. It's called machine learning.

A machine that learned from data instead of being handed rules. You already understand the concept; now you have the label.

The spam filters in Chapter 1? Machine learning. Face grouping? Machine learning. Spotify's recommendations? Machine learning. All of it, quietly working for well over a decade before most people started saying "AI" in everyday conversation.

When people say "AI" and "machine learning," they're usually talking about the same thing in different registers. "AI" is the broad umbrella term. "Machine learning" is the specific approach underneath that actually powers almost everything. In practice they get used interchangeably — and that's fine.

Three Kinds of AI, Three Different Jobs

In Chapter 1, you learned to spot the seam: did a human write the rules, or did the machine learn them from examples? Everything on the "learned from examples" side is machine learning. But machine learning isn't one thing — there are different kinds, and each one handles a different job. The best way to see this is to watch each one work.

Job 1: Find patterns and predict (Machine Learning)

Imagine you run a subscription service — a streaming app, a monthly snack box, whatever. Every month, some customers cancel. You want to predict which ones are about to leave, before they actually do, so you can offer them a deal or fix whatever's bothering them.

You could try writing rules yourself: "If a customer hasn't logged in for 30 days, flag them as at risk." That's automation — you wrote the rule yourself, based on a hunch. And you might be wrong. You're only looking at one thing. What about the customer who logs in every day but only watches one show and is about to cancel out of boredom? What about the one who hasn't logged in for three weeks but just paid for a full year upfront?

With machine learning, you take a different approach. You hand the system your historical data — thousands of past customers, everything you know about each one: how often they logged in, what they watched, how long they stayed per session, whether they ever contacted support, what plan they're on, how they signed up, how long they've been a customer. And you also tell it which of those people ended up cancelling.

The system looks at all of that — all those patterns across thousands of people — and finds combinations you never would have thought to look for. Maybe it discovers that customers who watch fewer than two hours a week AND originally signed up through a promotional deal AND haven't updated their payment method in six months are 80% likely to cancel within the next 30 days.

Nobody wrote that rule. Nobody even thought to look at payment method updates. The system found the pattern buried in the data, across thousands of examples.

That's the job of simple machine learning: "Look at all this historical data and find patterns that predict what's going to happen next."

How is this different from "if X then Y" (automation)? In automation, a person decides the rule: "if no login for 30 days, flag them." The person chose what to look at and what the threshold is. In machine learning, the system discovers the pattern from the data: "it's actually a combination of six factors, weighted in a specific way that no person would have written." The person supplied the data and asked the question. The machine found the answer.

Job 2: Understand complex things (Deep Learning)

Now imagine a completely different task. You're not looking at rows of data in a spreadsheet. You're looking at a photograph.

The task: "Is this a photo of a cat or a dog?"

Could the first kind of machine learning handle this? Not really. Because a photo isn't a spreadsheet. It's millions of tiny coloured dots (pixels). No single pixel tells you "cat." The cat-ness of the image lives in the relationships between thousands of pixels — the curve of an ear, the shape of an eye, the texture of fur, the proportions of the face. These aren't values in a column. They're patterns within patterns within patterns, spread across an entire image.

Simple machine learning is good at: "Given these 12 numbers about a customer, predict whether they'll cancel." It works with information that's already neat and organised.

Deep learning is good at: "Given these 3 million pixels, figure out what's in this image." It works with information that's massive, messy, and only makes sense as a whole.

Deep learning uses a structure called a neural network — and how that actually works is the subject of Chapter 5. For now, what matters is the kinds of tasks it unlocked. Here are real ones you use:

⏸️ "But what's actually different inside? Why can't simple ML handle a photo?" Here's the short version. Simple machine learning needs someone to tell it what to look at: "here are 12 measurements about each customer — login frequency, plan type, etc." A human picked those 12 things. The system finds patterns among them. Deep learning doesn't need that. You give it the raw, messy thing — all 3 million pixels of a photo — and it figures out for itself what's worth paying attention to. It builds its own hierarchy of features, layer by layer: the first layers notice edges and basic shapes. The next layers combine those into textures and patterns. Deeper layers combine those into recognisable parts — ears, noses, wheels, letters. The deepest layers combine parts into whole objects — "that's a cat," "that's a stop sign." Nobody told it "look for ears." It discovered that ears matter, on its own, because that's what helped it get the right answer across millions of training examples. That's what "deep" means — many layers, each one building more complex understanding on top of the last. You don't need to picture this precisely yet — the point is: simple ML needs neat data in columns; deep learning can handle the messy stuff directly.

  • "Who is in this photo?" — Your phone looks at millions of pixels and determines that this is the same face as the one in 400 other photos in your library. (Chapter 1's face grouping.)
  • "What words are being spoken?" — You speak into your phone, and it converts your voice (a stream of audio data — sound waves, frequencies, timing) into written text. That's not looking up words in a list — it's interpreting a messy, continuous signal.
  • "What does this sentence mean in French?" — Translation isn't just swapping words one by one. Meaning depends on word order, context, idioms. Deep learning can handle that complexity.
  • "Is there something wrong in this medical scan?" — A radiologist looks at an image. A deep learning system can look at that same image and spot patterns associated with disease — sometimes things too subtle for a human to notice.

In every case, the task is: "Look at this complex thing and tell me what's in it." Recognise. Classify. Detect. Translate. The system takes something messy and makes sense of it.

But notice: in all of these, the system is reacting to something you give it. You show it a photo, it tells you what's there. You speak, it writes down what you said. It never makes anything new. It answers questions about things that already exist.

🏆 Landmark Moment: The ImageNet Earthquake (September 2012) Every year, researchers competed to build the best image-recognition system in a contest called ImageNet. In 2012, the second-best team scored 26.2% using traditional methods — hand-crafted rules fed into algorithms. Then a team from the University of Toronto entered a deep neural network called AlexNet. It scored 15.3% — more than 10 percentage points ahead, so far beyond the field that competitors assumed it was a mistake. It wasn't. AlexNet let the system learn its own features directly from raw pixels, instead of relying on features humans had designed. Within two years, every serious computer vision team in the world had switched to deep learning. This single result ended a decades-long debate about whether neural networks actually worked at scale. The answer was yes — and nothing in AI has been the same since.

Job 3: Create something new (Generative AI)

Now a third kind of task — fundamentally different from the first two.

You don't show the system a photo and ask "what's in this?" You type: "Create an image of the inside of a 1990s library, with those chunky desktop computers and a card catalogue in the background."

There is no correct answer to look up. There's no existing thing to classify or label. You're asking the system to make something that doesn't exist yet. To create. From nothing.

Or: instead of asking "is this email spam?" you say: "Write me an email to my team explaining that Friday's meeting is moved to Monday." There's nothing to classify. You're asking it to produce a brand-new piece of writing, from scratch.

Or: "Generate a 90s-style jingle that sounds like a Spice Girls B-side." There's no music to analyse. You're asking it to compose something new.

That's generative AI. The same underlying technology (deep learning, neural networks) — but aimed at a fundamentally different job. Not "look at this and tell me what it is." Instead: "Make me something new."

⏸️ "But HOW does it create something new? How do you go from 'recognise a cat' to 'write a poem'?"

Here's the core mechanism in one paragraph — we'll go deep in Chapters 5 and 6, but the principle is simple enough to state now:

A language model (the text generators: ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini) is trained to predict what comes next. Given "The cat sat on the," it learned — from billions of examples — that "mat" is more likely than "elephant." Do this prediction one token at a time, billions of times over, and you get a system that can continue any text in a plausible direction. Ask it to continue "Dear hiring manager, I'm writing to apply for..." and it generates a cover letter — not because it "understands" applications, but because it's seen enough of them to know what tokens typically follow.

Image generators work differently — they learn to remove noise from a picture (Chapter 6). But the common thread across all generative AI is the same: the system learned so many patterns from so much data that it can produce new combinations that look coherent. It's not copying. It's pattern-completion at massive scale.

This is the layer that exploded in 2022. The AI that wrote back. The AI that drew pictures from descriptions. The AI you could have a conversation with. But it didn't appear from nowhere — to understand why it seemed to arrive overnight, we need to look at the timeline.

📌 Three Jobs — At a Glance

LayerThe jobThe question it answersExample
Machine learningFind patterns, make predictions"Given this data, what's likely to happen next?"Predicting customer cancellations, spam detection, product recommendations
Deep learningUnderstand complex inputs"What's in this photo / audio / sentence?"Face recognition, voice-to-text, translation, medical scan analysis
Generative AICreate something new"Make me something that doesn't exist yet"Writing an email, drawing an image from a description, generating code
Each layer builds on the one before it. Generative AI uses deep learning. Deep learning uses machine learning. They're nested.
How do neural networks actually process a photo or predict the next word? That's Chapter 5.

One more thing to know right now: In 2026, most frontier AI systems don't just do one of these jobs — they do several at once. You can show ChatGPT a photo AND ask a question about it in text, and it'll answer. You can ask Claude to read a chart and explain what it means. You can ask Gemini to generate an image from a description within the same conversation where it's writing text. These are called multimodal models — they handle multiple types of input and output (text, images, audio, video) in one system. When you use a modern AI product, you're typically using a multimodal model that combines all three jobs in the table above.

The Timeline: Why It Took So Long (and Then Seemed to Happen All at Once)

The term "artificial intelligence" was coined in 1956, at a summer workshop at Dartmouth College in New Hampshire. A small group of researchers proposed that "every aspect of learning or any other feature of intelligence can in principle be so precisely described that a machine can be made to simulate it." They thought it would take a generation. They were off by several.

What followed was a repeating cycle — over and over, for decades:

The 1960s–70s: Early optimism. Researchers built programs that could play checkers, solve logic puzzles, and prove mathematical theorems. The excitement was enormous. Funding poured in. People predicted human-level AI within twenty years.

The first "AI winter" (late 1970s–80s). The predictions didn't come true. The programs that could solve puzzles couldn't do anything else. They were brittle — good at one narrow task, useless at everything else. Funding dried up. People stopped talking about AI.

The 1980s: Expert systems. A new wave: instead of trying to make machines learn, researchers tried to hard-code human expertise into software. Thousands of if-then rules, manually written: "If the patient has fever AND a rash AND a stiff neck, consider meningitis." These were called AI at the time — but by our Chapter 1 definition, they're really automation (a human wrote the rules). They were expensive to build, impossible to maintain, and they broke when the world changed even slightly.

The second AI winter (late 1980s–90s). Expert systems failed to live up to their hype too. More disillusionment. More funding cuts. "AI" became almost an embarrassing term in academic circles. Researchers started calling their work "machine learning" or "pattern recognition" instead, partly to distance themselves from the broken promises.

The quiet decades (1990s–2010s). This is what was happening while nobody was paying attention. Three things were slowly, steadily growing.

  1. Data. The internet was producing more text, images, and information than had ever existed in human history. Every year, more. Every website, every email, every photo uploaded, every comment posted — all of it was potential training material.
  2. Computing power. Chips were getting faster every year. More importantly, a type of chip called a GPU (originally designed for video games — it's good at doing thousands of simple calculations at the same time) turned out to be exactly what machine learning needed. Why? Because training a neural network means adjusting millions of numbers slightly, over and over. Each individual adjustment is simple maths — but you need to do millions of them per step, and millions of steps per training run. A regular computer chip (CPU) does one thing at a time, brilliantly. A GPU does thousands of simple things at once — which is exactly what "adjust millions of numbers simultaneously" requires. By the 2010s, researchers figured out they could use GPUs to train much larger systems than ever before.
  3. Better approaches. Researchers kept refining the techniques. Deep learning (neural networks with many layers) started winning competitions in image recognition around 2012. By 2015, these systems could recognise objects in photos better than humans could. But they still couldn't generate anything — they could classify, but not create.

2017: The transformer. A team at Google published a research paper describing a new way to build neural networks, called the transformer. The paper was titled "Attention Is All You Need" — and it turned out to be one of the most important papers in the history of the field.

What made the transformer special: it was dramatically better at understanding relationships in language — which words in a sentence relate to which other words, even when they're far apart.

Here's a concrete example. In the sentence: "The cat sat on the mat because it was tired" — what does "it" refer to? Obviously the cat, not the mat. You know this instantly. But previous AI systems struggled with this — they processed words one at a time, in order, and by the time they reached "it," the cat was many words back and the connection was weak.

The transformer's breakthrough (called attention) lets the system look at ALL the words at once and ask: "For each word, which OTHER words in this sentence matter most to understanding it?" For "it," the system can directly connect back to "cat" no matter how far apart they are. That ability to handle long-range relationships in text — to keep track of what refers to what, what depends on what — is what made language AI go from "often wrong and clunky" to "impressively coherent."

⏸️ "Why does that matter so much?" Because everything we do with language involves long-range relationships. "The meeting that Sarah mentioned last week but that got rescheduled because the room was double-booked will now happen on Tuesday" — understanding that sentence requires connecting "will now happen on Tuesday" all the way back to "the meeting." The transformer is built to do exactly this. Previous approaches essentially forgot things as the sentence got longer. (We'll look at exactly how attention works in Chapter 5.)

Before transformers, language systems were limited and clunky. After, they got good. Then very good. Then good enough to fool you into thinking a person was writing.

Almost every AI system you've heard of since — ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini — is built on the transformer architecture. It's the shared design underneath the entire current generation.

🏆 Landmark Moment: AlphaGo's Move 37 (March 2016) In between the transformer breakthrough and ChatGPT's launch, one event proved what deep learning could really do. Google DeepMind built a system called AlphaGo to play Go — an ancient board game with more possible positions than there are atoms in the universe. Human experts said AI wouldn't beat a top player for another decade. AlphaGo beat Lee Sedol, one of the greatest Go players alive, 4 games to 1 in a televised match in Seoul. But the moment everyone remembers is Move 37 in Game 2: AlphaGo placed a stone in a position no human player would consider. Commentators thought it was a mistake. Lee Sedol left the room for fifteen minutes. AlphaGo won the game. The system had found a winning strategy that 3,000 years of human players had never discovered — DeepMind calculated the probability of a human making that move at roughly 1 in 10,000. This wasn't just a machine matching human ability. It was a machine finding something humans had missed entirely.

November 2022: ChatGPT. OpenAI (a company in San Francisco) released ChatGPT to the public. You could type a question and get a full, coherent, often impressively good written response. Within two months, over 100 million people were using it — the fastest-growing product launch in history at that point.

And suddenly AI was everywhere. Every company rushing to add it. Every headline about it. Every person you know asking "have you tried ChatGPT?"

But that timeline makes something clear: it didn't come from nowhere. The data had been building for twenty years. The computing power had

The AI was already there — in your spam filter, in your photo albums, in your playlists. What changed was: it became visible. It started talking back.

📰 *The Big Picture** investigates: "The Winters — Why AI 'Failed' Twice Before Getting It Right." The broken promises, the funding cuts, and the people who kept working through the quiet decades anyway.* → [link to Big Picture]

💡 Why does this matter for you? Because when you hear people say "AI is moving so fast" — you now have the context. It's not that the entire field appeared out of thin air in 2022. It's that decades of slow, steady progress crossed a threshold where the results became undeniable to everyone, all at once. The speed you're seeing now is real — but it's built on sixty years of groundwork. Understanding that timeline helps you sort hype from reality. When someone claims "this changes everything overnight" — you know that real AI breakthroughs are usually the result of many things converging over years, not one genius invention.

Agentic AI: The Layer That Acts

Everything we've covered so far — machine learning classifying, deep learning recognising, generative AI creating — shares one thing in common: you give it input, it gives you output. You ask, it answers. You prompt, it creates. But on its own, generative AI doesn't do anything in the world. It can write you a beautifully worded email, but it can't send it. It can describe the cheapest flight to Vancouver, but it can't book it.

That changed. The same chat window where a system once simply responded to your questions — or generated an image of your cat with a bob haircut (as requested) — can now take real action. Book the flight. Send the email. Fill out the form. Research across multiple websites and come back with an answer.

Agentic AI is the name for this shift — what happens when generative AI gets the ability to act. Specifically:

  • Tools — it can search the web, check a calendar, read documents, query a database, book a reservation, send a message
  • Memory — it remembers what happened earlier in the task, and sometimes across different sessions
  • A planning loop — it can take a goal ("find me a restaurant near my office that has a table for four on Friday at 7pm"), break it into steps, try each step, check if it worked, and adjust until the goal is achieved or it needs your input

The difference:

A chatbot answers your question. You ask, it responds, done.

An agent takes your goal and works toward it. It might search multiple restaurant sites, check availability, compare options, come back and ask "there's one Italian place and one Thai place available — which do you prefer?", and then actually make the booking once you confirm.

The key difference: an agent has a loop. It doesn't just respond once — it acts, observes the result, decides what to do next, acts again, and keeps going until the job is done or it needs you.

What the loop actually looks like, step by step:

Say you ask: "Find me a restaurant near my office, Italian, table for four on Friday at 7pm."

StepWhat the agent doesWhat happens
1Plans: "I need to search for Italian restaurants near [your office address], then check availability for Friday at 7pm for 4 people"Breaks the goal into sub-tasks
2Acts: Searches a restaurant database or the web for Italian restaurants in your areaUses a tool (web search)
3Observes: Gets back 8 resultsReads the output
4Reasons: "3 of these are too far. 2 don't take reservations online. 3 are viable options."Filters and decides
5Acts: Checks availability on each of the 3 viable restaurants for Friday 7pm, 4 peopleUses another tool (booking site)
6Observes: Two have tables available, one is fully bookedReads the output
7Decides: "I have two options. I should ask the user which they prefer."Decides it needs your input
8Responds to you: "I found two options: Trattoria Roma (0.3 km / 0.2 miles, 4.5 stars) or Bella Notte (0.6 km / 0.4 miles, 4.8 stars). Which would you like me to book?"Comes back with a question

That cycle — plan, act, observe, reason, act again — is what makes it an agent instead of a chatbot. A chatbot would have just said "Here are some Italian restaurants near you" and stopped. The agent kept working.

And this isn't a future prediction — it's happening right now, in 2026, in the same chat windows you already use. The technology powering what's behind that chat window has evolved from "respond to text" to "take action in the real world." How that works — what's actually different in the system behind the scenes that lets it do this — is something we'll build up to across the next several chapters.

This is the newest and fastest-moving layer of AI. We'll spend a full chapter on it later (Chapter 14) — including how agents work, what "tools" actually means in practice, and why giving AI the ability to act (not just talk) raises important questions about safety and trust.

📌 The Four Layers — Complete Picture

LayerWhat it doesExample
Machine learningLearns patterns from structured dataSpam filter, Discover Weekly, "people who bought X also bought Y"
Deep learningHandles complex inputs — images, audio, languageFace grouping, voice dictation, translation
Generative AICreates new contentChatGPT writing an email, image generators, AI coding tools
Agentic AITakes actions toward a goalAn AI assistant that researches, books, and sends on your behalf
Each layer builds on the one before it. An agent uses generative AI, which uses deep learning, which uses machine learning. They're nested.

Variations Within the Family: Size, Openness, and Thinking

You now have the four-layer framework. But within generative AI specifically, there are variations you'll keep seeing in the news — and they represent genuinely different trade-offs that affect what you can do with them. Think of this section as a quick field guide you can come back to. You've probably noticed that AI news throws around terms like "open-source model," "foundation model," "small language model," "reasoning model" — as if you're already supposed to know what these mean. Here's what they actually are:

Foundation models — what makes something "foundational"?

When OpenAI built GPT-4, they didn't build it for one task. They trained it broadly — on massive, diverse data — so it could be adapted to many different tasks afterward. GPT-4, Claude, Gemini, Llama — these are all foundation models. The word "foundation" means: this is the base that many products and applications are built on top of. The same foundation model might power a customer support bot, a coding assistant, and a creative writing tool — each adapted through different system prompts, fine-tuning, or RAG (all of which you'll learn in Chapter 9).

Small language models — why smaller is sometimes better

Not every task needs a trillion-parameter model. A model with 3–7 billion parameters can be fast enough to run on a phone, cheap enough to call millions of times per day, and good enough for focused tasks (summarisation, classification, simple Q&A). These are small language models — often distilled from larger ones (Chapter 9). The trade-off is straightforward: less capable at complex reasoning, but faster, cheaper, and deployable on devices without an internet connection.

Open-weight vs closed models — what you actually get

When Meta releases Llama as "open-weight," it means: you can download the trained weights (the file of numbers from Chapter 5), run the model on your own hardware, fine-tune it for your purposes, and inspect what's inside. You don't get the training data, the training code, or the RLHF preference data — but you get the finished model.

When OpenAI offers GPT-4 as a "closed" model, you can only access it through their API. You send input, you get output. You can't see the weights, can't run it locally, can't modify it, can't inspect its internals. You rent access; you don't own the thing.

Why this matters: open-weight models give you control (you can run them privately, modify them, deploy them anywhere) but require technical expertise and hardware. Closed models are easier to use (just call the API) but you depend entirely on the provider — their pricing, their policies, their availability, their decisions about what the model will and won't do.

Reasoning/thinking models — what "thinking out loud" does

In 2024–2025, a new variation emerged: models that think before responding. OpenAI's o1, Claude's "extended thinking," and similar systems pause before answering, working through the problem step by step in a hidden reasoning trace. You wait longer — maybe 10–30 seconds instead of 2 — but the answer to complex problems (maths, logic, multi-step planning, coding) is substantially better.

What's happening mechanically: the model generates a chain of reasoning tokens before producing its final answer. Those reasoning tokens aren't shown to you (or are shown collapsed), but they function like scratch paper — the model works through the problem, catches its own errors, considers alternatives, and then produces a more considered response. It's doing extra internal work before answering (which takes longer and costs more) in exchange for better accuracy on hard problems.

The trade-off: slower and more expensive per request, but dramatically better on tasks that require genuine multi-step reasoning. For simple tasks ("what's the weather?"), thinking models are overkill. For complex tasks ("find the bug in this 500-line function"), the extra thinking time pays off.

What's Next

You now know the family — machine learning, deep learning, generative AI, agentic AI — and you know that all of them learned from data.

But what data? Where does it come from? Who collects it, who prepares it, and who does the unglamorous work of labelling millions of examples so machines can learn from them? Because the single most important rule in all of AI turns out to be uncomfortably simple: what comes out is only as good as what went in.

That's Chapter 3.

📖 Key Terms — Quick Reference Machine Learning (ML) — Any system that learns patterns from data instead of following human-written rules. The dominant approach to AI since the 2000s. Coined 1959. Deep Learning — Machine learning that can handle complex, unstructured inputs (images, audio, language) using neural networks with many layers. Neural Network — The internal structure that powers deep learning. We'll look inside one in Chapter 5. Generative AI — AI that creates new content (text, images, code, audio) rather than just classifying or recognising existing content. The layer that became visible in 2022. Agentic AI — AI that acts toward goals using tools, memory, and a planning loop. Doesn't just answer — does things. Transformer — The neural network design (published 2017, Google) that made modern generative AI possible. Better at understanding relationships between words in language. GPU — A type of computer chip (originally for video games) that turned out to be ideal for training AI — because it can do thousands of calculations at the same time.

Chapter 3: Data — Everything Starts Here

Part II · How a Machine Learns

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

  • Explain where AI training data comes from and why it matters
  • Understand what "labelling" is and who actually does it (real people, real work)
  • Describe why biased data leads to biased AI — and spot where that bias enters
  • Explain what "garbage in, garbage out" means in practice
  • Understand what synthetic data is and why it's both useful and risky

Key Terms Introduced in This Chapter

TermPlain English
Training dataThe examples a system learns from — the textbook it studies before it can do the job
Labelling / annotationHumans marking examples with the right answer ("this is spam," "this is a cat," "this response is better than that one") so the system can learn what "correct" looks like
Supervised learningLearning from labelled examples — the system gets both the question and the correct answer
Unsupervised learningLearning from unlabelled data — the system finds patterns on its own without being told the "right" answer
Bias (in AI)When the training data over- or under-represents certain groups, perspectives, or scenarios — causing the system to produce skewed results
Synthetic dataTraining data generated by AI rather than collected from the real world
Data flywheelWhen using a product generates more data, which improves the product, which attracts more users, which generates more data...
Web crawlAn automated process that visits web pages, follows links, and downloads text — the way most internet training data is collected
Common CrawlA nonprofit that has crawled and archived over 9.5 petabytes of web data since 2008, freely available — the single biggest source of training data for AI
Model collapseWhen AI trains on AI-generated data across multiple generations, progressively losing diversity and accuracy until the output becomes bland and narrow

The Textbook Comes Before the Test

In 2015, Google released an image recognition system. Users quickly discovered it was labelling photos of Black people as "gorillas." Google's immediate fix: remove the "gorilla" label entirely from the system. As of 2026, most image recognition systems still don't have that label — not because they fixed the underlying problem, but because they couldn't figure out how.

The system wasn't broken in the way most people assumed. It wasn't programmed to be racist. It wasn't "thinking" anything. It had been trained on a dataset that didn't contain enough diverse examples of faces — so the patterns it learned were skewed. The system worked exactly as designed: it learned whatever was in the training data. And the training data was inadequate.

Everything in AI starts here. The spam filter learned from millions of emails marked "spam" or "not spam." The face grouping system learned from millions of photos labelled "same face / different face." ChatGPT learned from enormous amounts of text from the internet. All of it — the examples, the text, the photos, the labels — is training data.

And the single most important thing to understand about AI is this:

The system can only learn what's in the data.

If the training data is good — accurate, representative, diverse, well-labelled — the system has a decent shot at learning well. If the data is incomplete, skewed, or full of errors, the system will learn those problems too. It has no way to know the difference. It just learns whatever patterns are in the pile.

What makes this harder than it sounds: nobody fully agrees on what "representative" means. Representative of what? Of the internet (which over-represents English, Western perspectives, and popular domains)? Of the world's population (but whose version of the world)? Of "quality" writing (but who decides what quality is)? Every choice about what data to include and exclude is a choice about what the system will and won't know — and those choices are made by small teams at companies, often without public input. This isn't a solved problem. It's a permanent tension.

This is sometimes called "garbage in, garbage out" — but that phrase makes it sound like only obviously terrible data causes problems. The truth is more subtle: even data that looks fine can contain patterns you didn't intend to teach.

Where Training Data Comes From

Different kinds of AI need different kinds of training data. Let's look at where it actually comes from:

For large language models (the systems you chat with)

The training data is text. Enormous amounts of it. Where?

  • The open internet — web pages, Wikipedia articles, forums, blog posts, news sites. Billions of pages, crawled and collected.
  • Books — digitised libraries, published works (this has caused legal battles over copyright).
  • Code repositories — publicly shared code on platforms like GitHub, which is why these systems can write code.
  • Conversations — public discussions, social media posts, comment threads.

When people say "it was trained on the internet" — they mean it quite literally. These systems studied a significant chunk of everything that's ever been written and published online.

For image recognition systems (like face grouping)

The training data is labelled photos. Millions of images, each one marked by a human:

  • "This is a cat."
  • "This is the same person as that photo."
  • "This photo contains a stop sign, a pedestrian, and a bicycle."

Somebody had to look at each of those images and write those labels. (More on who does this in a moment.)

For recommendation systems (like Spotify or Netflix)

The training data is your behaviour. What you listened to, what you skipped, what you watched all the way through, what you abandoned after three minutes. Millions of users generating this data constantly, just by using the product.

For spam filters

The training data is labelled emails — every time you (and millions of other people) click "this is spam" or "this isn't spam," you're adding to the pile of examples the system learns from.

But how do you actually get all that data?

Saying "trained on the internet" sounds simple. In practice, collecting training data at the scale needed for a large language model is a massive industrial operation. The process:

Step 1: Crawling. A program called a web crawler (or spider) visits web pages automatically, following links from one page to the next, downloading the text as it goes. It's the same technology Google uses to build its search index — except instead of building search results, you're building a training set.

The single biggest source of crawled data for AI is a nonprofit called Common Crawl. Since 2008, they've been running web crawlers continuously, archiving what they find, and making the entire archive freely available. As of now, their archive is over 9.5 petabytes — that's roughly equivalent to 10 billion books' worth of text, or billions of web pages. Every major AI company — OpenAI, Google, Anthropic, Meta, Amazon — has used Common Crawl data for training.

A Mozilla Foundation report called it "training data for the price of a sandwich" — because Common Crawl gives it away free. The cost of actually building a large language model is in the compute, not the data collection.

Step 2: Cleaning. Raw web crawls are messy. They contain navigation menus, cookie notices, duplicate pages, spam sites, porn, legal boilerplate, garbled HTML. Before training, you have to filter and clean the text — remove duplicates, strip out junk, decide which pages are "high quality" and which aren't. This filtering step has enormous power: whoever decides what counts as "quality" shapes what the system learns.

Step 3: Deduplication. The same content appears on thousands of sites (copied articles, syndicated content, legal disclaimers). If you don't remove duplicates, the system will over-learn those patterns. So you run deduplication algorithms to find and remove copies.

Step 4: Filtering. Decide what stays and what goes. Common Crawl data skews heavily English, heavily Western, heavily toward well-linked popular domains. Pages from smaller communities, non-English languages, and digitally marginalised groups are under-represented. This is where bias enters before a single line of AI code has run.

Beyond Common Crawl — other routes to training data:

Common Crawl is the biggest free source, but not the only route. Companies building AI systems also:

  • Run their own crawlers — scraping the web themselves for fresher or more targeted data. This has gotten harder as websites increasingly block AI crawlers using robots.txt files and anti-bot systems.
  • License proprietary data — paying for access to content that isn't publicly available. News archives, academic papers, private forums. Reddit, for example, started charging AI companies for access to its archive of conversations.
  • Contract data labelling companies — for the supervised learning parts (where you need human-marked examples), companies like Scale AI or Surge AI produce labelled datasets to specification.
  • Use their own users' behaviour — if you already have a product with millions of users, their interactions generate training data continuously. This is the data flywheel we'll come back to later in this chapter.

⏸️ Wait — they just took the whole internet? More or less. Common Crawl's position is that they're archiving publicly available web pages, similar to how the Internet Archive (the Wayback Machine) does. AI companies' position is that training on publicly available text is transformative use, similar to how Google indexes the web for search. Not everyone agrees. Authors, artists, news organisations, and content creators have filed lawsuits arguing that using their work as training data without permission or payment is copyright infringement. These cases are still working through courts as of 2026. The outcome will shape who gets to build AI and from what.

📰 *The Big Picture** investigates: "They Trained It on Everything — Who Gave Permission?" The copyright wars, the lawsuits, and the question of whether your words on the internet belong to you or to the next AI model.* → [link to Big Picture]

💡 You're contributing training data right now Every time you use a product and it gets slightly better — your Netflix recommendations improve over time, your spam filter gets smarter — that's because your usage is generating data that the system learns from. You are both the user and the teacher. This is a data flywheel: more users → more data → better system → more users → more data. It's one reason early leaders in AI are hard to catch — they have a head start in data that competitors can't easily replicate.

Labelling: The Human Work Behind AI

Behind almost every AI system, there is (or was) an army of humans doing painstaking, repetitive work.

The task: Look at an example. Mark it with the correct answer. Repeat. Thousands of times.

For image recognition: humans draw boxes around objects in photos. "This box contains a car." "This box contains a person." "This box contains a traffic light." For self-driving cars alone, millions of images need every object outlined and named.

For spam filters: humans look at emails and mark "spam" or "not spam."

For large language models: the work is more complex. Humans are shown two possible responses from the system and asked "which one is better?" Which response is more helpful, more accurate, less harmful? These judgements get fed back to shape the system's behaviour. (This is a technique called RLHF — reinforcement learning from human feedback — which we'll cover properly in Chapter 9.)

Who does this work?

It's a global industry. Companies like Scale AI, Surge AI, and others employ tens of thousands of workers — some in the US, many in Kenya, the Philippines, India, and other countries. The work ranges from simple (label this image) to highly skilled (evaluate whether this medical summary is accurate). Pay varies enormously — from a few cents per task for simple labelling to $50+/hour for expert evaluators.

This work is often invisible. When people say "AI learned to do X," the hidden story is almost always: "thousands of people spent months marking examples so the system could learn." The human labour is baked into every AI system you use.

📰 *The Big Picture** investigates: "The Invisible Workforce — Who Labels the Internet?" The people behind the data, the gig economy of AI annotation, and what happens when the humans training AI are underpaid, unseen, and shaping how machines understand the world.* → [link to Big Picture]

⏸️ Wait — so AI doesn't "learn on its own"? Not in the way it sounds. The system does learn patterns autonomously — nobody writes the rules it discovers. But someone has to prepare the textbook. Someone has to mark the examples. Someone has to judge what "good" looks like. That human work is what the system learns from. When you hear "AI is replacing human workers" — remember that AI was built by human workers. Thousands of them, doing labelling work that most people will never see.

Supervised, Unsupervised, and the Spectrum Between

The training we've described so far — showing the system labelled examples and letting it learn the pattern — is called supervised learning. Supervised because a human provided the "right answers" (the labels).

  • Spam filter: supervised. Humans labelled emails as spam/not spam.
  • Face grouping: supervised. Humans labelled photos as same face/different face.
  • Customer churn prediction: supervised. The historical data showed which customers did and didn't cancel — those are the labels.

But not all learning needs labels.

Unsupervised learning is when the system finds patterns in data without being told what the right answer is. Nobody labels anything. You just hand it a massive pile of data and say "find the structure."

Example: you give a system a million customer purchase histories and say "group similar customers together." You don't tell it what the groups should be. It finds clusters — maybe one group buys luxury items on weekends, another group buys budget items in bulk, another buys only during sales. Nobody defined those categories. The system discovered them.

⏸️ "But what does 'find the structure' actually mean? What's it doing?" A concrete example. Imagine each customer as a dot on a giant map. Customers who buy similar things are placed near each other. Customers who buy different things are placed far apart. You don't tell the system how many groups exist, or what they should be. You just say: "Find the clusters — which dots are naturally close together?" The system measures distance between customers (based on what they bought, when, how often, how much they spent) and groups together the ones that are closest to each other. Nobody defined "budget shopper" or "luxury weekender" — the system noticed that certain customers naturally cluster together because their behaviour is similar to each other and different from the rest. That's unsupervised learning: instead of a human providing answers, the system discovers structure by measuring similarity.

Another example: much of the pre-training for large language models is unsupervised. The system reads billions of sentences and learns the patterns of language — which words tend to follow which other words, how sentences are structured, how ideas connect. Nobody labelled each sentence with "here's what this means." It just absorbed the patterns.

⏸️ "Wait — how can it learn language without anyone marking what's correct?" Because the text itself provides the training signal. Here's the trick: you take a sentence, hide the last word, and ask the system to predict it. "The cat sat on the ___" The right answer is right there in the original text — "mat" (or "couch" or "floor"). You don't need a human to label it. The text IS the label. Do this billions of times, with billions of sentences, and the system learns the patterns of language — grammar, meaning, facts, style — entirely from predicting what comes next. This is called self-supervised learning (a flavour of unsupervised) — the system creates its own labels from the structure of the data itself. It's how every large language model learns the basics before any human feedback shapes it.

In practice, most modern AI systems use a combination — a phase of unsupervised learning (absorb patterns from massive unlabelled data) followed by supervised fine-tuning (humans provide specific labelled examples or feedback to steer it in the right direction).

Garbage In, Garbage Out (And the Subtle Version)

You've probably heard the phrase "garbage in, garbage out." In AI, it means: if the training data is bad, the system will be bad. But "bad" is more nuanced than it sounds.

The obvious version

If you train a spam filter on badly labelled data — where a bunch of legitimate emails were accidentally marked as spam — the filter will learn wrong patterns and start binning real messages. Bad labels → bad system. Straightforward.

The subtle version (bias)

This is where it gets more important, and harder to see.

Imagine you're building a hiring tool. You want it to look at job applications and predict which candidates will be successful. So you train it on your company's historical data — the last ten years of applications and outcomes.

Here's the problem: if your company, for whatever reason, historically hired mostly men for engineering roles, then the training data shows a pattern: successful candidates tend to be men. The system learns that pattern. It's not "being sexist" — it has no concept of gender or fairness. It just found the pattern in the data: historically, the people who got hired and stayed had certain characteristics, and gender was correlated with those outcomes.

This actually happened. In 2018, Amazon discovered that an internal hiring tool they'd been developing was systematically downgrading applications from women. It had learned the bias in the historical data. They scrapped the project.

Where bias enters:

  1. The data reflects the world's existing inequalities. If the internet has more text written by men, the system's language patterns skew male. If historical hiring data reflects past discrimination, the system learns to discriminate.
  2. The data over-represents some groups and under-represents others. If a face recognition system is trained mostly on light-skinned faces, it will be less accurate on darker-skinned faces. (This has been documented repeatedly — studies have found error rates 10–100x higher for darker-skinned women compared to lighter-skinned men.)
  3. The labels themselves can be biased. If the humans doing the labelling bring their own assumptions ("this writing style sounds unprofessional" based on cultural expectations), those assumptions get baked into the training data.

What this means for you:

When an AI system makes a decision that affects you — whether a loan gets approved, which résumés get seen, whether content gets flagged — the system is applying patterns it learned from historical data. If that history contains bias, the system reproduces it. At scale. Faster than any human could.

This isn't a solvable-in-one-step problem. It's an ongoing challenge the entire field is working on. But now you know where it enters: the data.

📰 *The Big Picture** investigates: "Bias by Default — When AI Learns the Wrong Lesson." From Amazon's hiring tool to facial recognition failures, the real-world consequences when training data carries the past's prejudices into the future's decisions.* → [link to Big Picture]

📌 Bias in AI — The Core Issue AI doesn't "decide" to be biased. It finds patterns in the data it was given. If the data reflects existing inequalities, the system learns those inequalities as if they're just how the world works. The system has no way to distinguish "a pattern that exists because of historical discrimination" from "a pattern that exists because it's genuinely useful." That's why the people preparing and choosing training data have enormous power — even if most of them are invisible to the end user.

Synthetic Data: When AI Trains AI

There's a newer approach gaining traction: instead of collecting real-world data, you generate fake data that looks like real data. This is called synthetic data. Or, as the 1996 cinematic masterpiece Scream taught us... the call is coming from inside the house.

Why would you do this?

  • Privacy. If you need medical data to train a system, you can't just share real patient records. But you can generate fake patient records that have the same statistical patterns without belonging to any real person.
  • Cost. Labelling millions of real images is expensive and slow. Generating synthetic images (with automatic labels built in) is faster and cheaper.
  • Rare scenarios. If you're training a self-driving car, you can't wait around for a thousand near-miss accidents to happen. You can simulate them.
  • Running out of real data. We're approaching a point where AI systems have been trained on most of the high-quality text available on the internet. There's only so much human-written text in existence. Synthetic data offers a way to keep going.

One technical distinction matters before we get to the risk: this does not usually mean one model is training itself. A model generates new examples, and those examples may then be selected or mixed into the data used to train another model. Synthetic data can be useful; the danger comes from how it is produced, checked, mixed, and reused over time.

What's the risk? (Model collapse)

If AI generates training data, and that data trains the next AI, and that AI generates data for the next one... what happens?

Researchers call the answer model collapse. A 2024 paper in Nature (one of the most prestigious scientific journals) demonstrated it clearly: when AI systems train on data produced by earlier AI systems across multiple generations, they progressively lose information. The edges of what they know — the unusual, the rare, the diverse — disappear first. Over several generations, the system converges toward something bland and narrow, a version of itself that has forgotten the full range of what human-produced data actually looks like.

It's not that one round of synthetic data ruins everything. The picture is more like this:

  • First generation: Synthetic data is pretty good. It captures most of the patterns in the original data.
  • Second, third generation: The rare and unusual examples start dropping out. The system's "knowledge" becomes more generic.
  • Later generations: The output becomes increasingly homogeneous. Variety collapses. It's like making a photocopy of a photocopy of a photocopy — each time, you lose a little detail, and what's left gets blurrier and more uniform.

Can you avoid it?

Yes — partially. Researchers have found that if you mix synthetic data with real human-produced data (rather than replacing real data entirely), you can keep the system stable. The real data acts as an anchor, preventing the drift. The key finding: you can't go fully synthetic. The system always needs some connection to genuine human output to stay grounded.

Why this matters right now:

The internet is already filling up with AI-generated content. Blog posts, articles, social media comments, product reviews — an increasing percentage of text online was written by AI systems. That means the next generation of AI systems, trained on web crawls from 2025 and 2026, will inevitably be training on text that was itself produced by AI. Model collapse isn't a theoretical risk for the future. It's a problem the industry is dealing with today — trying to identify and filter AI-generated text from training data, or at minimum, managing the ratio.

📌 Model Collapse — The Short Version When AI trains on AI-generated data across multiple generations, it progressively loses diversity and accuracy. Rare patterns disappear first. Eventually, the system becomes a flattened, generic version of itself. Mixing real and synthetic data helps — going fully synthetic doesn't work.

📰 *The Big Picture** investigates: "The Ouroboros Problem — What Happens When AI Eats Its Own Tail?" How the internet is filling with AI-generated content, and what that means for the next generation of AI systems.* → [link to Big Picture]

The Data Flywheel: Why It's Hard to Catch the Leaders

One more concept that helps explain how AI compounds: the data flywheel.

Here's how it works:

  1. A company launches a product (like Gmail, or Spotify, or Google Search).
  2. People use it. Their usage generates data (what they clicked, what they searched, what they marked as spam).
  3. That data improves the AI behind the product.
  4. A better product attracts more users.
  5. More users generate more data.
  6. Back to step 3.

This is why companies like Google, Meta, and Spotify have such a head start in AI — they've been collecting usage data from billions of people for over a decade. That data is a resource no competitor can buy or shortcut. It's also why these companies offer products for free: you are the product, in the sense that your usage generates the data that makes their AI better.

🏆 Landmark Moment: AlphaFold Solves the Protein Problem (November 2020) For fifty years, biologists had struggled with a fundamental question: if you know the sequence of amino acids that make up a protein, can you predict the 3D shape it folds into? That shape determines what the protein does — and understanding it is key to developing new medicines. Solving one protein's structure in a lab used to take months or years. DeepMind's AlphaFold 2 system predicted protein structures with lab-level accuracy in seconds. It then released predicted structures for over 200 million proteins — nearly every known protein — freely available to researchers worldwide. The breakthrough won the Nobel Prize in Chemistry in 2024. But here's the data story: AlphaFold could only exist because decades of biologists had painstakingly determined ~170,000 protein structures through lab experiments and deposited them in a public database. That database was AlphaFold's training data. Fifty years of careful, invisible data work — then one system that could learn from all of it at once.

Try This

Think about a product you use every day. Pick one: Spotify, Netflix, Gmail, Google Maps, Instagram, TikTok, Amazon shopping.

Now answer:

  1. What kind of data is it collecting from you? (What you click, what you watch, what you skip, what you buy, where you are...)
  2. How does that data make the product better for you over time?
  3. Can you think of a way that data could be biased — where it might not represent everyone equally?

A harder one: An insurance company wants to use AI to predict which customers will make a claim. They train it on ten years of historical claims data.

  • What patterns might the system learn?
  • Could any of those patterns be unfair — reflecting historical discrimination rather than actual risk?
  • Who might be disadvantaged by this system, and why?

There's no single right answer — but if you can think through this, you understand the chapter.

What's Next

You now know where AI's knowledge comes from: data. Real-world data, collected and labelled by people, reflecting the world as it is — with all its strengths and flaws.

But how does a system actually learn from that data? You've been told "it finds patterns" — but what does that look like inside? How does it go from "here's a pile of examples" to "I can now recognise faces / write emails / predict the future"?

That's Chapter 4: Tokens — how a machine actually reads the data before it can learn from it. Before training can even begin, the data has to be translated into a form the machine can process. That process is more important (and more interesting) than it sounds.

📖 Key Terms — Quick Reference Training data — The examples a system learns from. All AI starts here. Labelling (annotation) — Humans marking examples with correct answers so the system can learn what "right" looks like. Web crawl — An automated process that visits web pages, follows links, and downloads text at scale. How most internet training data is collected. Common Crawl — A nonprofit that has been archiving the web since 2008. Over 9.5 petabytes of freely available data. The single biggest source of training text for AI. Supervised learning — Learning from labelled examples (the system gets questions AND answers). Unsupervised learning — Learning from unlabelled data (the system finds patterns without being told what to look for). Bias — When training data over- or under-represents certain groups or perspectives, causing the system to produce skewed results. Enters through the data, not through the system "deciding" to be unfair. Garbage in, garbage out — If the data is bad or biased, the system learns bad or biased patterns. Synthetic data — Artificially generated data used for training. Useful for privacy, cost, and rare scenarios. Model collapse — When AI trains on AI-generated data across multiple generations, progressively losing diversity and accuracy. The rare and unusual disappear first; the output becomes bland and narrow. Avoidable by mixing in real human-produced data. Data flywheel — A self-reinforcing cycle: more users → more data → better product → more users. RLHF (Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback) — A technique where humans judge AI outputs ("this response is better than that one") and that feedback shapes the system's behaviour. Covered fully in Chapter 9.

Chapter 4: Tokens — How the Machine Reads

Part II · How a Machine Learns

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

  • Explain what a token is and why AI doesn't read words the way you do
  • Understand why everything in AI is measured in tokens (cost, limits, speed)
  • Describe how text, images, and audio all get chopped into tokens before processing
