  • Explain what a "context window" is and why conversations have a length limit
  • Understand why token limits matter to you as a user — and how they affect what AI can do

Key Terms Introduced in This Chapter

TermPlain English
TokenA chunk of text the system processes as a single unit — not a word, not a letter, something in between
TokenisationThe process of chopping input (text, images, audio) into tokens before the system can work with it
VocabularyThe set of all tokens a system knows — its dictionary of chunks
Context windowThe maximum number of tokens a system can "see" at once — its working memory for a single conversation
Input tokensThe tokens in what you send (your question, your document, the conversation history)
Output tokensThe tokens the system generates in response

The Problem: Machines Don't Read

Ask an AI "how many r's are in the word strawberry?" and it will confidently tell you two. The answer is three. Try it yourself — this isn't a rare glitch. As of 2026, even frontier models get this wrong more often than they get it right.

Why would a system that can write poetry, explain quantum physics, and pass the bar exam fail at something a six-year-old can do?

The answer is in how the machine reads — or rather, how it doesn't read. Because a machine can't read English. It can't read any language. It doesn't see words, and it definitely doesn't see individual letters. What it works with is numbers — sequences of numbers. Every piece of maths inside the system operates on numbers, and your text has to become numbers before anything can happen.

The way text becomes numbers: it gets chopped into chunks — not words, not letters, something in between. Those chunks are called tokens. And the process of chopping is tokenisation.

This sounds like a minor technical detail. It isn't. Tokens are the fundamental unit of everything in AI: how the system reads, how it thinks, how fast it responds, how much it costs, and how much it can hold in its head at once. Understanding tokens is understanding the currency the entire system runs on.

And that strawberry question? The model never sees the individual letters. It sees straw + berry (or similar chunks) — and from those chunks, it has no way to inspect which letters appear inside. That's not a bug in its intelligence. It's a direct consequence of how this chopping works. Let's look at it.

What a Token Actually Is

📼 Rewind it: If you ever cut words and letters from magazines for a school-project title, you already know the basic move: build new text from a fixed box of reusable pieces. A tokeniser also works with reusable pieces — but its pieces may be a whole word, part of one, punctuation, or one character. It is not reading the pieces or deciding what they mean.

The intuition is simple: a token is a word. One word = one token.

That's close, but wrong in ways that matter.

A token is a chunk — sometimes a whole word, sometimes part of a word, sometimes just a single character or a punctuation mark. The system has a fixed vocabulary of these chunks (typically 50,000–100,000 of them), and every piece of text you give it gets broken into pieces from that vocabulary.

Real examples:

The sentence "I love playing basketball" might get split like this:

TextTokens
II
lovelove
playingplay + ing
basketballbasket + ball

"Love" is common enough to be one token. "Playing" gets split into "play" and "ing" — because "ing" appears at the end of thousands of words, so the system learned to treat it as its own chunk. "Basketball" splits into "basket" + "ball" — two familiar pieces.

Now a less common word: "tokenisation" might split into token + isation — or even token + is + ation, depending on the specific system.

And something unusual — a rare word, a name, a technical term the system hasn't seen often — gets chopped into smaller and smaller pieces, sometimes all the way down to individual letters.

The rule of thumb: In English, one token is roughly three-quarters of a word. So 100 words ≈ 130 tokens. A full page of text is about 500–700 tokens.

💡 Why not just use whole words? Because there are too many of them. English alone has hundreds of thousands of words, plus names, technical terms, slang, new words being invented constantly. You'd need an infinitely large vocabulary. By splitting into subword chunks, the system can handle any word it's never seen before — it just breaks it into smaller familiar pieces. It's a balance between efficiency (common words stay whole) and flexibility (rare words get broken down).

Think of it like Scrabble tiles — but instead of individual letters, you have tiles for common chunks: one tile for "the", one for "ing", one for "un", one for "tion". Common words are just one tile. Unusual words take several tiles pushed together. You can spell anything with enough tiles — but common things are efficient (one tile), and rare things cost more tiles. The model's "vocabulary" is its fixed set of tiles, and everything it reads or writes must be built from that set.

How Tokenisation Works (The Chopping Process)

The system doesn't choose how to split text on the fly. Its vocabulary — the full list of chunks it knows — was decided during a training step before the system ever saw your message. Here's how that vocabulary gets built:

Step 1: Start with individual characters. Every letter, number, and punctuation mark is a token: a, b, c, ... z, 0, 1, ... 9, ., ,, !, etc.

Step 2: Look at a massive pile of text. Find the pair of adjacent tokens that appears together most often. Maybe t and h appear next to each other more than any other pair. Merge them into a new token: th.

Step 3: Repeat. Now find the next most common pair. Maybe th and e → merge into the. Now "the" is a single token.

Step 4: Keep going — thousands of merges. Common words become single tokens. Common word-parts (like ing, tion, un, re) become tokens. Eventually you reach your target vocabulary size (say, 100,000 tokens) and stop.

This process is called Byte Pair Encoding (BPE), and it's what most major language models use — GPT, Claude, and others all build their vocabularies this way.

The result: common English words are typically one token each. Less common words split into 2-3 pieces. Very rare words might split into many small pieces. And the system can handle anything — even nonsense text or a language it barely knows — by falling back to smaller and smaller chunks.

⏸️ Why does this matter to you? Because the system "thinks" in tokens, not words. When it generates a response, it's producing one token at a time — not one word at a time. When it hits a limit, it's a token limit, not a word limit. When you're charged for using an AI, you're charged per token, not per word. The system's entire economy runs on these chunks.

🔍 Concept in Practice: Why AI can't count the r's in "strawberry" You may have seen this: ask an AI "how many r's are in strawberry?" and it confidently answers "2." The answer is 3. Why does it get this wrong? Because the model never sees individual letters. It sees tokens — and "strawberry" likely gets split into chunks like str + aw + berry. The system processes those chunks as units. It has no way to examine individual characters inside a token, the same way you can't easily count the number of times a specific thread appears in a woven fabric just by looking at the overall pattern. That's tokenisation in action: the model is extraordinarily good at language — grammar, meaning, reasoning, style — because it operates at the chunk level. But tasks that require character-level inspection (counting specific letters, reversing words letter by letter, checking spelling character by character) are structurally hard for it. Not a bug in the intelligence — a direct consequence of how the input gets chopped up before the model ever sees it.

Context Windows: The System's Working Memory

📼 Rewind it: Cher's closet computer was useful because it had the available clothes, the occasion, and the choice in front of it. Context is that temporary working set: what the system can see for this decision. It is not permanent memory, and it is not a perfect copy of your world.

Here's where tokens become something you feel directly as a user.

Every AI system has a context window — the maximum number of tokens it can hold in its working memory at once. Everything the system can "see" during a single conversation has to fit inside this window: your message, the system's instructions, any documents you've attached, and the entire conversation history up to that point. In this case, Mean Girls got it backwards: "The limit does not exist." Here, it does.

How big are context windows right now (2026)?

ModelContext windowRoughly equivalent to...
Older models (2023)4,000–8,000 tokensA few pages of text
GPT-4 (2023)128,000 tokensA short novel (~96,000 words)
Current frontier models (2026)1,000,000 tokens~750,000 words — roughly 1,500 pages
Gemini 3.1 Pro10,000,000 tokens~7.5 million words — an entire bookshelf

Context windows have grown roughly 1,000x in five years. In 2022, a system could barely hold a long essay in memory. Now, frontier models can hold the equivalent of multiple books at once.

⏸️ "But WHY is there a limit at all? Why can't it just hold everything?"

Because of attention (Chapter 5). Remember: at each step, the model calculates how every token relates to every other token. The number of calculations grows explosively — double the tokens and you quadruple the work. If you have 1,000 tokens, the model computes 1,000 × 1,000 = 1 million relationships. At 100,000 tokens: 10 billion relationships. At 1 million tokens: 1 trillion. Each doubling of the window requires 4× the compute for the attention calculation.

This is why larger context windows cost more (you're paying for all those extra computations), why responses slow down as conversations get long, and why there's still a maximum — at some point, the memory and compute required exceeds what the hardware can handle in a reasonable time. The limit isn't arbitrary. It's physics — specifically, the cost of calculating attention over a very large sequence.

But there's a catch: just because a system can hold 1 million tokens doesn't mean it uses all of them equally well. Research shows that most models are strongest at the beginning and end of their context window — information buried in the middle can get less attention. (This is sometimes called "lost in the middle.") The window is big, but attention isn't perfectly uniform within it.

Why Your Conversations Have Limits

Now you can understand something that's probably happened to you: you're deep into a long conversation with an AI system, and it starts forgetting things you said earlier. Or it tells you the conversation is too long.

What's happening: everything in the conversation — every message you've sent and every response it gave — is being stored as tokens inside the context window. Each new exchange adds more tokens to the pile. Eventually, the pile hits the limit.

When that happens, the system has to make a choice:

  • Drop the oldest messages — the beginning of the conversation falls out of memory. The system literally can't see what you discussed earlier.
  • Summarise — compress the earlier conversation into fewer tokens, losing detail but preserving the gist.
  • Stop — tell you the conversation is too long and ask you to start a new one.

This is why long conversations can feel like the system is losing the thread. It isn't getting dumber — it's running out of room.

Practical example: If you paste a 50-page document into a conversation and then ask questions about it, the document is taking up tokens inside the context window. The larger the document, the fewer tokens are left for the conversation itself. Everything competes for space inside the same window.

Tokens and Cost: Why You're Charged by the Chunk

If you've ever looked at AI pricing, you've seen numbers like "$3 per million input tokens" or "$15 per million output tokens." Now you know what that means.

Every time you send a message, the system processes your input tokens. Every time it responds, it generates output tokens. Both cost compute — the hardware doing the maths has to process each token, one at a time. More tokens = more work = more cost.

Why output tokens cost more than input tokens:

Reading your message (input) is one pass — the system processes all your tokens at once. But generating a response (output) is sequential — it produces one token at a time, each one depending on the one before it. That sequential process is slower and more expensive per token.

Current pricing (mid-2026) for reference:

A note on the names below: Haiku, Sonnet, and Opus are Anthropic's models; GPT Mini and GPT-4o are OpenAI's; Flash is Google's. The specific names change frequently — what matters is the pattern: cheaper models exist for simple tasks, expensive ones for complex work.

Model tierInput cost (per million tokens)Output cost (per million tokens)
Budget models (Haiku, GPT Mini, Flash)$0.20 – $1$1 – $5
Mid-range (Sonnet, GPT-4o)$2 – $5$10 – $15
Frontier (Opus, GPT-5.6, Fable)$5 – $10$25 – $50

For most personal use on a subscription ($20/month), you don't think about tokens directly — the subscription absorbs the cost. But for businesses running AI at scale (thousands of queries per hour), token costs are a major line item. And for developers building AI products, choosing a cheaper model vs a better-but-expensive one is a constant trade-off.

💡 What this means for you as a user When a system responds slowly, part of the reason is: it's generating tokens one at a time. A long response takes longer not because it's "thinking harder" but because it's producing more tokens sequentially. When a system charges more for a longer response, now you know why. More output tokens = more compute = more cost. When someone says "use the cheaper model for simple tasks" — they mean: why pay $50 per million output tokens when $5 will do the same job for a straightforward question?

Beyond Text: How Images and Audio Become Tokens

So far, we've been talking about text. But modern AI systems also process images, audio, and video. How do those get tokenised?

Images → patches

An image doesn't have words to split into chunks. Instead, it gets divided into a grid of small squares — patches. Think of laying a grid of sticky notes over a photo — each square gets its own sticky note. Each patch (typically 16×16 pixels, or in some systems 512×512 pixel tiles) becomes one token — or more precisely, gets converted into a set of numbers that represent what's in that square.

The system can't "see" the whole image at once as a photograph. It sees a sequence of numbered patches, each one representing a small square of the image. The maths inside figures out how those patches relate to each other — just like it figures out how word-tokens relate to each other in text.

What this costs in practice:

When you upload a photo to ChatGPT or Claude, the system tiles your image into 512×512 pixel squares and processes each tile. A low-detail image costs about 85 tokens. A high-resolution image costs 85 + 170 tokens per tile — a large photo might use 765+ tokens. That's the equivalent of several paragraphs of text, eaten up by a single image.

This is why:

  • AI tools warn you that image uploads "use more tokens"
  • Sending many images in one conversation can exhaust your context window fast
  • Some tools offer a "low detail" option that costs fewer tokens (smaller grid, less information preserved)

Video → sampled frames → patches

Video is just images over time. But you can't tokenise every single frame — a 60-second clip at 30 frames per second is 1,800 images. That would be hundreds of thousands of tokens.

Instead, the system samples frames. It picks every Nth frame (say, every 15th) or selects "keyframes" — frames where something visually changes. Each selected frame gets tokenised the same way as a photo (grid of patches → tokens). The tokens are stacked in sequence: frame 1 patches, frame 2 patches, frame 3 patches...

This is why:

  • Video analysis has length limits (you can't upload a 2-hour movie)
  • The model might miss things that happen between sampled frames (a quick gesture, a flash of text)
  • Video costs far more tokens than a single image

Audio → two different approaches

Audio tokenisation depends on which system you're using, because there are two fundamentally different approaches:

Approach 1: Transcribe first (most common)

The audio hits a separate speech-to-text model (like Whisper) that converts your voice into text. That text then gets tokenised normally — subword chunks, just like anything you type. The language model never "hears" your voice. It reads a transcription.

Token cost: same as text (because by the time the language model sees it, it IS text).

This is how most voice assistants still work — Siri, Google Assistant, Alexa. Your voice → text → model → text → spoken back to you.

Approach 2: Native audio tokens (newer, what GPT-4o uses)

The audio itself is chopped into tiny time slices and encoded directly as tokens — without being converted to text first. The language model processes these audio tokens alongside text tokens in the same sequence. It actually "hears" your tone, pace, hesitation, emphasis.

Token cost: 1 token per 100ms of input audio (so 10 seconds of speech ≈ 100 audio tokens). Output audio costs more: 1 token per 50ms. That's more than text but far less than video.

This is why GPT-4o's voice mode felt so different from Siri — the model is processing your actual audio, not a transcription. It can hear you laugh, hear you hesitate, hear your emphasis. And it can generate audio with appropriate tone, not just read text aloud in a flat voice.

The unifying principle

Whether the input is text, an image, audio, or video — the system always does the same thing first: chop it into a sequence of tokens.

InputHow it becomes tokensApproximate cost
Text you typeSplit into subword chunks (BPE)~1 token per word
Photo you uploadTiled into patches → each patch encoded~85 to 765+ tokens per image
VideoSampled frames → each frame tiled into patchesThousands of tokens per minute
Voice (transcribed)Converted to text first → normal text tokensSame as text
Voice (native audio)Chopped into time slices → encoded directly~10 tokens per second

Then everything downstream (the learning, the generating, the reasoning) works on that sequence of tokens. The architecture doesn't care what the tokens originally were — it just processes sequences of numbers.

⏸️ Wait — so when I upload a photo and ask a question, the image tokens and my text tokens just sit side by side? Yes. They all go into the same context window, in a single queue. The model processes them together — your text tokens and the image patch tokens are all just numbers in a sequence. The model learned during training how image tokens and text tokens relate to each other (because it was trained on millions of image-caption pairs). To the model, it's all one stream. This is also why uploading a photo "uses up" conversation space. Those image tokens take seats in the context window just like words do — and a single photo takes up as many seats as several paragraphs of text.

Chapter Summary

What you learnedThe key idea
TokensThe fundamental unit of everything in AI — a chunk of text (or a patch of image, or a slice of audio) that the system processes as one piece.
Tokenisation (BPE)The process of building a vocabulary by merging common character pairs. Common words = one token. Rare words = split into pieces.
Context windowThe system's working memory — how many tokens it can "see" at once. Currently up to 1M–10M tokens for frontier models.
Why conversations have limitsThe entire conversation history lives inside the context window. When it fills up, old messages get dropped.
Cost = tokensYou're charged per token processed. Output tokens cost more than input because they're generated sequentially.
Images and audioSame principle — chopped into patches (images) or frames (audio) before processing. Everything becomes a sequence of tokens.

Try This

Grab a paragraph of text — anything. Count the words. Now estimate the tokens: multiply the word count by 1.3. That's roughly how many tokens the system would process.

Now think about a long conversation you've had with an AI system (ChatGPT, Claude, whoever). If that conversation lasted 50 back-and-forth exchanges, with each message averaging 100 words (130 tokens) — that's roughly 13,000 tokens of conversation history sitting in the context window. On older models (2023), that would already be hitting the limit. On current models, you'd barely be getting started.

A harder one: You want to upload a 200-page PDF to an AI system and ask questions about it. At roughly 600 tokens per page, that's 120,000 tokens just for the document. If your model has a 200,000-token context window, how much room is left for the actual conversation? What would happen if you tried the same thing on a model with only 128,000 tokens?

What's Next

You now know how data gets into the system: collected (Chapter 3), then chopped into tokens (this chapter). The system has its atoms — its fundamental units to work with.

But having tokens isn't knowing anything. The system still needs to learn from those tokens — to find patterns, to understand that "playing" and "played" are related, that "bank" means different things in different sentences, that the order of words changes the meaning entirely.

That's Chapter 5: Training — how a system goes from "here's a sequence of tokens" to "I understand what these mean and can produce more of them." The maths that turns tokens into knowledge.

📖 Key Terms — Quick Reference Token — A chunk the system processes as one unit. In English text, roughly ¾ of a word. The system's atom. Tokenisation — Chopping input into tokens. Text → subword chunks. Images → patches. Audio → frames. Byte Pair Encoding (BPE) — The most common method for building a token vocabulary. Starts from individual characters, repeatedly merges the most common adjacent pairs until the vocabulary reaches its target size. Vocabulary — The full set of tokens a system knows (typically 50,000–100,000 for text models). Context window — The maximum tokens the system can hold at once. Its working memory for one conversation. Everything — your messages, its responses, any documents, system instructions — must fit inside. Input tokens — What you send. Processed in one pass. Output tokens — What the system generates. Produced sequentially, one at a time. More expensive because of this. Lost in the middle — The finding that models pay less attention to information in the middle of a long context window than to information at the beginning or end.

Chapter 5: Training — How a Model Gets Built

Part II · How a Machine Learns

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

  • Explain what happens during training (the guess-check-adjust loop)
  • Describe what a neural network actually is — in plain terms, not metaphors
  • Understand what "weights" and "parameters" are and why model size is measured in billions of them
  • Explain why the transformer architecture (2017) made everything after it possible
  • Understand what "attention" does and why it matters
  • Describe what a trained model actually IS when it's finished (a file of numbers)

Key Terms Introduced in This Chapter

TermPlain English
ModelThe finished product of training — architecture + learned weights. The thing that takes input and produces output.
ArchitectureThe design of the network — how layers are arranged, how many, what type. The blueprint.
Neural networkLayers of simple maths operations that transform input into output — each layer adjusts the signal slightly
Weights (parameters)The numbers inside the network that get adjusted during training — what the model "knows" lives in these numbers
Training loopThe repeating cycle: guess → measure how wrong → adjust → guess again. Millions of times.
Loss functionThe measurement of "how wrong was that guess?" — the score the system is trying to minimise
Gradient descentThe method for adjusting weights in the right direction — like rolling downhill toward the lowest error
BackpropagationThe technique that figures out which specific weights caused the error, so they can be fixed
TransformerThe architecture (2017) that made modern AI possible — processes all tokens in parallel and uses attention
AttentionThe mechanism that lets the system figure out which parts of the input are relevant to which other parts
Pre-trainingThe big, expensive first pass where the model learns general patterns from massive data
Scaling lawsThe finding that bigger models + more data + more compute = predictably better performance
Knowledge cutoffThe date at which training data was locked — the model doesn't know anything that happened after this point

First: What IS a Model?

We've been saying "the system" since Chapter 1. The spam filter learned. The face grouper recognised. The chatbot generated. But what is the thing that does the learning and the recognising and the generating?

It's called a model.

A model is the finished product of training. It's the thing that took in all that data, did all that learning, and came out the other side as something that can make predictions or generate output. When you hear "ChatGPT" or "Claude" or "Gemini" — those are products built on top of specific models.

What it is, physically: Imagine a recipe with billions of ingredients. Each ingredient has a specific amount — a pinch of this, a cup of that. Before training, all the amounts are random garbage. The dish tastes terrible. During training, the system tweaks the amount of every single ingredient, a tiny bit at a time, testing the result each time. After billions of adjustments, the amounts are dialled in and the dish tastes right.

A trained AI model is like that finished recipe: a long list of specific amounts (called weights) that, together, produce good output. No single amount does anything meaningful on its own. It's the combination of all of them — billions working together — that makes the system work. You can't point to one weight and say "that's where it learned grammar." The knowledge is spread across all of them.

Because that's literally what it is: a model of the patterns in its training data. Not a copy of the data — a compressed, generalised representation of the patterns within it. The way a globe is a model of the Earth — it captures the structure without containing every tree and building.

A trained model doesn't store articles or remember specific web pages. It stores patterns — statistical relationships between tokens — learned from billions of examples. When it produces a correct answer, it's because it learned a pattern during training that, applied to your input, generates the right output.

💡 Architecture vs Model vs Weights — three words you'll keep hearing Sticking with the recipe analogy: Architecture = the recipe structure. Which ingredients are included, what order the steps go in, how the whole thing is organised. Two different companies might design completely different recipe structures — and then train (adjust the amounts) independently. The architecture is designed by humans before training begins. Weights = the specific amounts. After training, these are the numbers that make the system produce good output. If you copied all the amounts onto another computer, you'd have an exact copy of the model — it would produce the same results. Model = both together. The recipe structure filled in with trained amounts. When you "use a model," you're running your input through that structure with those specific amounts. When someone says "we fine-tuned the model" — they mean: we took amounts someone else already trained and adjusted them further for our specific purpose. When someone says "we designed a new architecture" — they mean: we changed the structure itself (different ingredients, different steps) and trained from scratch.

The Core Idea: Guess, Check, Adjust

📼 Rewind it: If you remember recording an answering-machine greeting, listening back, hating one word, and doing another take, the shape is familiar: attempt, compare, adjust, repeat. Training does this with maths and enormous amounts of data — not taste, embarrassment, or self-awareness.

You now know where training data comes from (Chapter 3) and how it gets chopped into tokens (Chapter 4). The system has its raw material. Now: how does it actually learn?

The entire process in one sentence: The system makes a guess, checks how wrong it was, adjusts itself slightly in the right direction, and tries again. Millions of times.

That's it. Every AI system you've ever used — from the spam filter to ChatGPT — was built by this loop. The details vary, but the principle never does.

Let's make it concrete. Imagine training a language model — the kind of system you chat with. During training, the system is given a sequence of tokens and asked to predict the next one. Over and over. Billions of times.

One round of the training loop:

  1. Input: The system sees the tokens: The cat sat on the
  2. Guess: It predicts the next token. Maybe it guesses chair.
  3. Check: The actual next token in the training data was mat. The system was wrong. How wrong? A loss function calculates the distance between its guess and the right answer.
  4. Adjust: The system adjusts its internal numbers slightly — nudging them in a direction that would have made mat more likely and chair less likely.
  5. Repeat: Next example. Next guess. Next adjustment.

After billions of these rounds — billions of guesses, billions of tiny adjustments — the system has tuned its internal numbers to be very good at predicting what comes next in text. Not because anyone told it the rules of English. Because it saw so many examples that it absorbed the patterns: grammar, facts, style, reasoning, everything that shows up in the statistical structure of language. The unofficial training anthem is Oops!... I Did It Again — except every oops comes with a number showing how wrong the guess was.

Important: this is how language models train. Other types of models learn differently.

The guess-check-adjust loop is universal — every type of model uses it. But what it's guessing varies:

  • Language models (LLMs): Predict the next token in a sequence of text. That's the task described above.
  • Image classification models: Look at an image and predict which category it belongs to ("cat," "dog," "stop sign"). The training task is: given this image, guess the correct label.
  • Text-to-image models (like Midjourney, DALL-E): You type "a 1990s library with neon lighting" and the system produces an image. These are trained on millions of image-caption pairs — the system learned what things look like by seeing countless examples of images paired with descriptions of what's in them. During generation, it starts from visual noise (like TV static) and gradually shapes it into an image that matches your description. (How this actually works mechanically — the full process — is Chapter 6.)
  • Recommendation systems: Predict what you'll engage with next (which song you'll play, which video you'll watch) based on patterns in your behaviour and millions of other users' behaviour.
  • Speech recognition: Predict which words correspond to a given audio signal.
  • Multimodal models: Can handle multiple types of input and output. You can show them a photo AND ask a question about it in text, and they'll respond in text. Or you can ask for an image and they'll generate one. These are trained on mixtures of text, images, audio, and video — all at once. Most frontier models in 2026 (ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini) are multimodal: they can read images, generate images, process audio, and handle text all in one system.

The core loop is always the same (guess → measure error → adjust → repeat), but the specific task — the thing being predicted — changes depending on what the model is built to do. In this chapter, we'll keep using language models as our main example because they're what most people interact with, but the principles apply across all types.

⏸️ Wait — the language model just predicts the next word? That's it? For the text part, yes. Every capability you see in a language model — writing essays, answering questions, writing code, reasoning about problems — emerges from training it to predict what token comes next. The richness comes from doing that one thing at extraordinary scale, across extraordinary amounts of data. But remember: language models aren't the only type. Image generators work differently (shaping noise into pictures). Recommendation systems work differently (predicting what you'll click). And multimodal models combine several of these abilities into one system. "Predict the next word" is the trick behind text generation specifically — not all of AI.

Neural Networks: What's Actually Inside

The system that makes those guesses isn't magic. It's a specific structure called a neural network. What it actually is — no hand-waving.

But first — the name. Why "neural"? Why is it named after the brain?

The brain connection (and why it's misleading)

In the 1940s and 50s, researchers looked at how biological brains work — neurons connected to other neurons, each one receiving signals, processing them, and passing signals forward to the next set of neurons — and thought: "Could we build something similar in software?"

They built simplified mathematical versions of neurons. A software "neuron" does this:

  1. Receives numbers from several inputs (from the neurons in the previous layer)
  2. Multiplies each input by a weight (how important is this connection?)
  3. Adds them all up
  4. Decides whether to "fire" — passes the result forward if it's above a certain threshold, stays quiet if it isn't

That's a single artificial neuron. It's literally: multiply inputs by weights, add them up, decide whether to activate. Each one is simple maths — addition and multiplication.

Stack thousands of these in a layer. Stack dozens of layers in a chain. That's a neural network.

Here's the important part: the name is where the similarity ends.

Biological brainArtificial neural network
What a "neuron" doesComplex electrochemical signalling, grows new connections, changes physically over timeMultiplies numbers, adds them up, passes forward. That's it.
How it learnsUnknown — we still don't fully understand how biological memory and learning workBackpropagation: calculate error, trace it backwards, adjust numbers. Completely understood.
Number of neurons~86 billion, connected in 3D with trillions of synapsesMillions to billions of parameters (not the same as neurons — one layer might have thousands of "neurons" with millions of connections between them)
Runs onElectrochemistry, biology, organic tissueMaths on silicon chips
ConsciousnessYes (presumably)No. Not even close.
How connections formGrows organically based on experience, environment, emotion, physiologySet randomly, then adjusted by gradient descent on a loss function

The original inspiration was biological, but modern neural networks have long since diverged from anything resembling actual neuroscience. Calling them "neural networks" is a historical artifact — like how we still say "dial" a phone number or "roll down" a car window. The name stuck. The connection to brains didn't.

⏸️ "So AI isn't modelled on the brain?" Not anymore, no. The original idea in the 1950s was loosely inspired by brain structure. But modern deep learning has gone in its own direction based on what works mathematically, not what's biologically accurate. Neuroscientists and AI researchers work in almost completely separate fields now. When someone says "AI works like the brain" — that's marketing, not science. A better analogy: an artificial neural network is to a brain what a submarine is to a fish. Both move through water. Early submarine designers studied how fish move. But a submarine uses propellers, metal, and engines — completely different mechanisms from fins and muscles. It's not trying to be a fish. It's a different engineering solution to the same problem. Nobody asks "can a submarine swim?" — that's the wrong question. You ask "does it move through water effectively?" Same with AI: "does it think like a brain?" is the wrong question. "Does it produce useful outputs?" is the right one.

What a neural network actually looks like (the chain-of-people model)

Think of it like a chain of people passing a message. You whisper something to the first person. They change it slightly — emphasise one part, soften another — and pass it to the next person. That person adjusts it again, passes it on. By the time it reaches the last person in the chain, the message has been transformed through dozens of small adjustments into something new: a prediction.

Each person in the chain is a layer. Each layer takes what it received, adjusts it based on its own settings (its share of the recipe amounts — the weights), and passes the result forward. One layer does almost nothing interesting on its own. But stack dozens or hundreds together, and the chain transforms a sequence of input tokens into something meaningful — a prediction, a classification, a generated response.

What different layers do: Early layers in the chain tend to pick up on simple patterns (common word pairings, basic grammar). Middle layers detect more complex patterns (sentence structure, who's doing what to whom). Later layers pull everything together into the final prediction. No single layer "understands" the input — but together, the chain of transformations produces something that looks a lot like understanding.

What "deep" means: A "deep" neural network just means one with many layers. The "deep" in "deep learning" isn't a marketing term — it's literally describing the depth (number of layers) of the network. GPT-style models have dozens to over a hundred layers.

Weights and Parameters: What the Model "Knows"

Before training: All those numbers are essentially random. The network knows nothing. It produces garbage.

During training: The guess-check-adjust loop tunes each number slightly, billions of times, until the network produces good predictions.

After training: Those numbers are frozen. They don't change anymore. The trained model is, physically, just a very large file of numbers — the learned weights. When you ask ChatGPT a question, you're running your tokens through that file of numbers. The "knowledge" lives in the specific values of those billions of parameters.

How big are these files?

ModelParametersApproximate file size
A small model (phone-sized)3 billion~6 GB
GPT-3 (2020)175 billion~350 GB
GPT-4 (2023)~1.8 trillion (estimated)~3.6 TB
Largest open models (2026)2.8 trillion~5.6 TB
Current frontier (2026): GPT-5.6, Claude Opus 5, Gemini 3.5Undisclosed (estimated 2T+)Undisclosed

That's it. A trained model isn't a database of facts. It isn't storing articles or remembering conversations. It's a file of numbers — weights — that encode patterns extracted from training data. When the system produces a correct answer, it's because those patterns, applied to your input tokens, happen to generate the right output.

The Training Loop in Detail

Let's go deeper into each step of the loop, because this is where the actual learning happens.

Step 1: Forward pass (make a guess)

Your input tokens flow through the network, layer by layer, front to back — like passing through that chain of people from Section 5.3. Each layer adjusts the signal using its current weights. At the end, the network produces a set of likelihoods for every possible next token: "I'm 12% confident the next token is mat, 9% confident it's floor, 7% confident it's chair..."

Step 2: Loss function (how wrong was it?)

The loss function compares the network's guess to the correct answer. If the right answer was mat and the network only gave it 12% probability, the loss is high — the guess was poor. If it gave mat 95% probability, the loss is low — good guess.

The loss is just a number. Lower is better. The entire goal of training is to make this number as low as possible across all the training examples.

Step 3: Backpropagation (figure out what went wrong)

This is the clever part. The system needs to figure out: which specific weights in which specific layers caused the error? The network has billions of weights — you can't just randomly jiggle them and hope.

Backpropagation works backwards through the network (that's the "back" part). Think of it like tracing a bad dish back to the kitchen: the dish was too salty. OK — which step added the salt? And how much of the saltiness came from step 4 vs step 7? The system traces the error backwards through all the layers, figuring out how much each weight (each ingredient amount) contributed to the mistake. Every single weight gets a "blame score" — how responsible it was for the error.

Step 4: Gradient descent (adjust in the right direction)

Now the system knows which weights need to change, and in which direction. Gradient descent makes the actual adjustment — nudging each weight by a tiny amount in the direction that would reduce the loss.

"Gradient" means "slope" — it's figuring out which way is "downhill" (toward lower error) and taking a small step that way. Do this enough times and you end up at a low point — a set of weights that produces good predictions.

Step 5: Repeat

One training example adjusts the weights by a tiny amount. Then the next example. Then the next. Across billions of examples, those tiny adjustments accumulate into a system that has absorbed the patterns of language (or images, or audio — whatever it was trained on).

💡 Why "millions of times"? Because each individual adjustment is tiny. If you adjusted too much based on one example, the system would overfit to that one example and forget everything else. The power comes from doing it billions of times — each example pulling the weights in slightly different directions, until they settle into a position that works well across all the examples. It's not one insight that makes the system smart. It's the accumulation of billions of micro-corrections.

The Transformer: The Architecture That Made This Generation Possible

Everything described so far — the training loop, the layers, the weights — existed for decades before 2017. Neural networks have been around since the 1950s. So why did AI suddenly get so good?

The answer is a specific design for a neural network, published in June 2017 in a paper called "Attention Is All You Need" by eight researchers at Google. They called it the Transformer.

The problem it solved:

Before transformers, the main architecture for processing language was called a "recurrent neural network" (RNN). RNNs processed tokens one at a time, in order — like reading a sentence word by word, left to right. This created two problems:

  1. It was slow. You couldn't process tokens in parallel. A 500-token sentence required 500 sequential steps — no matter how many GPUs you had.
  2. It forgot things. By the time the network reached the end of a long sentence, information from the beginning had degraded. Long-range connections were hard.

What the transformer did differently:

The transformer processes all tokens at once. In parallel. Every token can "look at" every other token in a single step, regardless of distance. A word at the beginning of a paragraph is exactly one step away from a word at the end.

This made training dramatically faster (you could use all your hardware at once), and it made the system dramatically better at understanding relationships across long text.

The result: Within a few years of the transformer paper, every major language model adopted this architecture. GPT, Claude, Gemini — all transformers. It's the shared design underneath the current generation of AI.

🏆 Landmark Moment: "Attention Is All You Need" (June 2017) Eight researchers at Google published a 15-page paper proposing a new architecture they called the Transformer. Most of the AI community was sceptical — it threw out established approaches that had been standard for years. The paper's contribution was showing that you could build a powerful language system using only an attention mechanism, without the sequential processing everyone assumed was necessary. By 2026, it's among the most cited machine learning papers of the 21st century (over 250,000 citations). Every AI system you interact with today — every chatbot, every image generator, every code assistant — descends from this paper.

Attention: "What Should I Focus On?"

The transformer's key innovation is the attention mechanism. What it does:

When processing a sentence, the system needs to figure out which words are relevant to which other words. Take:

*"The animal didn't cross the street because **it** was too tired."*

What does "it" refer to? The animal or the street? You know immediately — "it" refers to the animal (animals get tired, streets don't). But how does a machine figure this out?

Attention lets each token "ask" every other token: "how relevant are you to me?" The system calculates a relevance score between every pair of tokens. "It" gives a high attention score to "animal" and a low attention score to "street" — because the patterns in the training data showed that tired things are usually living things.

This happens at every layer, for every token, simultaneously. The result: the system builds a rich understanding of which parts of the input relate to which other parts — grammar, meaning, logic, reference — all encoded in the attention patterns.

Why this matters practically:

Attention is why these systems can:

  • Follow a pronoun back to what it refers to, even paragraphs earlier
  • Understand that "bank" means something different in "river bank" vs "bank account"
  • Maintain coherence across long passages of text
  • Follow complex instructions where the order and relationships between parts matter

What's honestly not fully understood: We know what attention does (computes relevance between all pairs of tokens) and we know it works spectacularly well. What's less clear is why this specific mechanism produces such powerful general capabilities. The original 2017 paper ("Attention Is All You Need") showed it worked — and since then it's been the foundation of nearly every breakthrough. But researchers are still studying why attention heads self-organise into interpretable patterns (some specialise in grammar, some in logic, some in factual recall) without being designed to. The mechanism is simple — the emergent behaviour it produces is not, and we don't fully have a theory for why this particular mathematical operation unlocks general language understanding. It just does, and that's part of what makes this field both powerful and unpredictable.

Pre-Training: The Big Expensive First Pass

Training a large model happens in phases. The first — and by far most expensive — is pre-training.

During pre-training, the model is shown an enormous amount of data (trillions of tokens — books, web pages, code, conversations) and trained on the basic task of next-token prediction. This phase gives the model its general knowledge and capabilities.

How expensive is pre-training?

  • GPT-3 (2020): Estimated $4.6 million in compute (with some estimates up to $12 million including all costs).
  • GPT-4 (2023): Estimated $78–100+ million (OpenAI's CEO confirmed "more than $100 million").
  • Current frontier models (2025–26, e.g. GPT-5.x, Claude Opus 5, Gemini 3.5): Estimated $200 million–$1 billion+. Big Tech companies are collectively spending over $300 billion annually on AI-related capital expenditures as of 2025 (IDC).

This is why only a handful of companies can build frontier models from scratch. The hardware cost alone — thousands of specialised chips running for months — is a barrier that most organisations can't clear.

Pre-training produces a general-purpose model. It knows a lot, but it isn't specifically good at any one task yet. Making it useful for specific applications (following instructions, being helpful, refusing harmful requests) requires a second phase — fine-tuning — which we'll cover in Chapter 9.

Scaling Laws: Why Bigger Kept Being Better

In 2020, researchers at OpenAI published a striking finding: if you make a language model bigger (more parameters), train it on more data, and give it more compute — its performance improves in a smooth, predictable way. Not in jumps. Not randomly. Along a clean curve.

These scaling laws became the roadmap for the entire industry. They said: if you want a better model, the recipe is straightforward — make it bigger, feed it more data, spend more on compute. And it will be predictably this-much better.

The progression:

YearModelParameters
2018GPT-1117 million
2019GPT-21.5 billion
2020GPT-3175 billion
2023GPT-4~1.8 trillion (estimated; unconfirmed)
2025–26GPT-5.x, Claude Opus 5, Gemini 3.5Undisclosed (companies stopped publishing exact counts)

From GPT-1 to GPT-4: roughly 10x more parameters per generation, meaningfully better each time. After GPT-4, the major labs stopped disclosing exact parameter counts — but the models kept getting better, using a mix of scale, better data, and new training techniques.

But is it hitting a wall?

In late 2024, reports suggested that scaling was reaching diminishing returns — that making models bigger wasn't producing the same gains anymore. By 2026, the picture is more nuanced: raw scaling still works, but the industry has found other routes to improvement too (better training data, reasoning techniques, more efficient architectures). The pure "just make it bigger" era may be giving way to a "make it smarter about how it thinks" era. Both approaches continue in parallel.

The Chinchilla insight (2022): DeepMind showed that many large models were under-trained — they had too many parameters for the amount of data they'd seen. Think of it like a textbook with too few practice problems for the difficulty level — the student (model) has capacity to learn more but wasn't given enough examples. A smaller model trained on much more data could outperform a larger under-trained one at the same compute cost. This shifted the industry toward training models on more tokens, not just making them bigger.

🏆 Landmark Moment: GPT-3 and "Few-Shot Learning" (May 2020) When OpenAI released GPT-3, they showed something unexpected. The model could perform tasks nobody had explicitly trained it for — just by seeing a few examples written in the prompt. Show it three examples of English-to-French translation inside your message, and it would translate the fourth. Show it three question-answer pairs, and it would answer the fourth in the same style. The paper was titled "Language Models are Few-Shot Learners." It proved that scale alone could produce emergent abilities — capabilities that appear without being directly taught. This was the birth of "prompting" as a technique: you could program the model by writing examples in plain English, no code required.

What's honestly uncertain here: Scaling laws have held remarkably well from 2018 to 2026 — every time researchers made models bigger with more data, capabilities improved predictably. But nobody has a complete theory for why this works, or for how long it will continue. Is there a ceiling? Will transformers plateau at some capability level and need a fundamentally different architecture to go further? Some researchers (notably Yann LeCun at Meta) believe we've nearly exhausted what this approach can do. Others point to continued improvements and say the ceiling hasn't appeared yet. Both camps have evidence. As of August 2026, scaling is still working — but whether it will still be working in 2028 is a genuinely open question, not a settled one.

What a "Trained Model" Actually IS

Let's end on something concrete. After all the training — the billions of examples, the trillions of token predictions, the months of compute — what do you actually have?

A file of numbers.

That's it. The trained model is a very large file (or collection of files) containing the final values of all the weights. Those weights encode everything the model "knows" — every pattern of language, every fact it absorbed, every stylistic tendency, every reasoning shortcut.

When you use the model (ask it a question, generate text, upload an image), your input tokens flow through those frozen weights, layer by layer, and produce output tokens. The weights don't change — they were frozen at the end of training. What you're seeing is the fixed patterns, applied to your specific input.

The knowledge cutoff: why it doesn't know about yesterday

Because the weights are frozen at the end of training, the model only "knows" things that were in its training data. And training data has a cutoff date — the point at which the data was collected and locked. Anything that happened after that date simply doesn't exist to the model.

If a major event happened last week — a new law passed, a company launched a product, a celebrity died — the model has no way to know about it. The patterns in its weights reflect the world as it was when training data was collected, not the world as it is right now.

This is why you'll sometimes get confidently wrong answers about recent events. The model isn't lying — it's applying patterns from its training data, which doesn't include what happened after the cutoff. It has no way to distinguish "I know this" from "I'm generating a plausible-sounding pattern based on old information."

How this gets bridged: Modern AI systems (ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini) add tools on top of the base model — web search, live data access, browsing. When you ask about something current, the system can look it up in real time and include that information in its context window alongside your question. The model itself didn't learn anything new — but it was given current information to work with. The difference between knowledge (frozen in the weights) and information (provided live in the context) is important. We'll come back to this distinction in the chapters on inference and context engineering.

Why it doesn't know what time it is

A related limitation: the model has no internal clock. It's a mathematical function — tokens in, tokens out. It has no sense of "now." It doesn't know today's date, what time it is, or how long ago something happened unless it's explicitly told in its input.

Modern systems solve this by injecting the current date and time into the system instructions (the hidden instructions that get loaded before your message). So when you ask "what day is it?" and get a correct answer, that's not the model "knowing" — it's the model reading a date that was placed into its context window by the application layer. If that injection didn't happen, the model would have no idea.

This is the same principle: the model only works with what's in its context window. It doesn't sense the outside world. It doesn't have eyes, ears, or a clock. Everything it "knows" in the moment either comes from its frozen training weights or from information injected into its context window right before it responds.

The model doesn't "think" in the way you do. It doesn't have beliefs or knowledge in a drawer it can open. It has patterns encoded as numbers, and when those patterns are applied to your input, they produce output that looks like understanding. Whether it "is" understanding is a philosophical question. What it is, mechanically, is a very large function: tokens in, tokens out, with billions of learned parameters shaping the transformation.

Chapter Summary

What you learnedThe key idea
The training loopGuess → measure error → adjust weights → repeat. Billions of times.
Neural networksLayers of maths. Each layer transforms the signal. Stack enough layers and it can learn complex patterns.
Weights/parametersThe numbers that get adjusted during training. What the model "knows" lives here.
Loss function"How wrong was that guess?" — the score training tries to minimise.
BackpropagationTraces error backwards through layers to figure out which weights need fixing.
Gradient descentAdjusts weights in the direction that reduces error. Tiny steps, billions of times.
Transformers (2017)Process all tokens in parallel. Every token can attend to every other token. Made everything after possible.
Attention"Which parts of this input are relevant to which other parts?" — the mechanism that captures relationships.
Pre-trainingThe big expensive first pass. Trillions of tokens. Costs $50M–$1B+ for frontier models.
Scaling lawsBigger + more data + more compute = predictably better. But the industry is now finding other routes too.
A trained modelA file of numbers (frozen weights). Patterns encoded as parameters. Tokens in, tokens out.

Try This

Think about the training loop. The system sees "The cat sat on the ___" and guesses chair. The answer was mat. It adjusts.

Now imagine this has happened a trillion times, across all of Wikipedia, all of Reddit, all of published books, all of StackOverflow. After a trillion tiny adjustments:

  • It's absorbed the patterns of English grammar.
  • It's absorbed facts about the world (because facts appear in consistent patterns across text).
  • It's absorbed reasoning patterns (because logical arguments follow predictable token sequences).
  • It's absorbed style, tone, formality (because those are patterns too).

All from predicting the next token. That's the magic — and it's also the limitation. If a pattern doesn't appear in the training data, the system has no way to learn it.

A harder question: If the model is just a file of frozen numbers, and it doesn't learn from your conversation — why does it seem to "remember" what you said earlier in the chat? (Hint: think back to Chapter 4's context window. The answer isn't learning — it's memory.)

What's Next

You now know how a model gets built: data → tokens → training loop → frozen weights. The system has been trained. It's a file of numbers that can predict the next token with remarkable accuracy.

But we've been talking mostly about text — predicting the next word. What about when you upload a photo? What about when you type a description and get an image back? What about when you talk to it?

Chapter 6 takes you through exactly how AI handles each type of input and output — how it "sees" images, how it generates pictures from text (and why it's called "diffusion"), how it hears your voice, and why modern multimodal models feel so different from the stitched-together systems that came before.

📖 Key Terms — Quick Reference Model — The finished product of training: architecture + learned weights. What you interact with when you use GPT-5.6, Claude Opus 5, or Gemini 3.5. Neural network — Layers of mathematical operations that transform input to output. Each layer applies weights, adds bias, and passes the result forward. Weights / Parameters — The numbers inside the network. Adjusted during training. Frozen after. A model's "knowledge" lives in these values. Frontier models have trillions of them. Training loop — Predict → measure error (loss) → trace error back through layers (backpropagation) → adjust weights in the right direction (gradient descent) → repeat. Loss function — Measures how wrong a guess was. Training minimises this number across billions of examples. Gradient descent — Adjusts weights by taking small steps "downhill" toward lower error. Backpropagation — Traces error backwards through the network to calculate which weights need the most adjustment. Transformer — The architecture (2017) that processes all tokens in parallel using attention. Powers every major AI system today. Paper: "Attention Is All You Need." Attention — The mechanism that calculates relevance between every pair of tokens. "Which words matter to which other words?" — what lets the system understand context, reference, and meaning. Pre-training — The massive, expensive first phase of training where the model learns general patterns from trillions of tokens. Costs $200M–$1B+ for frontier models as of 2026. Scaling laws — The empirical finding that model performance improves predictably with more parameters, more data, and more compute. Chinchilla scaling (2022) — DeepMind's finding that many models were under-trained. Smaller models trained on more data can match larger under-trained ones. Shifted industry toward data efficiency.

Chapter 6: How AI Sees, Hears, and Creates

Part III · Using the Thing

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

  • Explain what actually happens when you upload a photo to an AI and ask "what's in this?"
  • Describe how text-to-image generation works (diffusion), step by step
  • Explain the difference between generating a new image, editing an existing one, and inpainting
  • Describe the three different voice pipelines (and why some feel so much better than others)
  • Understand how video generation and analysis work
  • Explain why native multimodal models feel different from "three models stitched together"

Key Terms Introduced in This Chapter

TermPlain meaning
Vision encoderA neural network that converts image patches into numbers a language model can read
Diffusion modelA model that generates images by starting from noise and gradually removing it
Forward diffusionThe training process of adding noise to an image until it's pure static
Reverse diffusion / denoisingThe generation process of starting from noise and sculpting it into an image
Cross-attentionThe mechanism that connects your text prompt to the image being generated — how words steer pixels
InpaintingEditing only a specific region of an image while leaving the rest untouched
img2img (image-to-image)Generating a new image using an existing image as the starting point instead of pure noise
ASR (automatic speech recognition)Converting spoken audio into text
TTS (text-to-speech)Converting text into spoken audio
Native audioProcessing voice directly as audio tokens, without converting to text first
MultimodalA model that handles multiple types of input/output (text, images, audio, video) in one system

The Central Question

You've learned how text gets tokenised (Chapter 4) and how models get trained (Chapter 5). But you're probably wondering:

OK, but how does it actually SEE a photo? How does typing words produce a picture? How does it hear my voice? How does it know to change the hair but leave the face alone?

These aren't one thing. They're several different processes, and they work in genuinely different ways. This chapter walks through each one mechanically — not just what they do, but how they do it, step by step.

How the Model "Sees" a Photo (Vision)

When you upload a photo to ChatGPT or Claude and ask "what's in this picture?", this is what actually happens:

Step 1: Chop the image into patches

Your photo gets divided into a grid of squares — typically 16×16 or 14×14 pixels each. (You learned this in Chapter 4.) A standard image might become 200-700+ patches depending on resolution.

Step 2: Run each patch through a vision encoder

Each patch goes through a separate neural network called a vision encoder — a model trained specifically to turn pixel data into a set of numbers that mean something. The vision encoder was trained on millions of images with captions, so it learned to convert visual information into the same kind of numerical representation the language model uses.

Think of it like a translator. You speak English, your colleague speaks Japanese. You don't learn Japanese — you just have a translator sitting between you who converts everything into a language you both understand. The vision encoder is the translator between the visual world and the number world the language model operates in.

Step 3: Image tokens enter the queue alongside text tokens

The output of the vision encoder — hundreds of image tokens — gets placed into the context window alongside your text tokens. They all sit in one sequence:

[image token 1] [image token 2] ... [image token 576] [What] [is] [in] [this] [picture] [?]

The language model processes this entire sequence together. It learned during training how image tokens and text tokens relate to each other (because it saw millions of images paired with descriptions). When it generates a response, it's attending to both the image tokens and your text question simultaneously.

What the model actually "sees"

What might be hard to accept: the model doesn't see your photo the way you do. It doesn't experience a picture. It reads a long sequence of numbers that statistically represent visual features — edges, textures, colours, shapes, spatial relationships. It's pattern-matching across those numbers, the same way it pattern-matches across text tokens.

But the results can be surprisingly good because the training data was enormous. It saw so many images described in so many ways that it learned rich associations between visual patterns and language.

🔍 Concept in Practice: Why AI sometimes gets image details wrong

When you ask "how many people are in this photo?" and the model says 4 when there are 5, it's not because it "miscounted." It doesn't count. It doesn't look at individual people and tally them. It processes patch tokens through statistical patterns, and those patterns are better at identifying what's in an image than precisely how many of something there are. Counting requires exact spatial reasoning across patches — which is harder than recognising objects. This is also why models sometimes hallucinate objects that aren't there, or miss small details in a corner of the image — some patches carry less informational weight than others.

How Text Becomes an Image (Diffusion)

This is the one that feels like magic. You type "a 1990s library with neon lighting" and an image appears. Where did it come from?

The name tells you the method

They're called diffusion models because the process is inspired by physical diffusion — like a drop of ink spreading through water. The ink starts as a concentrated droplet (structure, information, a clear image) and gradually spreads until the water is uniformly coloured (randomness, noise, no structure left). That spreading is diffusion.

The model learns to reverse that process.

How it's trained (the forward process)

Training is simple in concept:

  1. Take a clear image from the training dataset
  2. Add a tiny amount of random noise to it
  3. Add a little more noise
  4. Keep adding noise, step by step, until the image is completely unrecognisable — pure static, like TV snow

Do this to millions of images. Now you have millions of sequences showing the journey from "clear picture" to "pure randomness," one step at a time.

The model's training task: given a noisy image at any step in this sequence, predict what it looked like one step less noisy. That's it. Over and over, for millions of examples, across hundreds of noise levels: "here's a noisy image — make it slightly less noisy."

⏸️ Wait — you start with nothing? How do you make something from nothing?

You don't start with nothing. You start with everything at once, randomly. TV static isn't empty — every pixel is a random colour. That's not the absence of information; it's the maximum disorder of information. Every possible image is equally "close" to random noise. A photo of a cat, a painting of a library, a picture of your face — they're all the same distance from pure static.

What the model does is subtract. It doesn't add things to a blank canvas. It removes randomness from a chaotic starting point. Each step, it looks at the noise and nudges it in a direction that makes it slightly less random and slightly more like something.

It's less like painting and more like:

  • Sculpting from marble — the shape is already "in there," you chip away what isn't the shape
  • Tuning a radio — all the signals are already in the air, you dial into one specific station
  • A Polaroid developing — the image emerges from what looks like nothing, but the information was there in the chemistry

How it generates (the reverse process)

When you type a prompt, this is what happens:

StepWhat it looks likeWhat the model is doing
0Pure random staticStarting point — maximum chaos
1-5Static starts to organiseBig compositional decisions: "light area here, dark area there"
5-15Vague colour blobs, rough shapes"That's a shelf shape, that's a desk shape, warm tones over there"
15-30Recognisable scene, fuzzy detailsStructure is clear, now refining textures and edges
30-50Final imageSharpening details, small objects, text, fine textures

Each step, the model looks at the current (noisy) state and asks: "What would this look like with slightly less noise, given that the person asked for 'a 1990s library with neon lighting'?"

This is why image generation sometimes produces weird intermediate stages if you watch it live — you see the image go from blurry blobs to sharp details. That's the denoising steps running in sequence.

How your words steer the pixels (cross-attention)

Here's the piece that connects text and image: cross-attention.

Your text prompt goes through a text encoder — a small, fast helper model that converts your words into numerical representations (it runs once, quickly, at the start). During each denoising step, the image generation model uses cross-attention to ask: "Which parts of the text description are relevant to this region of the image I'm currently working on?"

  • Working on the upper portion of the image? Pay attention to "neon lighting" (lights are usually above)
  • Working on the left side? Maybe that's where "bookshelves" go
  • Working on textures and colours? Pay attention to "1990s" (warm tones, wood, specific aesthetic)

The text doesn't control the image like a set of coordinates. It guides the denoising process — nudging noise-removal in directions that align with what you described. Early steps are guided more strongly by broad concepts ("library," "neon"). Later steps are guided by finer details.

This is why:

  • More specific prompts produce more specific results (more guidance available at each step)
  • Word order sometimes matters (models can weight earlier words differently)
  • AI still struggles with exact spatial relationships like "the red ball is to the LEFT of the blue ball" — cross-attention is better at guiding "what's in the image" than "exactly where things are placed"

🔍 Concept in Practice: Why AI-generated images sometimes have weird hands

Hands are spatially complex — five fingers in specific positions relative to each other, foreshortening, overlapping. The model denoises pixel regions based on statistical patterns from training data, and hands appeared in wildly different configurations across millions of images. The noise-removal process sometimes "averages" between multiple plausible finger positions, producing something that looks like six fingers or oddly bent joints. This has improved dramatically with newer models (larger training sets, better architecture), but it's a signature failure mode because it requires precise spatial coordination across multiple patches — which is harder for cross-attention to guide than broad concepts like "a person standing."

What's honestly not understood about diffusion:

The denoising process works. The images that come out are extraordinary. But researchers don't have a complete theory for why it works this well. The maths says: "given noise, predict the slightly-less-noisy version." That's simple. What's not simple: why does repeating this process 30–50 times, starting from random static, reliably produce images with correct perspective, consistent lighting, physically plausible shadows, and coherent spatial relationships that the model was never explicitly taught?

The model wasn't trained on geometry or optics. It was trained on millions of image-noise pairs. Yet it learned something that resembles an understanding of 3D space, light physics, and object permanence — purely from 2D pixel patterns. Why this emerges from the training process (rather than producing convincing but physically incoherent images) is an active area of research. It suggests the statistical patterns in millions of photographs implicitly contain information about the physical world that generated them — and the model somehow extracts that information during training. How deep that "understanding" goes, and where it breaks down, is not settled.

Editing an Image vs Creating One from Scratch

You've probably done this: uploaded a photo and said "change the background to a beach" or "give my cat a top hat." That's fundamentally different from generating an image from nothing. Here's how:

Image-to-image (img2img)

Instead of starting from pure noise, the system starts from your actual image — but adds some noise to it first. Not all the way to static. Just enough to "loosen" the pixels so they can be rearranged.

The amount of noise controls how much changes:

Noise addedWhat happensExample prompt
Very little (10-20%)Subtle tweaks — colours, lighting, style. Subject stays identical"Make this photo warmer"
Medium (30-50%)Structure partly loosens — new elements can appear while overall composition stays"Give this cat a bob haircut"
A lot (70-90%)Major transformation — your image is just a rough compositional guide"Turn this photo into a watercolour painting"

Think of it like the Rubik's cube:

  • Pure generation: Start with a fully scrambled cube, solve it into "library with neon lighting"
  • Image editing: Start with a mostly-solved cube (your photo), scramble it just a little, then re-solve the scrambled parts toward your new description

The system runs the same reverse-diffusion process (denoise step by step, guided by your text), but because it started from something close to a real image instead of pure noise, the result stays anchored to your original photo.

This is why when you edit a photo:

  • Low-strength edits feel like Instagram filters (just colour/lighting shifts)
  • Medium-strength edits add or change elements while keeping the overall scene
  • High-strength edits barely resemble the original (you've scrambled too much of it)

Inpainting (editing just one specific area)

What if you don't want to change the whole image — just one spot? "Remove the person standing on the left." "Change the sky to sunset." "Fix the weird hand."

Inpainting works by masking:

  1. You draw (or the system identifies) a mask — the specific region that should change
  2. Noise is added only to the masked region. Everything outside the mask stays untouched.
  3. The model runs reverse diffusion only within the masked region, guided by your text prompt, while keeping the unmasked area frozen
  4. The denoising process also "sees" the surrounding context (the frozen areas), so it generates something that blends seamlessly with the rest of the image

This is why inpainting is so powerful — it respects the context. If you mask a person's hair and say "blonde bob," the model generates a blonde bob that matches the lighting, angle, and style of the rest of the photo, because it can see those surrounding pixels as context.

🔍 Concept in Practice: "Remove object" tools in phone photo editors

When your phone's photo editor offers "erase this object" — you tap something and it vanishes, replaced by plausible background — that's inpainting. The system masks the object, then generates new pixels for that region based on the surrounding context. It's not "cutting and pasting" background. It's generating fresh pixels that are statistically plausible given what surrounds the hole. This is why it works so well on simple backgrounds (grass, sky) and sometimes produces artifacts on complex ones (repeating brick patterns, crowds of people).

Sketch-to-image and ControlNet

One more variation: what if you draw a rough sketch and ask the model to turn it into a polished image?

This uses guidance networks (the most well-known is called ControlNet) that take an additional input — a sketch, an edge map, a pose skeleton — and use it to constrain the diffusion process. The model generates an image that matches the spatial structure of your sketch while filling in realistic details, textures, and colours.

Think of it as giving the sculptor a wire armature before they start shaping the marble. The armature defines the pose and proportions. The sculptor fills in everything else.

How AI Handles Voice

You've probably noticed: talking to Siri in 2020 felt like talking to a machine. Talking to ChatGPT's voice mode in 2025 feels like talking to a person — it laughs, it pauses, it picks up on your tone. Same concept (speak to AI, get spoken answer back), completely different experience. Why?

Because they use fundamentally different pipelines — and which pipeline you're using determines everything about how natural, responsive, and expressive the interaction feels.

Pipeline 1: Transcribe → Think → Speak Back (the chain)

📼 Rewind it: This chain can feel like a late-'90s phone tree: one specialist takes the message, passes it on, and another delivers the reply. Each handoff can lose tone or detail before the next step begins. A native voice model shortens that chain; it does not become human or understand you the way a person does.

This is how most voice assistants worked historically, and many still work this way:

Your voice → [Speech-to-text model] → Text → [Language model] → Text response → [Text-to-speech model] → Audio you hear

Three separate models in sequence, each doing one job:

Step 1 — Speech-to-text (ASR): A model trained on thousands of hours of recorded speech paired with written transcripts. It hears your audio waveform and predicts which words you said. (Whisper by OpenAI is a well-known example — trained on 680,000 hours of multilingual audio.)

Step 2 — Language model: Reads the text transcription. Generates a text response. Has no idea you spoke it — just sees text. All your tone, hesitation, emphasis, accent — lost in transcription.

Step 3 — Text-to-speech (TTS): Takes the text response and generates audio that sounds like a human voice. Trained on recordings of voice actors reading scripts. The output has appropriate pacing and emphasis, but it was generated from text — it has no awareness of how YOU spoke.

The problem with this chain: Each handoff loses information. Your sarcastic tone becomes flat text. The model's witty response becomes generic speech synthesis. There's latency at every junction (transcribe... think... synthesize...). And the three models can't coordinate — the speech model doesn't know what the language model is about to say, so it can't prepare.

This is why Siri and Alexa often feel robotic and slow — it's three separate systems passing notes.

Pipeline 2: Native Audio (voice-to-voice, end-to-end)

This is what GPT-4o introduced in 2024 — one model that processes audio tokens directly:

Your voice → [Audio tokens directly into the model] → [Model generates audio tokens directly] → Audio you hear

One model, end-to-end. The audio itself is tokenised — sound waves chopped into tiny time slices (about 100ms each), encoded as tokens — and fed directly into the same model that handles text. The model was trained on audio alongside text, so it processes both natively.

What this means mechanically:

  • The model "hears" your tone, pace, emphasis, hesitation, laughter
  • It generates audio with appropriate intonation, emotion, pacing — not just reading text aloud
