  • It can be interrupted mid-sentence and respond naturally (real-time processing)
  • Latency is much lower (one model, not three in a chain)
  • It can express surprise, amusement, urgency in its voice because it's generating audio directly, not converting text to speech

This is why GPT-4o's voice mode felt like talking to a person — you could interrupt, it could laugh, it matched your energy. The model isn't reading a script. It's generating voice the same way it generates text: token by token, each one informed by everything that came before.

🔍 Concept in Practice: Why talking to Siri feels different from talking to ChatGPT voice mode

Siri is Pipeline 1 — three models passing notes. There's a noticeable pause after you speak (transcription), another pause while it thinks (generation), and the voice that comes back sounds "read aloud" rather than "spoken naturally." ChatGPT voice mode (GPT-4o) is Pipeline 2 — native audio processing. The response starts before you've even fully stopped talking, the tone matches the conversation, and it can handle overlapping speech, laughter, and emphasis. It's not smarter — it's architecturally different.

Pipeline 3: Voice Cloning and Text-to-Speech Generation

Separate from conversation — this is "generate audio that sounds like a specific person's voice." A model is trained on samples of a target voice (sometimes just a few seconds, sometimes hours of recordings) and learns to generate new speech in that voice from any text input.

This is how:

  • AI audiobook narration works (train on a voice actor's existing recordings, generate new books)
  • Voice translation works ("speak this text in French, but keep it sounding like the original English speaker")
  • Deepfake audio is created (a concerning application — realistic fake audio of real people)

Video: Diffusion in Motion

Video generation (Sora by OpenAI, Kling, Runway) works the same way as image diffusion — but adds a dimension.

Instead of denoising a 2D image (width × height), the model denoises a 3D block (width × height × time). The noise is added and removed across frames as well as pixels.

Image diffusionVideo diffusion
Pure noise (one frame of static)Pure noise (many frames of static)
Remove noise step by step → single clear imageRemove noise step by step → sequence of clear frames
Cross-attention guided by text → "a library"Cross-attention guided by text → "a library, camera panning left"
Spatial coherence (pixels relate to neighbours)Spatial AND temporal coherence (pixels relate to neighbours AND to the same region in adjacent frames)

The extra challenge: temporal consistency. Each frame needs to be internally coherent (look like a real image) AND consistent with the frames before and after it (objects don't teleport, lighting doesn't randomly change, motion is smooth).

This is why:

  • Video generation is much more expensive than image generation (3D denoising, many more tokens)
  • Generated videos often have subtle flickering or objects that "morph" slightly between frames — temporal consistency is harder than spatial consistency
  • Generated videos are currently short (a few seconds to a minute) — longer videos compound the consistency problem

🔍 Concept in Practice: Why AI-generated videos still feel "off"

Even when individual frames look photorealistic, the motion between frames can feel uncanny. A person's arm might drift subtly. A background object might shift position. Physics might not quite work (liquid pouring wrong, fabric moving unnaturally). This is because the model is making statistically plausible frames — each one looks right in isolation — but maintaining perfect physical consistency across time is a much harder constraint than making one good image.

Why "Native Multimodal" Feels Different

Some AI systems handle multiple types by chaining separate specialist models:

  • An image tool uses one model for vision, a different one for language
  • A voice assistant uses three separate models (transcribe, think, speak)
  • An image editor uses a language model to interpret your request, then passes instructions to a diffusion model

Other systems — the frontier multimodal models of 2026 — handle everything in one system:

ApproachHow it worksFeels like
Chained specialistsSeparate models for each type, passing results between themThree people passing notes in a relay — slower, information lost at each handoff
Native multimodalOne model trained on text + images + audio togetherOne person who reads, sees, and hears simultaneously — faster, nothing lost in translation

Native multimodal models can do things chained systems can't:

  • Look at an image and talk about it with awareness of both visual and textual context simultaneously
  • Generate an image mid-conversation that references things discussed in text earlier
  • Process your voice, understand your words, AND notice your emotional tone — all in one pass
  • Seamlessly switch between input/output types without losing context

This is the direction all frontier models are heading. GPT-4o, Claude, and Gemini are all natively multimodal in 2026 — they were trained on mixed streams of text, images, and audio together, not retrofitted with separate modules.

Chapter Summary

What you wanted to knowThe answer
How does AI "see" a photo?Image gets chopped into patches → vision encoder converts patches to tokens → tokens enter the same context window as text
How does typing words produce a picture?Diffusion: start from pure noise, remove noise step by step, guided by your text description via cross-attention. Not "creating from nothing" — sculpting randomness into structure
How does image editing work?Same process but starting from your image (partially noised) instead of pure noise. Less noise = less change
How does inpainting work?Mask a region, noise only that region, denoise just the mask guided by surrounding context
How does it hear me (Pipeline 1)?Transcribe to text → language model reads text → text-to-speech reads response. Three separate models.
How does it hear me (Pipeline 2)?Audio tokenised directly → model processes audio tokens natively → generates audio tokens directly. One model, end-to-end.
How does video generation work?Diffusion in 3D — denoising across width, height, AND time simultaneously
What's "native multimodal"?One model trained on all types at once, vs separate specialists stitched together

Try This

1. Go to any AI image generator and generate the same prompt three times. Notice how each result is different — that's the random noise starting point. Same denoising process, different starting static = different "path" through the sculpting.

2. Try the same prompt with low step counts (10 steps) vs high step counts (50 steps). Watch how more steps produces more detail — you're giving the model more chances to refine.

3. If you have access to an img2img tool, try the same edit at different "strength" settings (0.2 vs 0.5 vs 0.8). Watch how higher strength scrambles more of the original image and lets more change.

4. Compare talking to Siri/Alexa vs ChatGPT voice mode. Pay attention to: response speed, ability to interrupt, how natural the voice sounds, whether it picks up on your tone. You're feeling the difference between Pipeline 1 and Pipeline 2.

5. Upload the same photo to an AI and ask the same question at different image quality settings (if available). Notice how "low detail" gives faster, cheaper answers — fewer tokens, less to process.

What's Next

You now understand how AI processes and generates different types of content — text, images, audio, video. You know what happens mechanically when you upload a photo, generate an image, talk to an AI, or ask it to edit something.

But all of this — the vision encoder, the diffusion process, the voice pipeline, the language model — is doing work in the background every time you press send. That work takes time, costs money, and uses computational resources. Some requests are fast and cheap. Others are slow and expensive.

Chapter 7 takes you inside what happens the moment you actually press send — the process called inference — and explains why some responses stream in instantly while others take 30 seconds, why companies charge by the token, and why inference (not training) is where most of the money is being spent.

📖 Key Terms — Quick Reference Vision encoder — Chops an image into patches and converts each patch into numbers a language model can read. How AI "sees." Diffusion model — Generates images by starting from random noise and gradually removing it, guided by your text prompt. Forward diffusion — The training process: take a real image and progressively add noise until it's pure static. The model learns to reverse this. Reverse diffusion (denoising) — The generation process: start from noise and sculpt it step by step into a coherent image. More steps = more detail. Cross-attention — The mechanism connecting your text prompt to the image generation process. At each denoising step, the model checks which words are relevant to which region of the image. How words steer pixels. Inpainting — Editing only a masked region of an image while leaving the rest untouched. The model generates new content for the masked area that blends with the surroundings. img2img — Using an existing image as the starting point instead of pure noise. Lower strength = closer to original; higher strength = more creative freedom. ASR (automatic speech recognition) — Converting spoken audio into text. The first step in Pipeline 1 (traditional voice systems). TTS (text-to-speech) — Converting text into spoken audio. The last step in Pipeline 1. Native audio tokens — Processing voice directly as audio tokens without converting to text first. How Pipeline 2 works (GPT-4o, modern voice modes). Enables interruption, tone matching, and natural conversation. Multimodal — A model trained on multiple types of input/output (text, images, audio, video) together, not retrofitted with separate modules. The direction all frontier models are heading. Pipeline 1 vs Pipeline 2 — Pipeline 1: three separate models (speech-to-text → language model → text-to-speech). Pipeline 2: one model processing audio natively. Pipeline 2 is why modern AI voice feels like talking to a person rather than dictating to a machine.

Chapter 7: Inference — When You Actually Ask It Something

Part III · Using the Thing

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

  • Explain the difference between training (building the model) and inference (using it)
  • Describe what actually happens — step by step — when you press "send" on a message
  • Understand why the same prompt can give different answers each time
  • Explain why some responses are fast and others are slow
  • Understand why inference (not training) is where most of the money goes at scale

Key Terms Introduced in This Chapter

TermPlain English
InferenceUsing a trained model to generate output — applying the learned patterns to new input. The "job" after "school."
PrefillThe first phase of inference: processing your entire input at once to build an understanding of it
DecodeThe second phase: generating output tokens one at a time, each dependent on the one before it
TemperatureA setting that controls randomness — low temperature = predictable/safe answers, high = creative/surprising
Top-k / Top-pFilters that narrow down which tokens the system can pick from at each step
LatencyHow long you wait for a response — the time between pressing "send" and seeing the first word appear
ThroughputHow many requests a system can handle at once — serving millions of users simultaneously
BatchingProcessing multiple users' requests together for efficiency

Training Was School. This Is the Job.

In Chapter 5, you learned how a model gets built: billions of examples, the guess-check-adjust loop, months of compute, millions of dollars. All of that work produces a single thing — a file of frozen weights.

Now the model is done. It's deployed. It's running on servers somewhere. And you open a chat window and type: "What's the best way to get coffee stains out of a white shirt?"

What happens next is inference — the model applying its learned patterns to your input and generating a response. No learning happens. No weights change. The model isn't getting smarter from your question. It's running your tokens through its frozen numbers and producing output.

The relationship between training and inference:

TrainingInference
What it doesBuilds the model (adjusts weights)Uses the model (applies frozen weights)
When it happensOnce (or occasionally, for updates)Every single time someone uses the system
How longWeeks to monthsSeconds
Cost per occurrence$50M–$1B+ for frontier modelsFractions of a cent per request
But at scale...One-time capital expenseOngoing operational cost — scales with every user

Inference is where most of the money goes — not training. Training is eye-wateringly expensive per run, but it happens once. Inference happens billions of times per day, every day, for as long as the product exists. Gartner reported in August 2026 that for the first time, global spending on inference infrastructure ($23.3 billion) has overtaken training ($19 billion). The cost of using AI now exceeds the cost of building it.

What Happens When You Press "Send"

Let's trace your message through the system, step by step.

You type: "What's the best way to get coffee stains out of a white shirt?"

Step 1: Your message gets tokenised

The text is split into tokens (Chapter 4). Your question becomes a sequence of maybe 15–20 tokens. These get combined with other things already in the context window:

  • The system instructions (hidden instructions telling the model how to behave)
  • The conversation history (everything you and the model have said so far)
  • Any documents or files you've attached
  • The current date/time (injected by the application layer)
  • Any tool results (if the system searched the web before answering)

All of this — your new message plus everything else — forms the complete input. This could be hundreds or thousands of tokens.

Step 2: Prefill (the model reads your input)

The model processes the entire input in a single pass. All those tokens flow through the network's layers simultaneously — this is where the transformer's parallel processing (Chapter 5) pays off. The model builds an internal representation of what you're asking: the context, the meaning, the relationships between words.

During this step, the model also creates a KV cache — a set of "scratch notes" about each token. These notes will be reused during generation so the model doesn't have to re-read the entire input for each new word it produces.

⏸️ "What's a KV cache, actually?" Remember attention (Chapter 5) — where each token calculates how relevant every other token is? That calculation produces two things for each token: a Key (which says "this is what I contain") and a Value (which says "this is what I contribute when attended to"). During prefill, the model computes these Keys and Values for every input token. Then it saves them. During generation, when the model is producing the 50th output token, it needs to attend to all previous tokens (your input + the 49 tokens it's already generated). Without the cache, it would have to recompute the Keys and Values for all previous tokens from scratch — every single step. With the cache, it just looks up the saved notes. Massively faster. It's like taking notes during a lecture so you don't have to re-listen to the whole recording every time you want to check a fact.

Prefill is fast relative to what comes next, because it processes all input tokens in parallel.

Step 3: Decode (the model generates a response, one token at a time)

Now the model starts producing output. The key: it generates one token at a time. Each new token depends on everything before it — the input plus all the tokens it has already generated.

The process at each step:

  1. The model calculates a probability for every token in its vocabulary (~100,000 options).
  2. It applies temperature and sampling settings (more on this in a moment) to narrow the choices.
  3. It picks one token.
  4. That token gets appended to the sequence.
  5. Back to step 1 for the next token.

This continues until the model produces a stop signal (a special token that means "I'm done") or hits a length limit.

This is why you see responses appear word by word in a chat interface — each word appears as it's generated. The model isn't writing the whole response and then showing it to you. It's producing it live, token by token.

🔍 Concept in Practice: Why responses stream in word by word When you watch an AI response appear gradually — word by word, sometimes pausing mid-sentence — you're watching the decode phase in real time. Each word you see appear is a newly generated token. The pauses happen when the model is computing the probability distribution for the next token. It's not "thinking" in a human sense — it's running the maths for the next prediction.

Why the Same Prompt Gives Different Answers

📼 Rewind it: Turning temperature down is a Miranda Priestly edit pass: fewer next-word options are allowed onto the runway, so the result is usually more consistent. That does not make it more intelligent, more factual, or automatically correct.

Ask the same question twice and you'll often get different responses. Not just different words — sometimes genuinely different content, structure, or reasoning paths. Why?

Because generation isn't deterministic. At each step, the model is looking at ~100,000 possible next tokens, each with a different likelihood. It doesn't always pick the most likely one. Instead, it draws from a weighted lottery — tokens with higher likelihood are more likely to be picked, but it's not guaranteed. Like drawing a name from a hat where some names are on more slips than others.

Temperature is the main dial that controls this:

  • Temperature = 0 (or very low): The model almost always picks the highest-probability token. Responses are consistent, predictable, and repetitive. Ask the same thing twice, get nearly the same answer.
  • Temperature = 1: The model samples normally from its learned distribution. Good balance of variety and coherence.
  • Temperature > 1: The model flattens the distribution — less-likely tokens get a bigger chance. Responses become more creative, surprising, and potentially incoherent.

Think of it like this: at low temperature, the model only picks from the obvious choices. At high temperature, it's willing to take riskier, less conventional options.

Top-k and Top-p are additional filters:

  • Top-k: Only consider the k most likely next tokens (e.g., top 40). Everything else gets zero probability, regardless of how reasonable it might be.
  • Top-p (also called nucleus sampling): Only consider tokens whose cumulative probability adds up to p (e.g., 0.9). This adapts — sometimes that's 5 tokens, sometimes 500, depending on how confident the model is.

In practice, you rarely control these yourself — the application (ChatGPT, Claude, etc.) sets them based on the task. But now you know why two people asking the same question get different answers, and why AI-generated text doesn't feel robotic — it's because there's controlled randomness baked into every single token choice.

⏸️ Wait — so it's just randomly picking words? Not randomly — probabilistically. The probabilities come from the patterns learned during training. The model knows that after "The capital of France is" the token "Paris" has very high probability. It's not random that it picks "Paris" — the randomness only matters when multiple tokens are similarly plausible. "The best way to remove coffee stains is" could reasonably continue with "vinegar" or "baking soda" or "cold water" — and which one it picks on any given run is where the sampling randomness enters.

Why Some Responses Are Slow (and Others Aren't)

You've probably noticed: sometimes an AI responds almost instantly, other times there's a noticeable delay. What determines the speed?

What makes inference faster:

  • Shorter input — less to process in the prefill phase
  • Shorter output — fewer tokens to generate one-by-one in the decode phase
  • Smaller model — fewer parameters to run the maths through
  • Better hardware — faster chips process tokens more quickly
  • Cached context — if the system instructions haven't changed since last time, the KV cache can be reused

What makes inference slower:

  • Long conversation history — everything in the context window gets processed every time
  • Long output — a 2,000-word essay takes longer to generate than a yes/no answer because it's 2,000 sequential token predictions
  • Large model — more parameters = more computation per token
  • High traffic — when millions of people are using the system simultaneously, requests queue up

Latency is what you feel as a user — the time between pressing send and seeing the first token appear. For current frontier models, this is typically 0.5–2 seconds for the first token, then 30–100 tokens per second streaming after that.

Throughput is what the company cares about — how many simultaneous requests the system can serve. This is where hardware, batching, and engineering determine whether the system can handle 10 users or 10 million.

Batching: Serving Millions at Once

When only one person is using the system, inference is straightforward — run their tokens through the model, generate a response. But ChatGPT alone handles hundreds of millions of conversations per week. Thousands of requests arrive every second. How do you serve all of them without needing a separate GPU for each person?

Batching. Instead of processing one request at a time (like a restaurant that only seats one table at once), the system groups multiple requests together and processes them simultaneously on the same GPU (like a restaurant that seats forty tables and has a kitchen running orders in parallel).

What makes this work mechanically: remember that inference has two phases (section 7.2) — prefill (reading all the input) and decode (generating output token by token). At any given moment, different users are at different stages. User A is in prefill (their input is being read). User B is on their 15th output token. User C is on their 200th. The GPU can work on all three simultaneously because they're all just matrix multiplications on different sets of numbers — and GPUs (Chapter 16) do thousands of multiplications in parallel by nature.

Modern inference engines (like vLLM, TensorRT-LLM) are specifically designed to maximise this: they continuously add new requests to the batch as old ones finish, keeping the GPU at near-100% utilisation. A single GPU that takes 2 seconds to serve one user might serve 50 users in those same 2 seconds through batching — not by going faster, but by doing all their work simultaneously.

The trade-off: batching increases throughput (more users served) but can slightly increase latency (each individual request might wait a fraction longer). Systems balance this constantly based on traffic.

The Economics: Why Inference Is Where the Money Goes

The cost split is counterintuitive:

Training: Eye-wateringly expensive once. A frontier model costs $200M–$1B+ to train. But you do it once (or occasionally for model updates). It's a capital investment.

Inference: Cheap per request. A single conversation costs fractions of a cent. But multiply by millions of users, millions of requests per day, every day, for years — and inference becomes the dominant cost.

By 2026, companies running AI at scale spend 70–90% of their AI compute budget on inference, not training. The training gets the headlines. The inference gets the bills.

Why output tokens cost more than input tokens (something you noticed in Chapter 4's pricing tables):

  • Input tokens (prefill): Processed in parallel. The model reads your entire prompt in one pass. Efficient.
  • Output tokens (decode): Generated sequentially, one at a time, each depending on the last. The model can't parallelise this — it must wait for each token before predicting the next. More compute per token.

That's why every API charges 3–10x more for output tokens than input tokens. It's not a pricing trick — it reflects the actual compute cost difference between reading (parallel) and writing (sequential).

🔍 Concept in Practice: Why AI subscription services limit "heavy" usage If you've hit usage limits on ChatGPT, Claude, or other AI services — being told you've sent too many messages or need to wait — now you know why. Each response you generate costs real compute (output tokens generated sequentially on expensive hardware). A subscription at $20/month is subsidising inference costs that could be $0.05–$1.00+ per long conversation. Heavy users sending hundreds of long messages per day can cost the company far more than the subscription covers. Usage limits are inference economics in action.

The Journey of a Request (Complete Picture)

Let's put it all together. When you press send:

  1. Your message enters the system
  2. Tokenisation — your text is chopped into tokens
  3. Context assembly — your tokens are combined with system instructions, conversation history, tool results, and metadata (like the current date)
  4. Prefill — the model processes the full context in one parallel pass, building an internal representation
  5. Decode — the model generates output tokens one at a time, each sampled from a probability distribution shaped by temperature and top-p settings
  6. Streaming — each token is sent to your screen as it's generated (that's why responses appear word by word)
  7. Stop — the model produces a stop token or hits its output limit. Response complete.

The entire process takes 1–30 seconds depending on response length. No weights changed. No learning happened. The model is exactly the same after answering your question as before. It just ran your input through its frozen patterns and produced the most probable continuation.

The System Picture So Far

What we've built up across the first six chapters:

[Data sources] → [Collection & Labelling] → [Tokenisation] → [Training Loop] → [Trained Model (frozen weights)]
                                                                                         ↓
                                                                              ──── INFERENCE ────
                                                                                         ↓
[Your message] → [Tokenisation] → [Context Window assembly] → [Prefill (parallel)] → [Decode (sequential, token by token)] → [Response streamed to you]

Your Learning So Far:

ChapterWhat you learned
1The difference between automation (human wrote rules) and AI (machine learned from examples)
2The AI family: ML → Deep Learning → Generative AI → Agentic AI
3Where training data comes from, who labels it, how bias enters, what synthetic data is
4Tokens — how text/images/audio get chopped into chunks the system can process. Why everything is measured in tokens.
5Training — the guess-check-adjust loop, neural networks, transformers, attention, what a model actually IS (a file of frozen numbers)
6Multimodal — how AI sees images, generates pictures (diffusion), hears voice, and makes video
7Inference — what happens when you actually use the model. Prefill → decode → streaming. Why answers vary. Why it costs money.

Where you are in the picture: You now understand the complete pipeline from raw data to a response appearing on your screen. Next, we'll look at how the raw trained model gets refined into something actually helpful — that's fine-tuning and RLHF.

Try This

Watch the streaming. Next time you use an AI chatbot, watch the response appear. Notice it comes word by word (or chunk by chunk). That's the decode phase in real time. Each word you see is a new token being generated.

Now try this: ask the same question twice. Notice the responses are different — different wording, maybe different structure or examples. That's temperature and sampling at work. The model isn't broken — it's drawing from a probability distribution, and different draws produce different (but usually valid) outputs.

A harder experiment: Ask a factual question with one correct answer ("What's the capital of Japan?") — you'll get the same answer every time because the probability distribution heavily favours "Tokyo." Now ask an open-ended creative question ("Write me a short poem about coffee") — you'll get a different poem every time because many tokens are similarly plausible at each step.

What's Next

The model can now generate responses. But a raw pre-trained model — one that's only been through the training described in Chapter 5 — isn't actually pleasant to use. It might produce toxic content, because toxic content was in the training data. It might not follow your instructions, because "follow the user's instructions" wasn't its training task. It might ramble, or refuse to answer, or respond in a format you didn't want.

Chapter 8 zooms in on something you might have already wondered: if the model has no memory between conversations, how does it seem to know things? Context windows, embeddings, vector databases, and RAG — the system that gives a model access to knowledge beyond its frozen training weights.

📖 Key Terms — Quick Reference Inference — Using the trained model. Applying frozen weights to new input. No learning happens. "Training is school. Inference is the job." Prefill — Phase 1: the model reads your entire input in one parallel pass, building an internal representation and KV cache. Decode — Phase 2: the model generates output tokens one at a time, sequentially. This is why output costs more than input. Temperature — Controls randomness. Low = predictable (always picks the most likely token). High = creative/surprising (willing to pick less likely tokens). Doesn't change what the model knows — just how it samples from what it knows. Top-k — Only consider the k most probable next tokens. Everything else gets zero chance. Top-p (nucleus sampling) — Only consider tokens whose cumulative probability reaches p. Adapts to confidence: when the model is sure, fewer options; when uncertain, more. Latency — Time from pressing send to seeing the first word. Determined by input size, model size, hardware speed, and traffic. Throughput — How many requests the system handles simultaneously. Determined by hardware, batching, and system design. Batching — Grouping multiple users' requests to process simultaneously on the same hardware. Increases throughput. KV cache — "Scratch notes" the model keeps about each token during prefill, so it doesn't have to re-read the entire input when generating each new output token.

Chapter 8: Context, Memory, and What the Model Can See

Part III · Using the Thing

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

  • Explain why AI has no memory by default — and what "memory" actually means when companies claim to offer it
  • Describe the difference between knowledge (frozen in weights) and context (provided live each time)
  • Explain what embeddings are and how they let machines measure "similarity"
  • Understand how RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) gives a model access to information it wasn't trained on
  • Describe why "the model knows things" is misleading — and what's actually happening

Key Terms Introduced in This Chapter

TermPlain English
ContextEverything the model can "see" right now — your message, the conversation history, system instructions, retrieved documents. All in one window.
Memory (in AI)Not actual memory — information stored externally and re-injected into the context window each time. The model itself remembers nothing.
EmbeddingA list of numbers that represents the meaning of a piece of text — arranged so that similar meanings sit near each other
VectorA list of numbers. An embedding is a specific type of vector that encodes meaning.
Vector databaseA specialised database that stores embeddings and lets you search by similarity — "find me things similar to this"
RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation)A system that retrieves relevant documents, injects them into the context window, and lets the model generate a response grounded in that information
Semantic searchSearching by meaning rather than by exact keywords — "find documents about the same concept, even if they use different words"

The Model Remembers Nothing

Let's start with something that surprises most people: the model has no memory.

When you have a conversation with ChatGPT or Claude, it feels like the system remembers what you said earlier. You mention your name at the start, and it uses your name later. You describe a problem in message 1, and it references it in message 10. It seems like it's remembering.

It isn't. What's actually happening:

Every time you send a new message, the entire conversation history — every message you've sent and every response it gave — gets loaded into the context window alongside your new message. The model reads the whole thing from scratch, every single turn. It's not recalling anything from memory. It's re-reading the transcript.

This is why, in Chapter 5, we said the model is stateless — it's a recipe that runs fresh every time. You pour your ingredients in (the tokens), the recipe runs (the weights do their work), output comes out. Then the kitchen is wiped clean. Nothing lingers between requests. The "memory" you experience is the application layer (ChatGPT's interface, Claude's interface) saving your conversation transcript and feeding the whole thing back in as input each time you send a new message.

🔍 Concept in Practice: Why starting a new conversation "resets" the AI When you open a new chat, the AI doesn't know who you are, what you were working on, or what you discussed yesterday. That's because the previous conversation's transcript isn't in the new context window. The model hasn't forgotten — it never knew in the first place. New chat, new context: "We were on a break!" Each conversation is a completely isolated context window. What was in the old window doesn't carry over unless something explicitly moves it.

The Two Sources of "Knowledge"

Based on everything you've learned so far, there are only two ways the model "knows" something when it responds to you:

Source 1: Training weights (frozen, general, potentially stale)

Everything the model absorbed during training (Chapters 3-5) is encoded in its weights. This includes patterns of language, general facts about the world, reasoning patterns, style — everything that was in the training data. This knowledge is frozen at the training cutoff date and never updates.

Source 2: Context window (live, specific, provided right now)

Everything placed into the context window for this specific request — your message, conversation history, system instructions, documents you uploaded, search results, the current date. This is live information, specific to this moment, and disappears the moment the response is complete.

The model can't tell the difference. When it generates a response, it draws on both sources simultaneously. It doesn't flag "this fact comes from my training" versus "this fact comes from the document you just uploaded." It processes everything in the context window as one combined input.

This distinction — training knowledge vs contextual information — is fundamental to understanding both the capabilities and limitations of these systems.

What "Memory" Actually Means (When Companies Say It)

📼 Rewind it: Calling every saved preference "memory" is how you end up with a Burn Book full of assumptions. Ask what was actually stored, where it lives, and whether the product will retrieve it next time. The model itself has not quietly become a person who remembers you.

In 2024–2026, many AI products launched "memory" features — ChatGPT's Memory, Claude's memory, and others. What are these actually doing?

They're not giving the model actual memory. They're doing this:

  1. Extracting facts from your conversations ("User's name is Ali." "User prefers concise answers." "User is working on a textbook project.")
  2. Storing those facts externally — in a database, outside the model.
  3. Injecting relevant facts back into the context window each time you start a new conversation.

The model still has no memory. But the application layer remembers things about you and feeds them back in each time, making it appear as if the model remembers. It's a clever design — but mechanically, it's just context window management.

The three tiers of AI "memory" (as of 2026):

TierWhat it isWhere it lives
In-contextThe current conversation. Everything the model can see right now.Inside the context window (disappears when the conversation ends)
Semantic memoryStored facts about you, your preferences, your projects.External database. Injected into context window when relevant.
Episodic memoryRecords of past interactions — what you discussed, when, what was decided.External database. Retrieved and injected when relevant.

All three tiers ultimately work the same way: information gets placed into the context window so the model can see it. The model itself is always stateless. The sophistication is in what gets stored and what gets retrieved — not in the model gaining actual memory.

⏸️ Wait — so it could "forget" things it supposedly memorised? Yes. If the memory system fails to retrieve a fact, or if the context window is too full to include it, the model won't have access to it — even if it's stored in the database. "Memory" in AI is retrieval, not storage inside the model. Anything that isn't in the current context window might as well not exist.

Embeddings: Turning Meaning Into Numbers

Here's a problem the system needs to solve: you've uploaded 500 documents and asked a question. The system needs to figure out which of those documents are relevant to your question — without reading all 500 every time (that would blow the context window and cost a fortune).

How would you solve this? Think of a library. If books were shelved in the order they were purchased, finding anything relevant would mean scanning every single shelf. But libraries don't work that way. They put books about similar topics near each other — dog training next to puppy behaviour, French cooking next to Italian cooking. If you know where one relevant book is, everything nearby is probably relevant too.

Embeddings do this for text — but instead of a physical shelf with spatial proximity, they use a space made of numbers. Every piece of text gets placed somewhere in this numerical space, arranged so that texts with similar meanings sit near each other.

The technical name for "a piece of text's location in this space" is an embedding — a list of numbers (typically 1,000–3,000 of them) that represents where this text sits in meaning-space.

What that looks like concretely:

Example:

  • The sentence "How do I remove coffee stains?" might become the vector [0.23, -0.87, 0.41, 0.12, ...]
  • The sentence "What's the best way to clean coffee off fabric?" — even though it uses completely different words — would get a very similar vector [0.24, -0.85, 0.39, 0.14, ...]
  • The sentence "What's the capital of France?" would get a very different vector [-0.56, 0.33, -0.71, 0.88, ...]

The embedding model has learned (through its own training) that the first two sentences mean the same thing — so it places them near each other in number-space. The third sentence means something entirely different, so it lands far away.

What's remarkable — and somewhat unexplained — is how well this works. The model wasn't taught a theory of meaning. It was trained on enormous amounts of text and somehow developed numerical representations where proximity genuinely corresponds to semantic similarity. It works across languages, across domains, even across concepts that were never explicitly paired in training. Why this produces such useful similarity measurements is not fully understood — but the practical result is that you can search millions of documents by meaning, not just by keywords, and get relevantly matched results.

How "near" and "far" are measured:

The system calculates how close together two sets of numbers are. Close numbers = similar meaning. Far-apart numbers = different meaning. (The specific technique is called cosine similarity — you don't need to know the maths, just that it's a reliable way to measure "how similar are these two things?")

This is how machines do "similarity" — not by understanding meaning the way you do, but by placing meanings in a numerical space where closeness = relatedness.

Vector Databases: The Similarity Search Engine

Once you can turn text into embeddings, you need somewhere to store them and search through them. That's a vector database.

A vector database stores thousands or millions of text chunks (documents, paragraphs, notes, previous conversations), each one converted to an embedding. When you ask a question, your question also gets embedded, and the database finds the stored chunks whose embeddings are closest to your question's embedding.

This is semantic search — searching by meaning rather than keywords. You don't need to use the exact same words as the stored document. You just need to ask about the same concept, and the embedding similarity will find the relevant information.

Example flow:

  1. You have 500 company documents, each split into paragraphs.
  2. Each paragraph gets embedded (turned into a vector of ~1,500 numbers) and stored in a vector database.
  3. A user asks: "What's our refund policy for damaged items?"
  4. That question gets embedded.
  5. The vector database finds the 3–5 stored paragraphs whose embeddings are most similar.
  6. Those paragraphs get injected into the context window.
  7. The model generates a response grounded in those specific documents.

The model didn't "know" your refund policy from training. It was given the relevant information in its context window, retrieved via embedding similarity.

RAG: Retrieval-Augmented Generation

📼 Rewind it: Elle Woods does not win because she sounds confident; she wins because she brings the relevant evidence into the room. Retrieval gives a model material to work from at answer time. You still check whether it fetched the right, current material.

The flow described above has a name: RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation). It's one of the most important architectural patterns in production AI (2024–2026).

The problem RAG solves:

  • The model's training knowledge is frozen (stale, general, potentially wrong)
  • The model can't access your company's documents, your emails, your databases
  • You need the model to answer questions grounded in specific, current, private information

The RAG pipeline:

User asks a question
        ↓
Question gets embedded (turned into a vector)
        ↓
Vector database searched for similar chunks
        ↓
Top matching chunks retrieved
        ↓
Chunks injected into the context window alongside the question
        ↓
Model generates a response using both its training knowledge AND the retrieved context
        ↓
User gets an answer grounded in actual documents

Let's watch it work — a real example, step by step:

You're using your company's AI assistant. You ask: "What's our parental leave policy?"

  1. Your question gets embedded. A small, fast embedding model converts "What's our parental leave policy?" into a vector — say, [0.31, -0.72, 0.18, ...] (1,536 numbers that represent the meaning of your question).
  1. Vector database searched. The system compares your question's vector against thousands of pre-embedded chunks from your company's HR documents. It calculates similarity scores. The chunk about parental leave scores 0.94 (very similar meaning). The chunk about vacation policy scores 0.71. The chunk about the office dress code scores 0.12 (irrelevant — distant in meaning-space).
  1. Top chunks retrieved. The system grabs the top 3–5 most similar chunks. In this case: the parental leave policy paragraph, the related benefits overview, and the FAQ about leave extensions.
  1. Chunks injected into context. These chunks get placed into the context window alongside your question. The model now sees:

`` [System instructions] + [Retrieved: "Parental leave: 20 weeks paid for birthing parents, 6 weeks for non-birthing parents, extendable to 12 with manager approval..."] + [Your question: "What's our parental leave policy?"] ``

  1. Model generates. The model reads everything in the context window — your question AND the retrieved policy document — and generates a response grounded in that specific text: "Your parental leave policy provides 20 weeks paid leave for birthing parents and 6 weeks for non-birthing parents, with the option to extend to 12 weeks with manager approval."
  1. Response delivered. You get an accurate answer about your company's specific policy — even though the model was never trained on your HR documents.

Total time: ~1.5 seconds. What made it possible: embedding + similarity search + context injection. The model didn't "know" your policy. It read it, right then, because the RAG pipeline put it in front of the model at the right moment.

Why this matters: RAG is how AI products answer questions about your data — your company's knowledge base, your notes, your files. The model wasn't trained on your documents. But it doesn't need to be — the documents get retrieved and placed into context in real time. The model reads them on the fly and generates an answer.

This is also how AI systems stay current despite their knowledge cutoff. A RAG system can retrieve today's news article and inject it into context — the model reads it and can answer questions about it, even though it was published after training ended.

💡 Training vs RAG — two ways to give a model information Training: Bake information permanently into the weights. Expensive, slow, frozen once done. Good for general knowledge and capabilities. RAG: Retrieve information on-the-fly and inject into context. Cheap, fast, always current. Good for specific, private, or frequently-changing information. Most production AI systems use both: a pre-trained model (general knowledge in weights) + RAG (specific current knowledge retrieved into context). Neither alone is sufficient.

Why "The Model Knows Things" Is Misleading

You now have the full picture to understand why this phrase is misleading:

When someone says "GPT knows about World War II" or "Claude knows Python" — what's actually happening is: during training, the model absorbed statistical patterns from text about WWII or Python code. Those patterns are encoded in its weights. When you ask a question, those patterns produce tokens that look like knowledge.

But:

  • It has no concept of truth. It generates the most probable next tokens based on patterns — which are usually correct for well-documented topics, but can be wrong.
  • It can't verify what it's saying. It has no mechanism for checking its output against reality.
  • It doesn't "know" in the way you know. You can introspect ("am I sure about this?"). The model can't — it just generates with whatever confidence the patterns produce.

When the model is wrong — when it "halluccinates" a fact, invents a citation, or confidently states something false — it's not lying. It's producing the most probable token sequence given its patterns, and those patterns happen to generate something that isn't true. There's no internal flag that says "I'm making this up."

🔍 Concept in Practice: Why AI invents fake citations Ask an AI to "give me three academic papers about X with full citations" and it will often produce author names, paper titles, journal names, and years — that don't exist. The generated text looks exactly like a real citation (correct format, plausible author names, real journal names) because the patterns of what citations look like are strong in training data. But the specific combination of author + title + year was never verified against reality. The model generated what a citation should look like — not what actually exists. This is pattern-completion without a truth-check.

The System Picture So Far

[Data] → [Tokenisation] → [Training] → [Trained Model (frozen weights)]
                                                    ↓
                                         ──── INFERENCE ────
                                                    ↓
                                        [Context Window Assembly]
                                          /        |        \
                        [System Instructions]  [Your Message]  [Conversation History]
                                          \        |        /
                                   + [Retrieved Documents via RAG]
                                   + [Memory facts re-injected]
                                   + [Tool results (search, etc.)]
                                                    ↓
                                    [Prefill → Decode → Response]

Where you are: You now understand the full journey — from raw data all the way through to a response appearing on your screen, including how the model gets access to information beyond its training. Next: how the raw model gets shaped into something actually helpful.

Try This

Test the "no memory" principle. Open a new conversation with any AI chatbot. Ask it: "What's my name?" It won't know — because nothing in the context window tells it. Now tell it your name, have a short conversation, then start a new conversation and ask again. Gone. The first conversation's context isn't in the new window.

Test semantic search thinking. Think of two sentences that mean the same thing but use completely different words:

  • "I need to cancel my subscription"
  • "How do I stop my monthly payments?"

A keyword search for "cancel subscription" wouldn't find the second one (no matching words). A semantic search using embeddings would — because the meaning is the same. That's the power embeddings give to retrieval systems.

A harder question: If the model generates a wrong answer, is the problem more likely to be (a) bad patterns in the weights, or (b) missing or irrelevant context in the window? The answer is usually (b) — and that's why RAG and better context management are where most improvement comes from in practice, not retraining the model.

What's Next

You now know that the model is stateless and that all "knowledge" comes from either frozen weights or live context. You know how embeddings and vector databases let systems find relevant information. You know how RAG injects that information into the model's view.

But a raw pre-trained model — even with great context — still isn't great to use. It might be rude, unhelpful, or produce harmful content. It doesn't know how to follow instructions reliably. It doesn't know what "be helpful" means.

Chapter 9 takes us to fine-tuning and RLHF: the process of taking a model that can generate text and teaching it to be helpful, harmless, and honest — to follow instructions, to refuse harmful requests, and to admit when it doesn't know something.

📖 Key Terms — Quick Reference Context window — Everything the model can see for this specific request. Conversation history + system instructions + retrieved documents + your message. Hard size limit (1M+ tokens for frontier models, 2026). Stateless — The model itself remembers nothing between requests. Each inference is independent. "Memory" is external systems re-injecting information. Embedding — A list of numbers (vector) that represents the meaning of text. Similar meanings → similar numbers → close in vector space. Vector database — Stores embeddings. Enables semantic search: "find stored text whose meaning is closest to this query." RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) — Retrieve relevant documents → inject into context → model generates a response grounded in those documents. How AI answers questions about your specific/current data. Semantic search — Searching by meaning, not keywords. Powered by embedding similarity. "Cancel subscription" and "stop monthly payments" match because their embeddings are close. Hallucination — When the model generates something false with confidence. Happens because it produces the most probable token sequence from patterns, with no mechanism to verify truth. Not lying — pattern-completing without a truth-check. Memory (in AI products) — External storage of facts about you, retrieved and injected into context each session. The model doesn't remember — the application layer does the remembering and re-feeding.

Chapter 9: Making It Smarter — The Customisation Spectrum

Part III · Using the Thing

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

  • Describe the full spectrum of ways to customise an AI system — from free and fast to expensive and powerful
  • Explain when you'd use prompting vs RAG vs fine-tuning vs full training
  • Understand how RLHF and DPO shape a model's behaviour after pre-training
  • Explain what distillation, quantisation, and mixture of experts do and why they matter
  • Use a decision framework: "Given what I need, which approach makes sense?"

Key Terms Introduced in This Chapter

TermPlain English
PromptingPhrasing your input carefully to get the output you want — the cheapest, fastest customisation
Few-shot promptingIncluding examples of what you want directly in your prompt — "here are 3 examples, now do the 4th"
Fine-tuningAdditional training on a smaller, specific dataset — adjusting the model's weights for a particular domain or behaviour
RLHFReinforcement Learning from Human Feedback — humans rate outputs ("A is better than B"), and those ratings shape the model's behaviour
DPODirect Preference Optimization — a simpler alternative to RLHF that skips the reward model step
Constitutional AIAnthropic's approach: giving the model a written set of principles and having it judge its own outputs against them
DistillationTraining a small, fast model to mimic a large, powerful one
QuantisationCompressing a model's numbers (e.g. from 16-bit to 4-bit) so it runs on less hardware
Mixture of Experts (MoE)A model architecture where only a small fraction of parameters activate for any given token — big capacity, lower compute

The Spectrum: Cheapest to Most Expensive

Say you're using an AI chatbot at work and it keeps getting your company's refund policy wrong. It confidently tells customers they have 60 days to return items when the actual policy is 30 days. It's costing you complaints.

You have options. You could rephrase your instructions to be clearer — that's free and takes thirty seconds. You could feed it the actual policy document so it has the right information — that takes a few days to set up. You could retrain the model on thousands of examples of correct customer service responses — that costs real money and takes weeks. Or you could build an entirely new model from scratch — but nobody does that unless they have hundreds of millions of dollars and a very good reason.

Those options aren't random. They sit on a spectrum — from cheapest and fastest to most expensive and powerful — and knowing where to start (and when to move down) is one of the most practical things you can learn about using AI in the real world.

Here's the spectrum, from lightest to heaviest:

LevelWhat it isCostTimeWho does it
1. PromptingPhrase your question wellFreeSecondsAnyone
2. Few-shot promptingInclude examples in your promptFreeMinutesAnyone
3. System instructionsSet behaviour rules the model follows every timeFreeMinutesAnyone with access
4. RAGRetrieve relevant documents into contextLow–mediumDays to weeks to buildEngineers
5. Fine-tuningTrain further on specific data (adjusts weights)$100–$100K+Days to weeksML engineers
6. RLHF / DPOShape behaviour through human preference ratings$50K–$1M+Weeks to monthsSpecialised teams
7. Full pre-trainingTrain an entire model from scratch$50M–$1B+MonthsOnly a handful of companies

The key insight from industry practice (2026): start at the top and only move down when you've genuinely exhausted the lighter options. Most problems can be solved with prompting + RAG. Fine-tuning is for behaviour changes that can't be encoded in a prompt. Full pre-training is almost never the right answer unless you're building a foundation model company.

The 2026 rule of thumb: prompt first, retrieve for knowledge, fine-tune for behaviour.

Prompting and Few-Shot (Levels 1–2)

Try this: ask an AI system to summarise a long document. You'll get a summary. Now ask the same system the same question, but this time add: "Summarise this in exactly 3 bullet points, each under 15 words, focusing only on financial implications." You'll get a dramatically different — and likely more useful — response.

Nothing changed about the model. You changed how you asked. That's prompting — and the gap between a lazy prompt and a precise one is often the gap between a useless answer and an excellent one.

Zero-shot: You just ask directly. "Summarise this article." No examples, no special formatting. Works for straightforward tasks where the model's default behaviour is close enough to what you want.

Few-shot: You include examples of what you want before asking. "Here are 3 customer emails and how I categorised them. Now categorise this 4th one." The model sees the pattern in your examples and applies it to the new case — without any retraining.

This was the breakthrough that GPT-3 demonstrated in 2020: you could "program" the model just by writing examples in plain English. No code, no training, no waiting. The examples go into the context window, the model reads them, and it follows the pattern. It's shockingly effective.

Why this works (mechanically): The model is a pattern-completion machine (Chapter 5). Its entire training was: given a sequence of tokens, predict what comes next. Show it a pattern of input→output pairs, and it continues that pattern — because that's literally the only thing it knows how to do. Three examples of "email → category" followed by a fourth email? The most probable next tokens are the correct category. The model isn't "reasoning about categorisation." It's completing a pattern it was shown.

When it's not enough: When you need the model to consistently behave in a very specific way (always use a particular format, always adopt a particular tone) across thousands of interactions without repeating the examples every time. Prompts live in the context window — they have to be sent every single time. If your behaviour modification takes 500 tokens of examples and instructions, you're paying for those 500 tokens on every single request, forever. That's when you consider baking the behaviour in permanently through fine-tuning.

RAG (Level 4) — Giving It Knowledge Without Retraining

Back to that refund policy problem from the start of this chapter. The chatbot says 60 days when it should say 30. Why is it wrong? Because the correct policy isn't in its training data — the model was trained on the internet, not on your internal HR documents. It's guessing based on whatever refund policies it saw during training.

RAG (Chapter 8) solves this directly: instead of hoping the model "knows" your policy, you retrieve the actual document and inject it into the context window. The model reads it right there, in real time, and answers based on what it just read — not what it vaguely remembers from training.

You've already seen the full mechanism in Chapter 8. In the context of the customisation spectrum, here's the key distinction:

What RAG changes: What the model knows about. It gives the model access to specific, current, or private information it was never trained on.

What RAG doesn't change: How the model behaves. It still responds in the same style, follows the same patterns, has the same tendencies. It just has more (and better) information to work with.

The practical test: If your problem is "the model doesn't know the right answer" — RAG. If your problem is "the model knows the right answer but won't format it correctly or tone it the way we need" — that's a behaviour problem, and RAG won't help. You need fine-tuning.

Fine-Tuning (Level 5) — Changing How It Behaves

Here's a situation where prompting and RAG both fail: you're building a medical chatbot that needs to respond in a specific clinical format — SOAP notes (Subjective, Objective, Assessment, Plan). You write it into the system prompt: "Always respond in SOAP format." It works... 80% of the time. The other 20%, the model slips back into conversational style. You add more emphatic instructions. It helps a little. You add examples. Better, but still not 100%.

The problem is that you're fighting against the model's trained behaviour — its weights (Chapter 5) encode patterns from billions of examples of normal conversation, and your prompt is trying to override all of that with a few sentences of instruction. Sometimes the override holds. Sometimes the deeper pattern wins.

Fine-tuning changes the approach entirely. Instead of instructing the model to behave differently, you actually change its weights — retrain it on thousands of examples of the exact behaviour you want. After fine-tuning, the SOAP format isn't something the model is being told to do. It's something the model does naturally, because its weights have been adjusted to make that format the default. Thousands of examples later: "What, like it's hard?"

The difference: prompting is like telling someone "please speak French" every time (they can, but they have to think about it and sometimes slip). Fine-tuning is like the person living in France for a year until French becomes their default.

When you'd use it:

  • You need a consistent output format across thousands of responses (e.g. always respond in JSON, always use a specific medical terminology style) and prompting isn't reliable enough
  • You need the model to adopt a domain-specific tone or vocabulary permanently — not "when reminded" but as its natural voice
  • You want faster inference (because the behaviour is baked in, you don't need long prompts explaining what to do every time — which also saves money on input tokens)
  • You have proprietary examples of exactly how you want the model to respond — hundreds or thousands of input→output pairs that demonstrate the behaviour

What it costs (2026): Anywhere from a few hundred dollars (fine-tuning a small model on a small dataset) to $100K+ (fine-tuning a large model on a substantial dataset with many examples). The cost depends on model size × dataset size × number of training epochs. Most companies start with 500–2,000 examples and a few hundred dollars — enough to see if the approach will work before investing more.

What it doesn't do: It doesn't teach the model new facts reliably. This is counter-intuitive — you'd think "train it on our company's data" would teach it about your company. But fine-tuning is better at shaping behaviour (style, format, tone, decision patterns) than at injecting knowledge (new facts about the world). Why? Because reliable factual recall requires the information to be reinforced across many training examples in many contexts — a few hundred examples about your company isn't enough to compete with billions of tokens of general training. For knowledge, use RAG. For behaviour, use fine-tuning. For both, combine them.

💡 The Rule: Prompt for instructions, RAG for knowledge, fine-tune for behaviour

  • Need the model to follow specific instructions? → Better prompting.
  • Need it to know about your documents/data? → RAG.
  • Need it to consistently behave differently (format, style, tone, domain patterns)? → Fine-tuning. In practice, production systems often combine all three: a fine-tuned model + RAG for knowledge + good system prompts for per-request instructions.

RLHF: How Human Feedback Shapes Behaviour

Here's something Chapter 3 promised we'd return to: RLHF — Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback.

After pre-training, a model can generate text — but it doesn't know what "helpful" means. It might produce toxic content (because that was in the training data). It might ignore your instructions (because instruction-following wasn't its training task). It might be verbose, evasive, or inconsistent.

RLHF is the process that shapes the model into something actually pleasant to use. Here's how it works:

Step 1: Collect human preferences. Show human evaluators two possible responses to the same prompt. Ask: "Which one is better?" Repeat this thousands of times across many different prompts and response pairs.

Step 2: Train a reward model. Use those human preferences to train a separate model — a "reward model" — that can predict what humans would prefer. This model learns patterns like: helpful > evasive, concise > rambling, accurate > wrong, safe > harmful.

Step 3: Adjust the main model to match what humans preferred. The system adjusts the main model's weights (the recipe amounts from Chapter 5) so it produces responses that score highly according to the reward model. The model learns: "responses that look like this get rewarded; responses that look like that get penalised." It's like training a new employee by showing them examples of work that got praised vs work that got corrected — over time, they produce more of the praised kind.

The result: A model that's been shaped by human preferences to be helpful, follow instructions, refuse harmful requests, and produce quality outputs. Every major chatbot you've used (ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini) has been through this process.

What's honestly strange about this: RLHF works better than anyone fully understands. Models trained with preference data sometimes exhibit behaviours that weren't explicitly in the preference examples — they generalise from "humans preferred A over B in these 50,000 cases" to broader behavioural patterns that nobody directly taught. Why this generalisation works as well as it does, and whether it will keep working as models get more capable, is an open question. Researchers use it because it produces good results — not because they can fully explain why the results are as good as they are.

DPO and Constitutional AI: Simpler Alternatives

RLHF works, but it's complex — you need to train a separate reward model, then run reinforcement learning. Researchers have found simpler approaches:

DPO (Direct Preference Optimisation): A shortcut. Instead of building a separate reward model first, DPO skips that step and directly adjusts the main model based on the preference pairs. "Humans preferred Response A over Response B — adjust the weights to produce more A-like responses." Same outcome, fewer steps. It's like cutting out the middleman — instead of training a judge and then training the employee based on the judge's scores, you just show the employee directly which work was preferred and let them learn from that.

Constitutional AI (Anthropic's approach): Instead of using human raters for every preference judgement, you give the model a written "constitution" — a set of principles like "be helpful," "don't produce harmful content," "acknowledge uncertainty." Then you have the model judge its own outputs against those principles and learn from its self-evaluations.

The constitution draws from sources like the UN Universal Declaration of Human Rights, Apple's terms of service, AI ethics research, and Anthropic's own guidelines. The model learns to critique and revise its own responses — a form of self-alignment.

This approach is called RLAIF (Reinforcement Learning from AI Feedback) — the AI is providing its own feedback, guided by the constitution, rather than relying on human raters for every judgement.

⏸️ Wait — the AI judges itself? Isn't that circular? Partially. The model that does the judging uses the constitution as a guide — it's not just making things up. And the constitution was written by humans based on human values. So human values still enter the system — just through written principles rather than thousands of individual rating tasks. It's not purely circular, but it does require carefully written principles.

Making Models Smaller and Faster

Not every improvement is about making models bigger. Several techniques make models smaller, cheaper, and faster to run:

Distillation

What it does: Takes a large, expensive model (the "teacher") and trains a smaller model (the "student") to mimic it.

How it actually works:

  1. Run the teacher model on thousands of examples. For each one, record not just the final answer but the full probability distribution — which tokens the teacher considered likely and how likely each one was.
  2. Train the student model to match those distributions — not just "get the right answer" but "rank the options the same way the teacher did."

Why the full distribution matters: if the teacher sees "The capital of France is ___" and assigns 92% to "Paris," 3% to "the," 2% to "a" — that ranking contains more information than just "the answer is Paris." It tells the student how confident to be and what the near-misses were. Training on these "soft labels" transfers the teacher's uncertainty and nuance, not just its conclusions.

Why it matters: A 3-billion-parameter model distilled from a much larger one can often match the larger model's quality on specific tasks — while being 10× cheaper and faster to run. This is how "phone-sized" AI models get made: distilled from the big ones.

Quantisation

What it does: Reduces the precision of the numbers in a model. Instead of storing each weight as a 16-bit number (high precision, takes more memory), you store it as a 4-bit or 8-bit number (less precision, takes less memory).

What that means mechanically: A 16-bit number can represent 65,536 different values. A 4-bit number can only represent 16. Quantisation rounds every weight in the model to its nearest value in that smaller set — like rounding every price in a catalogue to the nearest dollar instead of tracking pennies. You lose some exactness, but you can fit 4× as many numbers in the same memory space.

A model with 70 billion parameters stored in 16-bit takes ~140 GB of memory. Quantised to 4-bit, the same model fits in ~35 GB — suddenly within reach of a high-end consumer GPU instead of requiring a data centre rack.

Why it matters: A quantised model uses 2–4× less memory and runs faster — often with minimal quality loss. This is what lets large models run on consumer hardware (a laptop GPU, a phone chip) rather than requiring expensive data centre equipment.

The trade-off: Slight quality degradation. For most tasks, barely noticeable. For tasks requiring high precision (complex maths, very nuanced language), the rounding errors compound across billions of calculations and the quality difference shows.

Mixture of Experts (MoE)

The concept below is what matters — the mechanical details are optional depth.

What it does: Instead of running every token through all parameters, MoE divides the model into "experts" — specialised sub-networks. For each token, a router decides which experts to activate (typically 2 out of 8, or similar). The rest stay dormant.

How it actually works — step by step:

Imagine a hospital. You walk in with a problem. You don't see every specialist in the building — you see a triage nurse (the router) who looks at your symptoms and sends you to the right 1–2 departments. The orthopaedic surgeon and the dermatologist don't waste time on your sore throat. They exist, they're available if needed, but for your visit they stay idle.

That's MoE. Here's the mechanism:

  1. A token arrives at a layer in the network. (Say the token is "photosynthesis.")
  2. The router — a tiny neural network — looks at that token and produces a score for each expert: "Expert 3 scores 0.8, Expert 7 scores 0.6, the rest score below 0.1."
  3. The system activates the top-k experts (usually top-2). Only Experts 3 and 7 process this token.
  4. Each active expert runs its own set of weights on the token and produces an output.
  5. The outputs are weighted and combined (Expert 3 contributed 57%, Expert 7 contributed 43% — proportional to their router scores).
  6. That blended result moves to the next layer. The other 6 experts never fired.

The maths that matters: If a model has 8 experts per layer and activates 2, only 25% of the parameters do work on any given token. The model can have 1.8 trillion total parameters but run at the speed and cost of a ~220-billion-parameter dense model — because that's roughly how many parameters actually fire per token.

Why it matters: You get the capacity of a huge model (trillions of total parameters) with the cost of a much smaller one (because only a fraction activates per token). GPT-4 (2023) reportedly used this architecture — according to widely-cited leaks (never officially confirmed by OpenAI), it had ~1.8 trillion total parameters across 16 experts, activating 2 per token (~220 billion active per token, or about 12.5% of total capacity). Most frontier models in 2026 use some form of MoE.

The trade-off: MoE models use more total memory (all experts must be loaded even though most are idle per token). They're also harder to train — you need "load balancing" to prevent all tokens from routing to the same favourite expert while others atrophy from disuse. It's the architectural equivalent of making sure every department in the hospital still gets patients, not just the popular one.

As of 2026: MoE is the dominant architecture for frontier language models. It's how the industry builds larger, more capable systems while keeping inference cost manageable.

🔍 Concept in Practice: Why some AI models "fit on your phone" When you see a model described as "runs locally on your device," it has typically been distilled (trained to mimic a bigger model) AND quantised (compressed to use less memory). A model that originally required hundreds of gigabytes of server memory can be compressed to 2–4 GB while retaining much of its capability for everyday tasks. That's why Apple Intelligence, Google's on-device features, and local AI apps work without an internet connection — they're running small, compressed versions of models that were originally enormous.

Decision Framework: When to Use What

Here's how to think about which approach to use, based on what you're trying to change:

What you want to changeBest approachWhy
What the model knows about (your data, recent info)RAGRetrieves knowledge into context without retraining
How it follows specific instructions for this requestBetter promptingInstructions in context, no permanent change needed
Its consistent behaviour pattern (format, style, tone)Fine-tuningBakes behaviour into the weights permanently
Its safety and helpfulness characteristicsRLHF / DPOShapes deep behavioural tendencies through preference data
Making it run on cheaper/smaller hardwareDistillation + quantisationCompress without (much) quality loss
Making it bigger without proportionally more costMixture of expertsMore total parameters, fewer active per token

The order most teams follow (2026):

  1. Try prompting first (free, instant)
  2. Add RAG if it needs knowledge (days to build, low cost)
  3. Fine-tune only if behaviour needs permanent change (weeks, $$)
  4. Everything else is for specialised teams at model companies

The System Picture So Far

[Data] → [Tokenisation] → [PRE-TRAINING] → [Base Model]
                                                  ↓
                                         [RLHF / DPO / Constitutional AI] → shapes behaviour
                                                  ↓
                                         [Fine-tuning] → domain-specific behaviour (optional)
                                                  ↓
                                         [Distillation / Quantisation] → smaller/faster versions
                                                  ↓
                                         [Deployed Model] ← now ready to use
                                                  ↓
                              ──── INFERENCE (every request) ────
                                                  ↓
                    [System Prompt] + [RAG-retrieved docs] + [User Message] + [Conversation History]
                                                  ↓
                                    [Context Window → Prefill → Decode → Response]
