13Sandboxing: when AI acts (not just talks), you need safe practice spaces. The autonomy spectrum runs from fully sandboxed to fully autonomous. Irreversibility is the key factor in deciding how much autonomy to grant.

Where you are: You now understand the full safety picture — from shaping behaviour (alignment) to filtering outputs (guardrails) to testing adversarially (red-teaming) to containing actions (sandboxing). Next: the agents themselves.

Try This

Notice the sandboxing around you. Next time you use an AI tool that can take actions (email, calendar, file management), notice where it asks for permission and where it acts autonomously. Map those to the autonomy spectrum: which actions are sandboxed? Which are human-in-the-loop? Which (if any) are fully autonomous?

Think about reversibility. For each action the system could take, ask: if this goes wrong, can it be undone? That's the framework companies use to decide where the sandbox boundaries sit.

What's Next

You now understand why agents need safety boundaries. But what exactly is an agent? How does a system go from answering questions to actually doing things? What gives it the ability to plan, use tools, and work toward goals?

Chapter 14 takes us to agents: AI that acts — the architecture, the planning loop, the tools, and what makes an agent fundamentally different from a chatbot.

📖 Key Terms — Quick Reference Sandbox — An isolated environment where actions can't escape to the real world. A safe practice space for AI systems that take actions. Human-in-the-loop — System pauses before acting and requires human approval. The default for most production agent systems (2026). Autonomy spectrum — Fully sandboxed → human-in-the-loop → supervised autonomy → fully autonomous. Most current systems sit at levels 2–3. Irreversibility — The key factor in deciding autonomy. If an action can't be undone (send, delete, purchase), it needs more oversight than one that can (draft, move, organise). Code execution sandbox — AI can write and run code in a container that can't access your real system. Used by ChatGPT's Code Interpreter, Claude, and others.

Chapter 14: Agents — AI That Acts

Part V · Agents

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

  • Explain what makes an agent different from a chatbot (tools + memory + a planning loop)
  • Describe the agent loop: perceive → reason → act → observe → repeat
  • Understand how "tool calling" works — how a model triggers real actions
  • Explain what MCP (Model Context Protocol) is and why it standardises how agents connect to tools
  • Describe the difference between single agents and multi-agent systems
  • Understand where agents are now (2026) — what they can reliably do, and where they still struggle

Key Terms Introduced in This Chapter

TermPlain English
AgentAn AI system that doesn't just generate text — it takes actions, uses tools, maintains state, and works toward goals over multiple steps
Tool callingWhen a model generates structured output that triggers a real action (search the web, send an email, run code)
Agent loopThe repeating cycle: perceive → reason → plan → act → observe → decide whether to continue or stop
PlanningBreaking a complex goal into smaller steps the agent can execute one at a time
Multi-agent systemMultiple agents working together — collaborating, specialising, or handing tasks between each other
MCP (Model Context Protocol)An open standard (Anthropic, 2024) that standardises how agents discover and use tools — the "USB-C for AI tools"
A2A (Agent-to-Agent)A protocol for agents communicating with other agents (distinct from MCP, which connects agents to tools)

What Makes an Agent Different From a Chatbot

A chatbot answers questions. You ask, it responds. One exchange. Done.

An agent does work. It takes a goal, breaks it into steps, uses tools, evaluates its own progress, and keeps going until the job is done — or until it gets stuck and asks for help.

The three things that make something an agent rather than a chatbot:

  1. Tools. The system can do things beyond generating text — search the web, read files, send messages, execute code, interact with other software.
  2. A loop. Instead of one-shot (question → answer), the system runs in a cycle: think about what to do → do it → observe the result → decide what to do next → repeat. It can take multiple actions in sequence without waiting for you to tell it each step.
  3. State and memory. The system maintains awareness of what it's already done, what it's tried, what worked, and what didn't — within a task and (sometimes) across tasks.

A chatbot generates text. An agent generates text and takes actions and evaluates its own work and iterates until a goal is reached.

The Agent Loop

Every agent, regardless of how it's built, follows the same fundamental loop.

Why a loop? Because the model can only do one thing per turn: generate text. It can't simultaneously search the web AND check a calendar AND compose an email. It does one thing, sees what came back, then decides what to do next. It's not iterating because it's uncertain (like a person) — it's iterating because that's mechanically how text generation works: one output at a time, each informed by what came before.

The loop isn't a design choice someone made — it's a structural consequence of how these systems generate. You can't produce a ten-step plan in one shot when steps 3–10 depend on what step 1 returns from the real world.

Here's the structure:

         ┌──────────────────────────────┐
         │                              │
         ↓                              │
    [PERCEIVE]                          │
    What do I know right now?           │
    What's in my context?               │
         ↓                              │
    [REASON / PLAN]                     │
    What should I do next?              │
    Do I need a tool? Which one?        │
         ↓                              │
    [ACT]                               │
    Call a tool, generate output,       │
    take an action                      │
         ↓                              │
    [OBSERVE]                           │
    What happened? What came back?      │
    Did it work?                        │
         ↓                              │
    [DECIDE]                            │
    Am I done? Or do I need             │
    another loop?                       │
         │                              │
         └── (if not done) ────────────┘

A concrete example: "Book me the cheapest flight from Vancouver to Toronto next Friday."

  1. Perceive: I have the user's request. I need dates, route, and price comparison.
  2. Reason/Plan: I should search for flights, compare options, and present the cheapest. I'll use the flight search tool.
  3. Act: Call the flight search tool with parameters: YVR → YYZ, next Friday.
  4. Observe: Results come back — 6 flights, ranging from $289 to $712.
  5. Decide: I have the results. The cheapest is $289 on WestJet at 6:15am. But should I just tell the user, or book it? I'll present options and ask.

That's one loop — but a complex task might involve 5, 10, or 50 loops before it's complete.

Tool Calling: How a Model "Does Things"

The model itself can't search the web, send emails, or book flights. It can only generate text. So how does it "use tools"?

Through tool calling — a specific format of text generation that the system interprets as an instruction to execute an action.

How it works:

  1. The system prompt tells the model: "You have access to these tools: web_search(query), send_email(to, subject, body), calendar_create(title, date, time)."
  2. When the model decides it needs to use a tool, it generates structured text: {"tool": "web_search", "query": "cheapest flight YVR to YYZ August 22"}
  3. The harness (Chapter 10) intercepts this structured output, recognises it as a tool call, and executes the actual search.
  4. The results come back and get injected into the context window.
  5. The model sees the results and generates its next response (or makes another tool call).

The key insight: The model doesn't execute anything. It generates text in a specific format. The system around the model (the harness) reads that text, executes the real action, and feeds the results back. The model's "power" to act comes entirely from the system built around it.

A real example, step by step:

You ask: "What's the weather like in Vancouver right now?"

StepWhoWhat happens
1YouType your question
2ModelReads the question + its system prompt (which says: "You have a weather_lookup tool"). Decides it needs current data it doesn't have in its weights.
3Model generatesInstead of answering directly, it outputs: {"tool": "weather_lookup", "location": "Vancouver, BC"}
4HarnessSees the structured tool-call output. Pauses the model's generation. Makes an actual API call to a weather service.
5Weather APIReturns: {"temp": "18°C", "conditions": "partly cloudy", "humidity": "72%"}
6HarnessInjects this result back into the context window: "Tool result: temperature 18°C, partly cloudy, 72% humidity"
7ModelSees the tool result in context. Resumes generating tokens. Produces: "It's 18°C (64°F) and partly cloudy in Vancouver right now, with 72% humidity."
8YouSee the final response

From your perspective, you asked a question and got an answer. Behind the scenes, the model generated text → the system intercepted it → an external action ran → results came back → the model finished its response. This all happens in 2-4 seconds.

What makes agents different from a single tool call: an agent can do this multiple times in a row without your involvement. It might:

  • Call tool A, read the results
  • Decide it needs more information, call tool B
  • Read those results, realise there's a conflict
  • Call tool C to resolve the conflict
  • Finally produce its answer to you

Each cycle is the same pattern: model generates a tool call → harness executes → results injected → model continues. The loop is what makes it an agent.

⏸️ Wait — it just writes text and the system does the real thing? Yes. The model has no direct connection to the internet, your email, or any external system. It generates structured text (essentially instructions), and the harness follows those instructions. This is important: the model doesn't "decide" to send an email in any mechanical sense different from generating any other text. It produces tokens that, in context, are interpreted by the harness as an action request. The decision to actually execute is made by the harness layer, which is where guardrails, sandboxing, and human-in-the-loop checks live.

MCP: The Standard for Connecting Tools

Before 2024, every integration between a model and a tool was custom-built. Want your agent to use Google Calendar? Write a custom integration. Want it to use Slack? Write another custom integration. Every tool, every model, every combination — bespoke code.

MCP (Model Context Protocol) changed this. Introduced by Anthropic in November 2024 and donated to an industry-wide standards body in 2025, MCP is an open standard that defines a universal way for agents to discover and use tools. It's now the dominant way AI tools connect to each other — adopted across the industry within months.

The analogy that's actually accurate: MCP is to AI tools what USB-C is to devices. Before USB-C, every device had its own proprietary charger. MCP gives every tool a standard "port" that any agent can plug into.

How it works:

  • Tools run on separate MCP servers (lightweight programs that expose capabilities)
  • The agent connects to these servers at runtime
  • The server tells the agent: "This is what I can do" (a list of available tools with descriptions)
  • The agent can then call any of those tools using the standard protocol
  • Results come back in a standard format

By mid-2026: MCP SDK downloads passed 97 million monthly. It's become the default way agents connect to external systems.

Planning and Decomposition

Simple tasks ("search for X") need one tool call. But real tasks ("plan a team offsite") require breaking the goal into sub-tasks:

  1. Check team members' availability
  2. Research venue options in the area
  3. Compare costs
  4. Draft an itinerary
  5. Send a proposal to the team

This is planning — the ability to decompose a complex goal into an ordered sequence of steps, execute them one at a time, and adjust the plan based on what each step reveals.

How agents plan (2026):

  • Explicit decomposition: The model writes out a plan (numbered steps) before executing. It can then work through the steps and revise the plan as new information arrives.
  • Implicit reasoning: For simpler tasks, the model just decides the next action at each loop without writing a full plan — reasoning step-by-step within the loop.
  • Hierarchical planning: For very complex tasks, a "planner" agent creates the high-level plan and delegates sub-tasks to specialised "worker" agents.

Where agents still struggle (2026):

  • Very long plans (20+ steps) — errors compound, plans drift
  • Tasks requiring precise sequencing where one mistake invalidates everything
  • Knowing when to give up vs when to try a different approach
  • Recovering from unexpected tool failures mid-task

Why this is hard (the honest mechanical reason):

The model doing the "reasoning" in the agent loop is a next-token predictor. It wasn't trained to plan — it was trained to produce plausible-sounding text. When it generates a plan, it's producing tokens that look like a plan (because it saw many plans in training data), not executing a planning algorithm. The difference matters: a real planner can verify that step 3 logically follows from step 2. A language model generating "step 3" is just producing the most probable continuation of the text that includes steps 1 and 2. It often works — because plans in training data were usually coherent. But it breaks on novel problems, unusual sequences, and edge cases where the "most probable next step" isn't the correct one.

This is the deepest open question in agentic AI (2026): can next-token prediction learn to plan, or does reliable planning require a fundamentally different mechanism? Nobody knows. Some researchers argue that scale will solve it (bigger models plan better). Others argue that planning is algorithmically different from text generation and needs dedicated architectural components. Both camps have evidence. The field is building agents anyway — using scaffolding (retries, verification steps, human checkpoints) to compensate for the planning limitation.

Multi-Agent Systems

Sometimes one agent isn't enough. Complex tasks benefit from multiple specialised agents working together:

  • A researcher agent gathers information
  • A writer agent produces content based on the research
  • A reviewer agent checks the writer's work for accuracy
  • A coordinator agent manages the workflow and routes tasks

This is a multi-agent system — and it's how many of the most capable AI products work internally (2026). When you ask a complex question that requires research, reasoning, and composition — the system might internally route your request through several specialised agents.

A2A (Agent-to-Agent protocol): Just as MCP standardises how agents talk to tools, A2A (announced by Google in 2025) standardises how agents talk to each other — discovery, handoffs, status updates, and shared context between agents made by different companies.

🔍 Concept in Practice: Why complex AI tasks sometimes take 30+ seconds When you ask an AI system to do something complex — research a topic, create a document, solve a multi-step problem — and it takes 30 seconds or more to respond (much longer than a simple question), that delay is the agent loop in action. The system is making multiple tool calls, reading results, reasoning, calling more tools, checking its work — looping multiple times before delivering the final response. Each loop takes a few seconds (tool call + model inference). Five loops = the delay you're experiencing. It's not "slow" — it's doing genuine multi-step work.

Real Examples of Agents (2026)

Agent typeWhat it doesHow it works
AI coding assistantReads your codebase, writes code, runs tests, fixes errorsLoop: understand task → write code → run tests → read errors → fix → rerun tests → repeat until passing
Research agentInvestigates a topic across multiple sourcesLoop: search → read → extract key points → identify gaps → search more → synthesise findings
Email/calendar agentManages your inbox, schedules meetings, drafts repliesLoop: read email → classify → draft response → wait for approval → send. Schedule: check availability → propose times → book
Customer support agentResolves customer issues using company knowledgeLoop: understand issue → search knowledge base → attempt resolution → escalate if stuck
Computer use agentControls a computer (clicking, typing, navigating) to complete tasksLoop: take screenshot → understand what's on screen → decide what to click/type → execute → take new screenshot → repeat

Each of these follows the same fundamental loop — perceive, reason, act, observe, repeat. The difference is in the tools available and the complexity of the planning required.

Try This

Identify the loop. Next time an AI assistant does something multi-step for you — research, planning, document creation — think about the agent loop. Can you identify the steps? What was the perceive moment? What tools were called? How many loops did it take?

Notice tool boundaries. Ask an AI to do something that would require a tool it doesn't have (e.g., "book me a restaurant" on a system without booking tools). Notice how it handles the limitation — it can describe what it would do, but it can't actually execute without the tool.

Think about MCP. The next time you connect an app to an AI assistant (email, calendar, files), you're essentially plugging a tool into the agent's toolkit. Before MCP, each of those connections was custom. Now they're standardised.

What's Next

You now understand what agents are, how they work, and what they can do. But the people building, managing, and improving these systems need specific skills — and those skills have evolved rapidly. In 18 months, the craft went from "prompt engineering" to "context engineering" to "loop engineering" to "graph engineering."

Chapter 15 takes you through that evolution: what each discipline means, how it builds on the last, and what these people actually do day-to-day.

📖 Key Terms — Quick Reference Agent — AI that acts, not just talks. Has tools + a loop + state. Takes goals, breaks them into steps, executes, evaluates, iterates. Agent loop — Perceive → Reason → Plan → Act → Observe → Decide → repeat until done. The fundamental architecture every agent shares. Tool calling — Model generates structured text (e.g. JSON function calls). The harness intercepts and executes the actual action. Model itself doesn't "do" anything — it writes instructions. MCP (Model Context Protocol) — Open standard for connecting agents to tools. Introduced by Anthropic in 2024, now industry-wide. The "USB-C for AI tools." A2A (Agent-to-Agent) — Protocol for agents communicating with other agents. Handles discovery, handoffs, shared context. Planning — Decomposing complex goals into steps. Can be explicit (write a plan) or implicit (decide next action each loop). Multi-agent system — Multiple specialised agents (researcher, writer, reviewer, coordinator) working together on a task.

Chapter 15: From Prompting to Graph Engineering — How the Craft Evolved

Part VI · The Engineering Disciplines

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

  • Trace the evolution of AI engineering: prompt → context → loop → graph (2023–2026)
  • Explain what each discipline actually means and what practitioners do day-to-day
  • Understand why each is a "zoom-out" from the last
  • Evaluate AI products more critically — when one works better than another, these layers are usually why
  • Read AI articles from any period (2023–2026) without getting confused by shifting terminology
  • Know where the field is today and what's likely next

Key Terms Introduced in This Chapter

TermPlain English
Prompt engineeringThe craft of phrasing instructions to get good output from a model — how you ask (2023)
Context engineeringDesigning everything the model can see when it responds — documents, memories, tools, instructions. Prompt is a subset. (mid-2025)
Loop engineeringDesigning the execution cycle an agent runs in — triggers, stopping conditions, iteration limits, feedback mechanisms (June 2026)
Graph engineeringWiring multiple agents/loops into an orchestrated system — nodes, edges, parallel branches, handoffs (July 2026)

The Same Job Got Renamed Four Times

In 2023, someone got hired as a "prompt engineer." Their job: write really good instructions for AI systems. They got good at it. They learned all the tricks — chain-of-thought, few-shot examples, structured formatting.

By 2025, their LinkedIn title changed to "context engineer" — same person, same desk, same company. By mid-2026, they were calling themselves an "AI systems engineer." Their scope had expanded three times in three years, not because they'd changed careers but because the field kept zooming out.

If you've been paying attention to AI since 2023, you've noticed this: the terminology keeps changing. It looks like hype churn. It isn't. Each term represents a genuine expansion of what the job actually involves. The craft got bigger, and each new name reflects a wider scope of responsibility.

Here's the progression:

2023:  PROMPT ENGINEERING         → How you ask
                   ↓ (zoom out)
2025:  CONTEXT ENGINEERING        → Everything it can see
                   ↓ (zoom out)
2026:  LOOP ENGINEERING           → The cycle it runs in
                   ↓ (zoom out)
2026:  GRAPH ENGINEERING          → The multi-agent system

Each level includes everything below it. A graph engineer still writes prompts. They just also design loops, context systems, and multi-agent orchestrations. Let's look at each one.

⏸️ "But why did it keep expanding? Why wasn't prompt engineering enough?"

Because each level hit a ceiling that only the next level could break through:

  1. Prompt engineering hit a wall when people realised: no matter how perfectly you phrase the question, if the model doesn't have the right information in its context window, the answer will be incomplete or hallucinated. You can't prompt your way to knowledge the model doesn't have access to. → Solution: control what the model sees, not just what you say. That's context engineering.
  1. Context engineering hit a wall when agents started doing multi-step tasks. You could assemble a perfect context window for one call — but an agent making 15 tool calls in sequence needs different information at each step. The context has to evolve as the task progresses. → Solution: design the cycle the agent runs in, including how context changes across iterations. That's loop engineering.
  1. Loop engineering hit a wall when tasks got too complex for one agent. A single agent trying to do research, check calendars, verify budgets, AND book a restaurant burns through its context window and loses coherence. → Solution: split into specialised agents that each handle one piece and hand off to each other. That's graph engineering.

Each wall was real. Each zoom-out solved a problem the previous level couldn't.

Prompt Engineering (2023): How You Ask

What it is: The craft of phrasing your instruction to the model in a way that produces good output.

What practitioners do:

  • Write clear, specific instructions
  • Structure prompts with examples (few-shot)
  • Test different phrasings to find what works
  • Add constraints ("respond in JSON," "maximum 3 sentences")
  • Use techniques like chain-of-thought ("think step by step")

The scope: One message. One call. The prompt is the input, the response is the output. The practitioner's job is making that one exchange as good as possible.

When it was the frontier: 2023 — when ChatGPT was new and the main skill was learning how to ask it for what you wanted effectively.

Where it is now: Still essential — it's the foundation of everything above. But understood as just one piece of a larger system. Like knowing how to write a good sentence is essential for being a novelist, but being a novelist involves much more.

Real example — booking a team dinner:

At the prompt engineering level, you'd carefully write:

Find me an Italian restaurant near downtown Vancouver
that's good for groups of 8, available this Friday at 7pm,
with a private dining option. Budget: $50-80 per person.

That's it. One prompt, one response, one shot. The skill is making that single instruction as clear and specific as possible so the model gives you a useful answer on the first try.

💡 As we go through each level, watch how the same task — booking a team dinner — gets handled with progressively more sophisticated engineering. The task doesn't change. The system around it does.

Context Engineering (mid-2025): Everything It Can See

What it is: Designing the complete context window — not just the prompt, but everything the model can access when it generates a response.

The shift: People realised that the quality of AI output depends less on how you phrase the question and more on what information the model has access to when answering. The prompt matters, but it's maybe 10% of what determines output quality. The other 90% is: what documents were retrieved? What system instructions are loaded? What conversation history is retained? What tools are available?

Real example — booking a team dinner (context engineering level):

Same prompt as before. But now watch what happens before the model ever sees your words:

  1. Your message arrives at the system.
  2. The retrieval layer fires: it searches your company's HR database for your team's dietary restrictions. Finds: "Priya is vegan. Marcus is gluten-free."
  3. It queries your calendar history for past team dinners. Finds: "Last 3 dinners were at Italian restaurants rated 4+ on the post-dinner Slack poll."
  4. It pulls the expense policy document. Finds: "$80/person max for team dinners, pre-approval required for groups over 6."
  5. It checks the current date, your office location (downtown Vancouver), and your team's current roster (8 people).
  6. All of this gets assembled into the context window — along with your original prompt — as a single combined input. The model sees everything at once.

The prompt didn't change. But the model's answer is dramatically better because it knows more when it answers. The skill isn't in the question — it's in what you assembled around the question before the model saw it.

⏸️ "Wait — so the context engineer's job is mostly happening BEFORE the model runs?" Yes. By the time the model generates a response, the context engineer's work is done. They designed the retrieval logic, chose which databases to query, decided what gets priority when the window is too full, and built the assembly pipeline. The model just processes whatever landed in the window. If the right information is there, the response is great. If it's missing, no amount of model intelligence helps. This is why Tobi Lütke called it "the real skill" — you're engineering the inputs, not the model itself.

What practitioners do:

  • Design RAG pipelines (what gets retrieved and how)
  • Write system prompts that shape behaviour across all interactions
  • Decide what goes into the context window and what gets left out
  • Manage context window budgets (you can't fit everything — what's most important?)
  • Design memory systems (what gets stored, what gets re-injected when)
  • Choose chunking strategies for documents
  • Optimise embedding models for retrieval quality

The scope: Not one message — the entire context window assembly for every request.

Key insight (Tobi Lütke, Shopify CEO, June 2025): "I really like the term 'context engineering' over prompt engineering… the art of providing all the context for the task to be plausibly solvable by the LLM." The real skill isn't writing a clever prompt — it's ensuring the model has the right information available when it needs to answer.

Loop Engineering (June 2026): The Cycle It Runs In

What it is: Designing the execution cycle an agent runs in — not just what it sees, but how it iterates.

The shift: Once agents started running in loops (Chapter 14) — perceive, reason, act, observe, repeat — a new set of design decisions emerged.

Real example — booking a team dinner (loop engineering level):

Now it's not one call and one answer. It's an agent running a loop:

  1. Search for Italian restaurants near downtown Vancouver → gets 12 results
  2. Filter by group size (8+) and private dining → narrows to 4
  3. Check availability for this Friday at 7pm → 2 are available
  4. Cross-check against team's dietary restrictions → 1 has vegan options
  5. Check price range against expense policy → within budget
  6. Present two final options to you → waits for your choice
  7. Books the reservation

Loop engineering is designing that cycle: How many steps is it allowed? What if step 3 returns no availability — does it try Saturday, or try non-Italian restaurants? What if the booking tool fails? When should it ask you for clarification vs making a decision itself? What's the token budget for this whole task?

⏸️ "But what is the loop engineer actually DECIDING? What does their work product look like?"

What a loop engineer specifies for the restaurant-booking agent. This is literally the configuration they write — you don't need to read every line, but notice how much of it is about handling what goes wrong:

LOOP CONFIGURATION: Team Dinner Booking
─────────────────────────────────────────
Max iterations:        12
Token budget:          50,000 (≈ $0.15)
Timeout:              90 seconds

STOPPING CONDITIONS:
  ✓ Reservation confirmed → DONE
  ✓ User says "cancel" → ABORT
  ✓ 12 iterations reached → ESCALATE to human
  ✓ All restaurants unavailable → WIDEN (try Saturday)

ERROR RECOVERY:
  Tool timeout → retry once, then skip that restaurant
  Booking API down → present options, ask user to book manually
  Calendar API down → ask user for availability verbally

ESCALATION TRIGGERS:
  Budget exceeded → ask user before proceeding
  Dietary conflict (no restaurant satisfies everyone) → present trade-offs
  Ambiguity ("Friday" = this Friday or next?) → ask user

That's the loop engineer's deliverable. Not prose — a specification for how the agent handles every branch, failure, and edge case in its execution cycle. Without this, the agent either loops forever, gives up too early, or silently makes bad decisions.

What practitioners do:

  • Design stopping conditions (when has the agent done enough?)
  • Set iteration caps (maximum 10 loops before escalating to human)
  • Build feedback mechanisms (how does the agent evaluate its own progress?)
  • Design error recovery (what happens when a tool call fails?)
  • Set budget limits (maximum $X in inference costs per task)
  • Create escalation paths (when should the agent ask for help?)
  • Tune the balance between thoroughness and speed

The scope: Not one message, not one context window — the entire iterative cycle an agent runs over a task.

Coined by: Addy Osmani (Google), June 2026, in a widely-shared post that articulated the distinction: "Context engineering is what it knows. Loop engineering is what it does with what it knows, over time."

Graph Engineering (July 2026): The Multi-Agent System

📼 Rewind it: A multi-agent graph is closer to an old-school phone tree than a crowded group chat: each handoff has a named destination and a condition for what happens next. Software still needs checks for a message that is dropped, repeated, or sent down the wrong branch.

What it is: Wiring multiple agents and loops into an orchestrated system — designing how they connect, hand off, share state, and coordinate.

The shift: Once you have multiple agents (Chapter 14), you need to design how they work together.

Real example — booking a team dinner (graph engineering level):

Now it's not one agent running one loop. It's multiple specialised agents working in parallel:

  • Scheduling Agent checks everyone's calendars and finds available times
  • Restaurant Agent searches restaurants, checks menus, verifies dietary compliance
  • Budget Agent validates against expense policy and processes pre-authorisation
  • Booking Agent makes the actual reservation once the others agree
  • Communications Agent drafts a message to the team with details

A coordinator manages the handoffs: Scheduling Agent finds Friday 7pm works → passes to Restaurant Agent → passes shortlist to Budget Agent → passes approved options to you → your choice goes to Booking Agent → confirmation goes to Communications Agent.

Graph engineering is designing that entire network: which agents exist, what each one does, how they pass work to each other, what happens when one fails, and how the whole system recovers from partial results.

What actually happens when one agent in the graph fails:

Let's say the Scheduling Agent checks calendars and finds: nobody is free Friday. What the graph does — mechanically, step by step:

  1. Scheduling Agent returns: {status: "no_availability", tried: "Friday 7pm", all_conflicts: ["Priya: kid's concert", "Marcus: flight"]}
  2. The coordinator receives this. It checks the graph's recovery rules.
  3. Recovery rule says: "If no availability on requested day → Scheduling Agent retries with ±1 day."
  4. Scheduling Agent runs again. Finds: Thursday 7pm works for 7/8 people (Marcus still flying).
  5. Coordinator checks quorum rule: "7/8 is above the 75% threshold → proceed."
  6. Coordinator passes Thursday 7pm to Restaurant Agent and Budget Agent (in parallel — they don't depend on each other).
  7. Both return results. Coordinator assembles the combined state and passes to the user for final approval.

Total elapsed time: ~8 seconds. Total inference cost: ~$0.40 across all agents. What the user saw: "Friday didn't work for everyone — Thursday 7pm works for 7 of 8 (Marcus is travelling). Here are two restaurant options within budget. Want me to book?"

The user never saw the failure, the retry, the parallel calls, or the quorum logic. They just got a smart result. That's graph engineering working.

What practitioners do:

  • Design the graph topology (which agents connect to which)
  • Define handoff protocols (how Agent A passes work to Agent B)
  • Manage shared state (information both agents need access to)
  • Design parallel execution (which agents can run simultaneously)
  • Build verification nodes (agents that check other agents' work)
  • Handle failures (what happens when one node in the graph fails?)
  • Optimise for latency and cost (the whole graph, not just individual agents)

The scope: Not one agent, not one loop — the entire multi-agent system working together across potentially hundreds of steps.

Where we are (August 2026): Graph engineering is the newest discipline. Only a handful of teams are working at this level — mostly at model labs and large technology companies. But as multi-agent systems become more common, this will become the standard way complex AI applications are built.

The Through-Line: Each Is a Zoom-Out

DisciplineWhat you're designingThe question you're answering
Prompt engineeringThe instruction"How do I ask this clearly?"
Context engineeringEverything the model sees"What does it need to know to answer well?"
Loop engineeringThe execution cycle"How should it iterate toward completing the task?"
Graph engineeringThe multi-agent system"How should multiple agents coordinate to achieve the goal?"

Each level includes all the levels below it. You can't design a good graph without understanding loops. You can't design good loops without understanding context. You can't engineer context without writing good prompts.

💡 Why this matters for you as a reader When you read an AI article from 2023 that says "prompt engineering is the key skill," that was true for its moment. When a 2025 article says "prompt engineering is dead, context engineering is everything," that's describing the zoom-out. When a 2026 article introduces "loop" or "graph" engineering — it's the next zoom-out. None of them are wrong for their time. They're describing an expanding scope. Understanding this progression means you can read material from any period without being confused by the terminology shifts.

What These People Actually Do (Day-to-Day)

Stripped of hype, what work at each level looks like:

Prompt engineering (still the most common):

  • Writing and iterating on system prompts
  • Testing prompt variations and measuring which produces better results
  • Documenting "prompt patterns" that reliably work for specific tasks
  • Teaching non-technical colleagues how to get better results from AI tools

Context engineering:

  • Building and maintaining RAG pipelines
  • Deciding how to chunk documents, which embedding model to use
  • Managing context window budget ("we only have 128K tokens — what's most important?")
  • Designing memory systems that retain useful information across sessions

Loop engineering:

  • Defining agent behaviours and constraints
  • Setting budget and timeout limits
  • Building evaluation criteria (how does the agent know it's done?)
  • Debugging agent loops that get stuck or go in circles

Graph engineering:

  • Designing multi-agent architectures
  • Managing shared state and communication between agents
  • Orchestrating parallel and sequential workflows
  • Building observability (tracing what happened across the entire graph when something goes wrong)

Try This

Place yourself on the spectrum. If you've used an AI system and improved your results by changing how you phrase things — you've done prompt engineering. If you've attached a document to give it more context — you've done context engineering. If you've set up a workflow that runs multiple steps without you intervening — that's loop thinking. If you've orchestrated multiple AI tools working together — that's graph territory.

Most people are prompt engineers without knowing it. Now you have the vocabulary for what comes next.

What's Next

You understand the software and the skills. But all of this runs on physical hardware — chips that cost tens of thousands of dollars each, in data centres that consume the electricity of small cities. Chapter 16 takes us to the physical machine: GPUs, compute, and why AI is expensive.

📖 Key Terms — Quick Reference Prompt engineering (2023) — How you phrase the instruction. The foundation. Still essential, but now understood as one piece of a larger system. Context engineering (mid-2025) — Everything the model can see: documents, memories, system instructions, tools, conversation history. Prompt engineering is a subset. The biggest determinant of output quality. Loop engineering (June 2026) — The execution cycle an agent runs in. Triggers, stopping conditions, feedback, iteration caps, budgets. Chapter 16: Chips, Compute, and Why AI Is Expensive

Part VII · The Physical Machine

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

  • Explain why AI needs specialised chips (not regular computer processors)
  • Describe the difference between CPUs, GPUs, and TPUs — and why GPUs dominate AI
  • Understand why NVIDIA controls ~80% of the AI chip market and what "CUDA lock-in" means
  • Explain what makes AI compute so expensive (and what's changing)
  • Understand the concept of "parallel processing" and why it matters for AI

Key Terms Introduced in This Chapter

TermPlain English
CPU (Central Processing Unit)The general-purpose brain of a computer — good at doing many different things, one at a time, very fast
GPU (Graphics Processing Unit)Originally designed for rendering graphics; turns out to be perfect for AI because it does thousands of simple calculations simultaneously
TPU (Tensor Processing Unit)Google's custom chip, built from scratch specifically for AI workloads — not general-purpose at all
Parallel processingDoing thousands of calculations at the same time instead of one after another
CUDANVIDIA's programming framework for GPUs — the reason developers are locked into NVIDIA's ecosystem
VRAMThe memory on a GPU chip — determines how large a model you can run
FLOPSFloating-point operations per second — a measure of raw processing power

Why Regular Computers Can't Do This

In 2012, the team that won the ImageNet competition (Chapter 2's landmark moment) trained their network on two NVIDIA GTX 580 graphics cards — gaming GPUs, the kind you'd buy to play Call of Duty. Total hardware cost: about $1,000. Training time: roughly a week.

By 2024, training a frontier model required 25,000 specialised GPUs running simultaneously for months. Total hardware cost: over $300 million. One company — NVIDIA — controlled about 80% of that market. Their stock price rose 800% in two years.

How did gaming hardware become the most strategically important technology on Earth? And why can't you just use a normal computer?

Your laptop has a CPU. It's brilliant at general tasks — running your browser, processing a spreadsheet, handling email. It works by doing one thing at a time, extremely fast, switching between tasks so quickly it appears to do everything simultaneously.

AI doesn't need a processor that's brilliant at one thing at a time. It needs a processor that can do thousands of simple things at once.

Remember training from Chapter 5: the model has billions of parameters (weights). During training, the system needs to:

  • Multiply those billions of weights by incoming data
  • Calculate how wrong the result was
  • Adjust every weight slightly
  • Do it again, millions of times

Each individual calculation is simple — basic multiplication and addition. But there are billions of them, and they can mostly be done at the same time (they don't depend on each other's results).

A CPU handles calculations like a single brilliant mathematician working one problem at a time: fast, but sequential. A GPU is like 10,000 average mathematicians working simultaneously: each one is slower, but together they finish the job far faster because the work is parallel.

⏸️ "But WHY can AI calculations be done in parallel? Why don't they depend on each other?" Think about a single layer in a neural network (Chapter 5). It has, say, 10,000 neurons. Each neuron takes the same input, multiplies it by its own weight, and produces its own output. Neuron 1 doesn't need to wait for Neuron 2's answer — they're all doing their own independent multiplication at the same time. That's one layer, one step: 10,000 independent multiplications. A real model has hundreds of layers, each with millions of neurons. During the forward pass, within each layer, all those multiplications happen simultaneously. Then the results pass to the next layer, and the next set of millions of multiplications happen simultaneously. It's like a factory assembly line where each station has 10,000 workers all doing the same task at once, then passing to the next station. A CPU would be one worker running between all 10,000 positions. A GPU gives you all 10,000 workers at once. Real numbers: A modern GPU like NVIDIA's H100 has 16,896 "cores" — 16,896 tiny processors each doing a multiplication simultaneously. That's why a single GPU can handle trillions of floating-point operations per second while a CPU with 8-16 cores cannot come close.

🔍 Concept in Practice: What ONE training step looks like across a GPU cluster

You're training a frontier model. You have 4,000 H100 GPUs. What happens in a single training step — one pass through one batch of data:

  1. Data distributed. A batch of training examples (say, 4,096 text sequences) is split across the 4,000 GPUs. Each GPU gets a portion.
  1. Forward pass. Each GPU runs its portion of the data through its portion of the model (the model is too large for one GPU — it's split across groups of 8). Within each GPU, 16,896 cores fire simultaneously, doing matrix multiplications. Time: ~200ms. Power: 4,000 × 700W = 2.8 megawatts for this step.
  1. Loss calculated. Each GPU computes how wrong its predictions were (the loss function from Chapter 5). Results are numbers — tiny error signals for each parameter.
  1. Gradients communicated. Here's the expensive part: every GPU needs to share its error signals with every other GPU so they can all update in the same direction. 4,000 GPUs exchange data over NVLink and InfiniBand networking. Terabytes move between chips. This networking step is often the bottleneck — not the computation itself.
  1. Weights updated. All GPUs apply the averaged gradient to their portion of the model's weights. Every parameter shifts slightly. One step done.
  1. Repeat. Next batch. Same process. Millions of times over weeks to months.

One step consumed: ~200ms of time, ~0.15 kWh of electricity (enough to run a microwave for 9 minutes), and generated enough heat to warm a small room. Multiply by millions of steps and you understand why training costs $100M+.

GPUs: The Accidental AI Chip

GPUs were designed for video games. Rendering a 3D scene means calculating the colour and brightness of millions of pixels simultaneously — the same "thousands of simple parallel calculations" pattern that AI training requires. When researchers in the early 2010s realised that the maths of training neural networks looked almost identical to the maths of rendering graphics, GPUs became the default hardware for AI.

The key insight: Nobody designed GPUs for AI. They just happened to be perfect for it. The architecture that makes 3D gaming smooth (massive parallelism, high memory bandwidth) is the same architecture that makes training a neural network fast.

NVIDIA's Dominance (and Why It Persists)

In January 2023, NVIDIA was a well-known but not spectacular company — market cap around $360 billion, primarily thought of as a gaming chip maker. By June 2025, it briefly became the most valuable company on Earth, passing Apple and Microsoft. In two and a half years, its value grew more than 8×.

What happened wasn't that NVIDIA suddenly got better. What happened was that everyone else suddenly needed what NVIDIA had been building for 17 years.

NVIDIA controls approximately 80% of the AI chip market (2026). Their data centre GPU revenue hit $62.3 billion in a single quarter. One company, making one category of product, accounting for most of the physical infrastructure that modern AI runs on. How?

1. They started 17 years early. In 2006, NVIDIA released CUDA — a programming language that lets developers talk directly to NVIDIA's chips. At the time, this seemed niche. But over the next 17 years, every major AI research lab built their tools, tutorials, code examples, and workflows on CUDA. By the time AI exploded in 2022, switching away from NVIDIA meant rewriting a decade of code. The moat isn't the chip — it's the software ecosystem built on top of it. This is called "CUDA lock-in," and it's one of the strongest competitive advantages in technology.

2. Each generation leapfrogs the last. The B200 (Blackwell architecture, 2024) delivers approximately 2.2× the training speed of the previous H100. The upcoming Vera Rubin architecture (2027) promises another leap. Every time a competitor starts to close the gap, NVIDIA releases a new generation and re-opens it.

3. They sell the whole stack, not just chips. NVIDIA doesn't just make GPUs — they sell the networking that connects thousands of GPUs together (NVLink), the software libraries that make them fast (cuDNN, TensorRT), and complete data centre solutions. Competing with NVIDIA means competing on chips AND networking AND software AND developer tools AND customer relationships, simultaneously. Nobody has managed all four.

The numbers (2026):

GPUReleasedTraining throughputPrice (approx.)
A1002020Baseline~$10,000
H1002022~3× A100~$25,000–40,000
H2002024~1.9× H100 (inference)~$25,000–35,000
B200 (Blackwell)2024~2.2× H100 (training)~$30,000–40,000

The Competitors: TPUs, Trainium, and Custom Silicon

NVIDIA isn't the only option — and the alternatives are growing:

Google TPUs (Tensor Processing Units):

  • Custom-designed chips built specifically for AI (not repurposed from graphics)
  • Optimised for "tensor operations" (the core maths of neural networks)
  • Used internally by Google for training Gemini and other models
  • Available to external customers via Google Cloud
  • Latest generation: Ironwood (2026)
  • Advantage: lower training cost-per-FLOP than GPUs for Google's specific workloads

Amazon Trainium:

  • Amazon's custom AI chip, designed specifically for training
  • Used to train Amazon's own models
  • Available via AWS (Amazon Web Services) at lower cost than equivalent GPU instances
  • Designed to break NVIDIA dependency for Amazon's infrastructure
  • Latest generation: Trainium2 (2025)

AMD:

  • Makes GPUs that compete with NVIDIA (MI300 series)
  • Gaining market share but still far behind (~10-15%)
  • Advantage: often cheaper than equivalent NVIDIA hardware

The pattern: Every major cloud company (Google, Amazon, Microsoft) is designing custom AI chips to reduce dependence on NVIDIA. But NVIDIA's ecosystem advantage (CUDA, 17 years of software) keeps them dominant for now.

What's honestly uncertain: Will NVIDIA's dominance last? The arguments cut both ways. The CUDA ecosystem is deep — millions of lines of research code, every ML framework optimised for it, 17 years of accumulated tooling. Switching costs are enormous. But custom chips are getting better fast, and the companies designing them (Google, Amazon) are also the companies buying the most GPUs — meaning they're both NVIDIA's biggest customers and its most motivated competitors. If TPUs or Trainium reach "good enough" performance at significantly lower cost, the lock-in could break faster than historical analogies (like Intel's x86 dominance) would suggest. Or it might hold for another decade. Nobody knows — and billions of dollars of infrastructure investment are riding on the answer.

💡 Why custom chips matter for cost A general-purpose GPU has to be good at lots of things (graphics, scientific computing, AI, crypto mining). A custom chip like a TPU can be designed for ONLY AI workloads — which means it can be more efficient (faster, less power, lower cost) for that specific task. The trade-off: it can't do anything else. This is why Google trains Gemini on TPUs rather than buying NVIDIA GPUs — for their specific workload, purpose-built is more cost-effective.

What Makes AI Compute Expensive

Training a frontier model costs hundreds of millions of dollars in compute alone. Why?

1. Scale of computation. Training a frontier model requires approximately 10²⁵ floating-point operations (or more). That's a number with 25 zeros. No single chip can do this — you need thousands of GPUs running for months.

2. The chips themselves. A single H100 costs ~$30,000. Training a frontier model requires thousands of them. A 10,000-GPU cluster costs $300 million in hardware alone.

3. Memory (VRAM). Larger models need more memory to hold all their parameters during training. The H100 has 80GB of VRAM; the H200 has 141GB. If a model doesn't fit in one GPU's memory, you split it across multiple GPUs — adding complexity and cost.

4. Electricity. A single H100 consumes about 700 watts under load. A 10,000-GPU cluster consumes 7 megawatts — enough to power approximately 5,000 homes. At commercial electricity rates, that's millions per month.

5. Cooling. Those chips generate enormous heat. Data centres need sophisticated cooling systems (increasingly liquid cooling for AI clusters).

6. Networking. Thousands of GPUs need to communicate constantly during training. The networking hardware (NVLink, InfiniBand) to connect them at sufficient speed is itself extremely expensive.

Estimated training costs (2026):

Model classApproximate training cost
GPT-3 (2020)$4.6–12 million
GPT-4 (2023)$78–100+ million
Frontier models (2025–2026)$200 million – $1 billion+

Inference Is Now the Bigger Expense

The part that's easy to miss: in 2026, the industry spends more on inference than training.

Training happens once. Inference happens every time anyone uses the model — billions of

This is why model efficiency matters so much: techniques like quantisation (Chapter 9 — reducing precision from 16-bit to 4-bit), smaller specialised models, and better hardware for inference all reduce the cost of serving every request.

🔍 Concept in Practice: Why AI subscriptions cost $20/month and still lose money Running inference on a frontier model costs real money per request. When you send a long conversation to a model, you're consuming GPU time, electricity, and memory on expensive hardware. The $20/month subscription price for consumer AI products is often below cost — companies subsidise it to grow the user base, betting they'll find ways to reduce inference costs over time. The hardware described in this chapter is what that $20 is actually paying for: a fraction of a fraction of a second on a $30,000 GPU in a data centre.

The System Picture So Far

│  [TPU/Custom] → purpose-built AI chips (Google, Amazon)   │
│                                                           │
│  Connected by: NVLink, InfiniBand (high-speed networking) │

                    ↓

Where you are: You can now explain not just how AI works in software, but what physical machines it runs on and why it's expensive.

Try This

Check GPU prices. Look up the current price of an NVIDIA H100 or B200 GPU. Then look up how many GPUs Meta or Google used to train their latest model. Multiply. That's just the hardware cost — before electricity, cooling, networking, or the engineers to run it all.

Notice "GPU" in the news. Now that you understand what GPUs are and why they matter, you'll notice them everywhere in AI news: chip export restrictions, supply shortages, new architectures, stock prices. The physical scarcity of these chips is one of the real constraints on AI progress.

What's Next

These chips don't run on your desk. They live in data centres — enormous facilities consuming the electricity of small cities, filled with tens of thousands of GPUs networked together, cooled by sophisticated systems. Chapter 17 takes you inside those buildings.

📖 Key Terms — Quick Reference CPU — General-purpose processor. Good at many things sequentially. Not efficient for AI's parallel workload. GPU — Massively parallel processor (thousands of simple cores). Originally for graphics, now the default AI chip. NVIDIA dominates (~80% market share). TPU — Google's purpose-built AI chip. Optimised specifically for tensor (matrix) operations. More efficient than GPUs for Google's specific workloads. CUDA — NVIDIA's software ecosystem (released 2006). 17 years of developer tools, libraries, and frameworks built on it. The real source of NVIDIA's lock-in — switching means rewriting code, not just swapping chips. VRAM — Memory on the GPU chip. Determines how large a model you can load. H100: 80GB, H200: 141GB. When a model doesn't fit, you split across multiple GPUs. Parallel processing — Doing thousands of calculations simultaneously. Why GPUs beat CPUs for AI: neural network maths is mostly independent operations that don't need to wait for each other. Inference > Training (2026) — The industry now spends more on serving models ($23.3B) than training them ($19B). Training happens once; inference happens billions of times per day.

Chapter 17: Data Centres and Energy

Part VII · The Physical Machine

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

  • Describe what an AI data centre actually looks like and how it's structured
  • Explain the scale of energy consumption (and why it's growing so fast)
  • Understand the relationship between AI growth and electricity demand
  • Explain cooling, water usage, and the physical challenges of running AI at scale
  • Understand the current energy mix powering AI and what's changing (nuclear, renewables, gas)
  • Place the environmental cost of AI in perspective without editorialising in either direction

Key Terms Introduced in This Chapter

TermPlain English
Data centreA building (or campus of buildings) filled with servers — the physical home of cloud computing and AI
HyperscalerA company that operates data centres at massive scale: Amazon (AWS), Microsoft (Azure), Google (GCP), Meta
PUE (Power Usage Effectiveness)How efficiently a data centre uses electricity. 1.0 = perfect (all power goes to computing). The industry average is ~1.55 (meaning 55% of electricity goes to cooling and overhead). Best-in-class hyperscalers achieve 1.1–1.2.
Liquid coolingUsing liquid (not air) to cool chips — increasingly necessary as AI chips generate more heat than air cooling can handle
TWh (Terawatt-hour)A unit of energy. Global data centres used ~485 TWh in 2025 — about 1.5% of all electricity generated worldwide
ColocationRenting space in someone else's data centre rather than building your own

What's Actually Inside

Every time you ask an AI a question, somewhere in the world a building full of machines uses enough electricity to power a small neighbourhood — for a fraction of a second — to answer you. That building exists. It has an address. It uses real water for cooling, draws real power from a real grid, and generates real heat that has to go somewhere.

"The cloud" is not a metaphor you're supposed to take literally. It's a marketing term for "someone else's very large, very expensive building." Let's go inside one.

A data centre is a large building — or a campus of buildings — filled with rows of metal racks. Each rack holds servers. In an AI-focused data centre, those servers contain the GPUs from Chapter 16 — thousands of them, networked together.

What you'd see if you walked through one:

  • Rows of 2-metre (6.5-foot) tall metal racks, spaced evenly, stretching the length of a warehouse
  • Thick bundles of fibre optic cables running overhead and under raised floors
  • The constant hum of fans (or, increasingly, the quiet of liquid-cooled systems)
  • Blinking status lights — thousands of them
  • Security: biometric access, cameras, mantrap entries
  • Outside: massive electrical transformers, backup diesel generators, cooling towers

Scale: A single hyperscale AI data centre campus might contain 50,000–100,000 GPUs. Microsoft's planned clusters for training frontier models involve over 100,000 GPUs working together as a single system. Meta's AI infrastructure spans multiple campuses across the world.

⏸️ "So when I ask ChatGPT a question... it goes to one of these?"

Yes. Here's the physical journey — every step your request takes through the real world:

  1. Your fingertip hits the key. Your device packetises the message and sends it over your Wi-Fi to your router.
  2. Your ISP routes it. The packets travel through fibre optic cables — possibly across an ocean — to the nearest point of presence for the AI provider's network. Elapsed: ~20ms.
  3. Load balancer receives it. A routing system at the data centre decides which GPU cluster has capacity. Your request is assigned to a specific server in a specific rack in a specific row.
  4. Tokenisation runs on a CPU. Your text is split into tokens (Chapter 4). This is lightweight — a standard processor handles it. Elapsed: ~25ms.
  5. Tokens hit the GPU cluster. Your tokens — plus the system prompt, conversation history, and any retrieved documents — flow into GPU memory. The prefill phase (Chapter 7) runs: all input tokens processed in parallel across thousands of GPU cores. The chip consumes 700 watts during this burst. Heat dissipates into the liquid cooling system. Elapsed: ~200ms.
  6. Decode begins. The GPU generates output tokens one at a time. Each token requires a forward pass through the network's billions of parameters. Each pass consumes electricity. Each pass generates heat. The first word appears on your screen.
  7. Tokens stream back. Each generated token is immediately packetised and sent back through the same route — data centre → fibre → ISP → your router → your screen. You see words appearing one by one. Elapsed: 1–3 seconds for the full response.
  8. Cleanup. The KV cache (Chapter 7) is held briefly in case you respond immediately, then released. The GPU's memory is freed for the next user's request.

Total energy consumed by your one question: approximately 0.001–0.01 kWh — enough to power a LED lightbulb for 3–30 minutes. Multiply by billions of queries per day, and you understand Chapter 17's energy numbers.

Every AI response you've ever received was generated by actual hardware, in an actual building, consuming actual energy, generating actual heat that had to be physically removed.

The Energy Numbers

Here's a way to feel the scale: data centres used approximately 485 TWh of electricity globally in 2025. That's enough to power roughly 45 million average US homes for a year — more than the entire country of France uses. It's about 1.5% of all electricity generated on Earth — from a category of building that most people have never seen or thought about.

And that's the number before AI agents became mainstream.

Where it's going (IEA, UN, multiple sources):

YearGlobal data centre electricityFor perspective
2025~485 TWhMore than France
2030 (low estimate)~600 TWhMore than Germany
2030 (IEA central projection)~945 TWhApproaching Japan
US alone, 2030400–600 TWhWould require dozens of new power plants

Why it's growing this fast: A single AI inference query uses approximately 10× more electricity than a standard web search. Ask Google a question — a few servers look up an index and return results. Ask ChatGPT a question — thousands of GPU cores run billions of calculations to generate each word. And usage is skyrocketing: more people using AI, more complex tasks (agents that make 15 tool calls use 15× more compute than a simple Q&A), and larger models.

The paradox (IEA, April 2026): "Power consumption per AI task is declining rapidly, with efficiency improving at a rate unprecedented in energy history. However, more people are using AI, and energy-intensive uses — such as AI agents — are on the rise." In other words: each query is getting cheaper, but the total number of queries is growing far faster than the efficiency gains. It's like cars getting better fuel economy while total miles driven triples — you still burn more fuel.

Cooling: The Hidden Challenge

Chips generate heat. The more powerful the chip, the more heat. An H100 GPU consumes 700 watts and converts almost all of it to heat. Multiply by 10,000 GPUs in a cluster and you have 7 megawatts of heat that needs to go somewhere.

Air cooling (traditional): Cold air is pushed through the server racks, absorbing heat, then exhausted and cooled again. Simple but increasingly insufficient for dense AI hardware. For efficient hyperscale centres, cooling accounts for about 7% of total electricity use. For less-efficient facilities, it can be over 30%.

Liquid cooling (the trend): Liquid circulates through or directly contacts the hot chips, absorbing heat far more efficiently than air. Some systems use cold plates (liquid flows through a metal plate touching the chip); others use immersion (chips submerged directly in non-conductive liquid). Most new AI-focused data centres (2025+) are designed for liquid cooling from the start.

Water usage: Cooling towers evaporate water to reject heat. A large data centre can use millions of litres of water per day. This is increasingly a constraint in drought-prone regions and a point of community concern when new centres are proposed.

Where the Electricity Comes From

Current energy mix powering data centres globally (IEA):

  • Renewables (wind, solar, hydro): ~27%
  • Natural gas: ~26%
  • Nuclear: ~15%
  • Coal and other: remaining

What's changing (2025–2026):

Every major hyperscaler has announced plans to power AI data centres with cleaner energy:

  • Nuclear: Multiple companies have signed deals to restart or build nuclear plants specifically for data centres. Microsoft signed a deal to restart Three Mile Island's Unit 1 reactor. Amazon purchased a nuclear-powered data centre campus. Small Modular Reactors (SMRs) are being developed specifically for data centre power.
  • Renewables: Massive solar and wind farm contracts. Google claims to run on 100% renewable energy (via matching — they buy enough renewables to offset usage, though not all at the same hour).
  • Natural gas: Still heavily used, especially for new capacity that needs to come online quickly (gas plants are faster to build than nuclear or large-scale renewables).

The tension: AI companies have pledged net-zero or carbon-free targets. But AI electricity demand is growing so fast that their overall carbon footprints are increasing even as the percentage from renewables grows. Google's emissions rose 13% in 2023 and continued rising in 2024-2025, despite more renewable purchases — because total energy use grew faster.

The Geography of AI

Data centres cluster in specific locations for specific reasons:

FactorWhy it mattersExample locations
Cheap electricityPower is the biggest operating costOregon, Iowa, Quebec, Nordic countries
Cool climateLess cooling needed = less energy wastedIreland, Sweden, Finland, Canada
Fibre connectivityNear internet backbone connectionsNorthern Virginia (US), Amsterdam, Singapore
Water availabilityFor cooling tower evaporationNear rivers or coast
Permitting/policyFavourable regulations, fast approvalsVaries by jurisdiction
Grid capacityEnough electrical supply to connectIncreasingly a constraint — some regions have waitlists

Northern Virginia is the world's largest data centre hub — over 300 data centres in a single corridor. But grid capacity there is increasingly strained. New AI centres are being built in places with available power: the Midwest US, Nordic countries, parts of Canada.

🔍 Concept in Practice: Why "the cloud" is actually very physical "The cloud" sounds ethereal — as if your data floats somewhere abstract. In reality, every AI query you make travels via fibre optic cables to a specific building in a specific location, is processed on a specific GPU that's consuming electricity and generating heat, and the result travels back. When cloud services go down (as they occasionally do), it's often because something physical happened: a power failure, a cooling system breakdown, a fibre cable cut. The "cloud" is a marketing term for "someone else's very large, very expensive computer building."

Try This

Look up a data centre near you. Search for "data centres in [your city/region]." You may be surprised how many there are — they're often in industrial parks, nondescript buildings you've driven past without noticing.

Notice "data centre" in the news. Planning disputes, electricity grid concerns, water usage debates, nuclear power deals — these stories are everywhere once you know to look for them. The physical infrastructure of AI is one of the biggest construction and energy stories of the decade.

What's Next

Chips don't appear from nowhere. GPUs are among the most complex objects humans manufacture — requiring rare materials, specialised factories, and a global network of suppliers. Chapter 18 covers the AI supply chain: who makes what, where it comes from, and what happens when it breaks.

📖 Key Terms — Quick Reference Data centre — A building filled with servers. The physical home of AI. Hyperscale centres contain 50,000–100,000+ GPUs.
